For a dipole, the voltage falls off with the square of distance, much faster than the simple inverse law for a lone charge, because the plus and minus ends increasingly cancel as you back away - the quantitative echo of the open-field and closed-field story from Chapter 5.

6.2.2 What a scalp wave is and is not

It is worth pausing to assemble everything so far into a plain statement of what a scalp wave actually is.

A scalp wave is the instantaneous, skull-blurred, distance-weighted sum of the dipole moments of many patches of cortex, each patch contributing only insofar as its pyramidal cells receive synchronized slow synaptic currents and stand aligned with one another.

A scalp wave is not a picture of neural firing, since spikes barely contribute.

It is not local, since every electrode blends sources from across much of the head.

It is not deep, since dipole fields weaken with the square of distance, leaving structures like the hippocampus, thalamus, and brainstem faint or invisible at the scalp unless their activity entrains cortex.

It is not a direct measure of excitation, since inhibitory synaptic currents onto pyramidal cells produce dipoles just as well - indeed rhythmic inhibition is often the main sculptor of the field.

And a flat trace is not an idle brain: desynchronized, high-frequency, spatially fragmented activity - the signature of an engaged cortex - largely cancels itself out, which is why the alert brain's EEG looks smaller than the drowsy brain's.

Reading EEG honestly means holding all five of these negations in mind at once, and much of the apparatus in Part Three of this book exists precisely to keep them in view.

6.2.3 The forward problem, the ill-posed inverse problem, and current source density

The forward problem asks: given known sources inside a head of known shape and conductivity, what voltages appear at the sensors?

This problem is solvable and well behaved: with a realistic head model built from a magnetic resonance imaging scan, assigning conductivities to brain, cerebrospinal fluid, skull, and scalp, the answer is unique and computable.

The inverse problem asks the reverse: given the sensor readings, what were the sources?

This problem is fundamentally ill-posed, a fact known since Hermann von Helmholtz's analysis of volume conductors in 1853: infinitely many different source configurations inside a conductor produce identical fields on its surface.

The ambiguity is not a matter of noisy data or too few electrodes; it is a mathematical property of the physics, and no amount of engineering removes it.

Practical source localization therefore always adds assumptions - that the sources are few, or lie in the cortical sheet, or are as smooth or as sparse as possible - and the popular algorithms, from equivalent dipole fitting to distributed methods such as low resolution electromagnetic tomography, differ mainly in which assumptions they impose.

The sober rule for reading any brain-wave study is this: a source map is a hypothesis conditioned on its assumptions, not an observation.

Intracranial researchers have a partial escape called current source density (CSD) analysis: by recording the field at many closely spaced depths and taking the second spatial derivative - roughly, measuring how sharply the voltage profile bends across space - one can estimate where current actually enters and leaves the tissue, unmasking the local sinks and sources and stripping away volume-conducted contamination from afar.

CSD analysis, developed for laminar recordings and refined through the work of researchers including Charles Nicholson and John Freeman in 1975 and by Gaute Einevoll's modeling groups more recently, is how we know, layer by layer, where the currents behind hippocampal theta and cortical evoked responses actually flow.

It is the closest thing electrophysiology has to turning the frosted glass transparent, and it works only where electrodes can go - inside.



================================================================================

PART FOUR. HOW OSCILLATIONS ARISE

================================================================================

CHAPTER 7. INTRINSIC AND PACEMAKER MECHANISMS (CORE)

We now cross the book's great divide, from how rhythms are measured to how they are made.

There are two broad ways to build an oscillator out of neurons: endow single cells with an intrinsic beat, the way a metronome ticks on its own, or wire cells together so the circuit oscillates even though no single cell would - a rhythm of the ensemble.

This chapter takes the first path, from resonant membranes to the thalamus, the brain's central relay station, whose pacemaker cells and loops with the cortex generate some of the most dramatic rhythms in all of neuroscience - the sleep spindle and the slow oscillation of deep sleep.

7.1 Single-cell rhythm

Before any circuit tricks, some neurons simply prefer certain frequencies, and some oscillate spontaneously.

This section builds the single-cell toolkit: resonance, bursting, and subthreshold oscillation.

7.1.1 Membrane resonance and preferred frequencies

Push a child on a swing at just the right tempo and small pushes build a large motion; push at the wrong tempo and your effort fights itself.

That property - responding maximally to input at one preferred frequency - is resonance, and many neurons have it.

In a definitive 2000 review, Bruce Hutcheon and Yosef Yarom explained how neuronal resonance arises from the combination of two filters.

The neuron's membrane, being a leaky capacitor - a charge-storing device that also lets charge dribble away - smooths out fast inputs, acting as a low-pass filter that attenuates high frequencies.

Certain slow voltage-dependent channels act as the opposite, a high-pass filter: currents such as the hyperpolarization-activated cation current, written Ih, and the muscarine-sensitive potassium current, written IM, activate slowly and push back against slow voltage changes, attenuating low frequencies.

Sandwich a high-pass filter and a low-pass filter together and only a middle band gets through: the neuron responds best to inputs near one resonant frequency, often in the 1 to 10 hertz range depending on cell type and temperature.

Measured directly, resonance shows up when experimenters inject a sinusoidal current whose frequency sweeps slowly upward - a so-called ZAP stimulus - and the voltage response forms a hump at the preferred frequency.

Add amplifying currents, such as the persistent sodium current, and the hump can sharpen or even tip over into spontaneous oscillation, making resonance and rhythm two ends of one continuum.

The functional idea is important for everything that follows: a network of resonant neurons is biased soil - rhythms near the resonant frequency take root easily, and inputs arriving at that tempo are heard preferentially.

7.1.2 Thalamic pacemaking and the burst-and-tonic switch

The thalamus is a paired egg-shaped structure at the brain's center through which nearly all sensory information passes on its way to the cortex.

Its relay neurons possess a remarkable dual personality, characterized in detail by work from Rodolfo Llinas and Hernan Jahnsen in 1984 and synthesized in a landmark 1993 review by Mircea Steriade, David McCormick, and Terrence Sejnowski in Science.

When a relay neuron's membrane sits relatively depolarized - less negative, near minus 60 millivolts - it operates in tonic mode, firing single spikes whose rate faithfully tracks its input, like a telegraph operator relaying a message word for word.

When the membrane is hyperpolarized - more negative, below roughly minus 65 millivolts - a special class of channel changes everything: the low-threshold T-type calcium channel.

T-type channels are inactivated, effectively locked, at depolarized voltages, and hyperpolarization unlocks them; a subsequent nudge upward then opens them, and the resulting calcium influx produces a slow hump of depolarization called a low-threshold calcium spike, crowned by a rapid burst of two to seven conventional sodium spikes.

Pair this with Ih, the hyperpolarization-activated current from the previous section, and the cell becomes a self-winding clock: hyperpolarization turns Ih on, Ih slowly depolarizes the cell, the depolarization triggers a T-type burst, the burst and its aftermath re-hyperpolarize the cell, and the cycle begins again.

A thalamic relay cell so configured oscillates intrinsically at roughly 0.5 to 4 hertz - the delta rhythm frequency of deep sleep - with no external pacing whatsoever.

The switch between modes is thrown by neuromodulators - broadcast chemicals such as acetylcholine, norepinephrine, and histamine, released by brainstem and forebrain arousal systems - which depolarize relay cells during waking, disabling the T-type mechanism and enforcing faithful tonic relay.

When arousal chemicals withdraw at sleep onset, the thalamus hyperpolarizes, the bursting machinery unlocks, and the whole thalamocortical system slides into rhythmic modes - one of the cleanest examples in neuroscience of a state switch implemented in channel biophysics.

7.1.3 Subthreshold oscillations in entorhinal and olivary cells

Some neurons hum quietly to themselves below the firing threshold, and two famous examples anchor this idea.

The first lives in layer II of the entorhinal cortex, the gateway region that funnels information into the hippocampus.

In 1989, Angel Alonso and Rodolfo Llinas reported in Nature that the stellate cells of this layer produce continuous subthreshold membrane potential oscillations - voltage ripples of roughly 1 to 5 millivolts - at theta frequencies, in the vicinity of 4 to 10 hertz, arising from an interplay between the persistent sodium current, which pushes the voltage up, and Ih together with membrane repolarization, which pulls it back down.

These cells do not fire on every cycle; instead, when input arrives, spikes tend to leave on the crests of the internal wave, so the cell's private rhythm biases the timing of its public output.

Because the entorhinal cortex is a principal theta structure and later became famous as the home of grid cells - neurons that fire on a hexagonal spatial lattice - these subthreshold oscillations fueled years of theorizing about single-cell contributions to theta and spatial coding, a debate Chapter 21 will revisit.

The second example lives in the inferior olive, a nucleus in the brainstem that teaches the cerebellum, the coordination structure at the back of the brain.

Llinas and Yosef Yarom showed in the 1980s that olivary neurons generate continuous subthreshold oscillations at roughly 4 to 10 hertz, driven by calcium conductances, and - crucially - that olivary neurons are coupled to one another by gap junctions, direct electrical connections that let current flow between cells.

Coupling synchronizes the humming across the population, so the olive behaves like a bank of metronomes bolted to the same shelf, drifting into step and providing a shared timing signal implicated in the fine temporal patterning of movement.

Together the two examples establish the principle of this section: rhythmicity can be a property of the cell itself, written in its channel proteins, before a single synapse is engaged.

7.2 The thalamocortical loops

Single-cell pacemakers rarely act alone; the sleeping brain's grand rhythms emerge when intrinsically rhythmic thalamic cells are embedded in loops with the reticular nucleus and the cortex.

This section builds the two signature rhythms of non-rapid-eye-movement sleep from their circuit diagrams: the spindle and the slow oscillation.

7.2.1 Spindle generation in the reticular nucleus

A sleep spindle is a waxing-and-waning burst of roughly 10 to 16 hertz oscillation lasting about half a second to three seconds, one of the defining features of stage 2 sleep, visible on any overnight EEG as a brief flurry that swells and fades like a spun thread - hence the name.

Its generator centers on the thalamic reticular nucleus, a thin shell of inhibitory neurons wrapped around the thalamus like a shield, through which thalamocortical and corticothalamic fibers must pass, giving off connections as they go.

Steriade and colleagues showed in 1987 that the reticular nucleus, surgically isolated from cortex and the rest of the thalamus, could still generate spindle rhythmicity, establishing it as the spindle pacemaker.

The loop works like a game of rhythmic tag between the reticular shield and the relay cells it covers.

Reticular neurons, which are inhibitory and richly endowed with their own T-type calcium channels - the Cav3.3 subtype is characteristic - fire bursts that release GABA onto relay neurons, hyperpolarizing them.

That hyperpolarization is exactly what unlocks the relay cells' T-type channels, so as the inhibition decays the relay cells rebound with bursts of their own.

The rebound bursts excite reticular neurons anew, which burst and re-inhibit the relay cells, and the loop cycles at spindle frequency, each full lap taking roughly 70 to 100 milliseconds.

Relay-cell bursts also travel up to the cortex, rhythmically exciting pyramidal cells and thereby writing the spindle into the EEG through exactly the open-field dipole mechanics of Chapter 5.

The cortex is no passive audience: corticothalamic feedback helps initiate spindles and synchronize them across the thalamus, and the gradual buildup of calcium inside relay cells during a spindle activates processes that dampen the loop, contributing to the waning of each spindle and the pause before the next.

A pharmacological footnote connects this circuit to everyday life: benzodiazepine and related sleeping medications, which enhance GABA receptor function, boost spindle-band activity, and the spindle's role in sleep-dependent memory consolidation is taken up in Chapter 14.

7.2.2 The cortical slow oscillation and its up and down states

The deepest stages of sleep are ruled by the slow oscillation, a stately rhythm slower than 1 hertz - typically 0.3 to 1 cycle per second - discovered and dissected in a celebrated 1993 series of papers by Mircea Steriade, Angel Nunez, and Florin Amzica.

Recorded inside a cortical neuron, the slow oscillation is astonishing to see: the entire membrane potential of the cell swings between two distinct levels, as if the neuron had two resting states.

In the up state, the neuron is depolarized by roughly 10 to 20 millivolts, bombarded by synaptic activity, and fires much as it would in waking; in the down state, synaptic input collapses almost completely, the membrane hyperpolarizes, and the cell falls silent, sometimes for half a second or more.

Virtually every neuron in a cortical region makes these transitions near-simultaneously, which is why the scalp EEG of deep sleep shows huge slow waves: the synchrony and the slowness maximize exactly the summation that Chapter 5 taught.

Unlike the spindle, the slow oscillation is cortically generated: Steriade's group showed it survives removal of thalamic input and appears in isolated cortical slabs, and Maria Sanchez-Vives and David McCormick demonstrated in 2000 that even a slice of cortex in a dish, kept in the right ionic bath, spontaneously produces up and down states.

The accepted mechanistic sketch is a cycle of recovery and exhaustion: during the down state, recurrent excitation is quiescent and resources recover, until spontaneous synaptic events or intrinsically active cells - with cortical layer 5 pyramidal neurons prominent among the igniters - trip the densely interconnected network into regenerative, self-sustaining firing, the up state.

The up state then undermines itself over hundreds of milliseconds: activity-dependent potassium currents build up, synaptic transmission fatigues, and inhibition organizes, until the network tips back into silence and the cycle restarts.

The thalamus, though not required, is a full partner in the intact brain, helping trigger and synchronize up states, and the slow oscillation acts as the master frame of deep sleep, grouping spindles and hippocampal sharp-wave ripples into its depolarized phase - the temporal nesting at the heart of the memory consolidation dialogue explored in Chapter 14.

For the engineering-minded reader, the slow oscillation is the closest thing biology has to a global synchronization pulse, a slow system-wide frame signal within which faster local transactions are scheduled; the analogy is developed properly in the konnectome chapters.


CHAPTER 8. INHIBITION AS THE CLOCK (CORE)

If Chapter 7 built rhythms from cells that tick on their own, this chapter builds them from a circuit motif so important that it earns its own chapter: inhibition as the timing signal.

The star rhythm here is gamma, the fast oscillation of roughly 30 to 80 hertz associated with attentive processing, and the star cell is the fast-spiking inhibitory interneuron, in particular the parvalbumin-expressing basket cell - a GABA-releasing neuron, named for a calcium-binding protein it contains, whose axon weaves basket-like nests around the cell bodies of pyramidal neurons.

By the end of the chapter, the paradox from Chapter 5 resolves into a principle: the cells nearly invisible in the field are the ones holding the stopwatch.

8.1 The two gamma mechanisms

Theorists and experimenters converged in the 1990s on two related circuit recipes for gamma, distinguished by whether the excitatory pyramidal cells are part of the clockwork or merely its audience.

Both recipes share one essential ingredient - the decay of inhibition - and this section presents them and the experiment that tested the shared core.

8.1.1 Interneuron network gamma from mutual inhibition

The first recipe, interneuron network gamma, abbreviated ING, needs nothing but interneurons inhibiting one another.

The founding demonstrations were a 1995 experiment and model by Miles Whittington, Roger Traub, and John Jefferys, who found that hippocampal networks bathed in drugs that silenced fast excitatory transmission but activated interneurons still produced robust gamma oscillations, and a 1996 computational study by Xiao-Jing Wang and Gyorgy Buzsaki showing that a model network of mutually inhibitory interneurons synchronizes in the gamma range when driven.

The mechanism reads like a self-imposed curfew.

Suppose the interneuron population receives steady excitatory drive, so every cell wants to fire.

Whichever cells fire first release GABA onto the rest, silencing the whole population, including, through mutual connections, cells like themselves.

Nobody can fire again until the inhibition decays, and because all the cells were silenced together, they all recover eligibility together and fire in a near-synchronous volley - which reimposes the curfew, and the cycle repeats.

The population thus ticks with a period set by how long the inhibition lasts, and Wang and Buzsaki's analysis showed the synchrony is robust when the inhibitory synapses are relatively slow compared to the firing period and the network is sufficiently interconnected, with gap junctions between interneurons further stabilizing the beat.

ING is a pure example of the chapter's principle: the clock is made entirely of inhibition, and excitatory cells, where present, are simply entrained listeners who can only fire in the gaps the curfew allows.

8.1.2 Pyramidal-interneuron network gamma from the excitation-inhibition loop

The second recipe, pyramidal-interneuron network gamma, abbreviated PING, makes the excitatory cells full partners in the loop.

The cycle runs as a two-beat conversation between the two populations.

Beat one: pyramidal cells, driven by whatever input the circuit is processing, begin to fire, and their excitatory synapses rapidly recruit the fast-spiking interneurons - a strong, fast, reliable connection, which is exactly what makes parvalbumin basket cells the responders of choice.

Beat two: the interneurons fire a volley a few milliseconds later and blanket the pyramidal cells with inhibition, shutting them down; then the inhibition decays, the still-present drive brings the pyramidal cells back, and beat one begins again.

The signature of PING in recordings is precisely this few-millisecond lag of interneuron firing behind pyramidal firing within each gamma cycle, a phase relationship documented in hippocampal and cortical data and formalized in models by theorists including Nancy Kopell, Bard Ermentrout, Roger Traub, and Whittington around 2000.

PING has an attractive functional property: because the pyramidal cells' own activity launches each round of inhibition, the rhythm's strength and frequency track the excitatory drive - stronger drive recruits inhibition faster and shortens the cycle.

That same property is the source of a critique this book flagged in its opening discipline: Supratim Ray and John Maunsell showed in 2010 that visual cortical gamma frequency shifts with stimulus contrast, which is natural behavior for a PING oscillator but awkward for theories that need gamma to be a stable reference clock shared between regions.

Mechanism and function must therefore be kept separate: PING as a circuit mechanism is on firm ground; what the resulting rhythm is for remains the contested territory of Part Five.

In real cortex, ING and PING are not mutually exclusive; the mechanisms can coexist and trade dominance depending on how much drive reaches each population, and hybrid regimes are the rule in detailed models.

8.1.3 The experimental adjudication by driving parvalbumin cells

For over a decade the two recipes lived mainly in dishes and equations, until optogenetics - the technique of installing light-sensitive channel proteins in genetically chosen cell types so their firing can be commanded with laser light - allowed a direct causal test in living animals.

Two papers published back to back in Nature in 2009 delivered it.

Jessica Cardin, Marie Carlen, and colleagues, working with Christopher Moore and Li-Huei Tsai, expressed the light-activated channel channelrhodopsin-2 in either parvalbumin interneurons or pyramidal cells of mouse somatosensory cortex and drove each population with rhythmic light pulses across a range of frequencies.

Driving parvalbumin interneurons selectively amplified oscillations in the gamma range, with the strongest resonance near 40 hertz, whereas driving pyramidal cells preferentially amplified low frequencies instead - a double dissociation showing that the gamma band is specifically the province of the fast-spiking inhibitory population.

Vikaas Sohal, Feng Zhang, Ofer Yizhar, and Karl Deisseroth ran the complementary manipulations: suppressing parvalbumin interneurons with an inhibitory light-driven pump reduced gamma power, enhancing their activity boosted it, and imposing gamma-frequency inhibition on pyramidal cells reduced their spike-timing variability and enhanced the information their spike trains carried about inputs.

Together the 2009 studies established, causally and in vivo, the shared core of both recipes: rhythmic parvalbumin-cell inhibition is sufficient to generate and pace cortical gamma.

The resonance near 40 hertz also indicated that the cortex is not a neutral medium but a tuned one, ready to ring at gamma when its inhibitory clock is struck.

This is one of the two clean causal anchors named in this book's grading discipline, and it is worth stating the grade precisely: what is established at the level of COMPUTATION-grade evidence here is the mechanism-to-rhythm link, and Sohal's gain-and-information results begin - but only begin - the rhythm-to-function link, whose fuller adjudication, including later work by Siegle and colleagues in 2014 on behavioral effects, belongs to the gamma function chapters.

Overselling the 2009 papers as proof that gamma binds perception would violate the discipline; underselling them would too, for they are as direct as circuit causation gets.

8.2 Inhibition-based timing as a principle

The gamma story generalizes: across rhythms and regions, inhibition is the brain's preferred material for building clocks.

This closing section states the principle quantitatively and then widens it into the interneuron division of labor.

8.2.1 The inhibitory decay constant sets the gamma period

Why is gamma the frequency it is?

The answer, remarkable in its simplicity, is that the gamma period is set chiefly by a molecular time constant: the decay of the fast inhibitory synaptic current.

When GABA binds the GABA-A receptor - the fast, chloride-conducting receptor type - the resulting inhibitory current rises in about a millisecond and decays away with a time constant typically in the range of 5 to 10 milliseconds at fast-spiking synapses.

Add the few milliseconds needed for the cells to recover and reach threshold, and the natural cycle length lands near 15 to 30 milliseconds - which is to say 30 to 70 hertz, the gamma band.

The dependence was demonstrated directly: in the Whittington, Traub, and Jefferys experiments of 1995 and much subsequent work, drugs that prolong the GABA-A current, such as barbiturates and anesthetic agents, slow the oscillation in proportion, while conditions that shorten inhibition speed it up.

Wang and Buzsaki's 1996 model made the same point from theory, mapping network frequency as a function of the inhibitory decay constant and drive.

Pause on how strange and how elegant this is: a network-level tempo, visible in a field recording summing millions of cells, is inherited from the closing speed of a single species of receptor channel - biology setting a system clock with a molecular resistor-capacitor circuit, the same way an electronic oscillator's period is set by how fast a capacitor discharges through a resistor.

The principle extends beyond gamma: slower GABA-B receptor currents, with decay constants of hundreds of milliseconds, have been implicated in setting slower rhythms, and wherever an oscillation's period must be explained, the decay times of the participating inhibitory currents are the first place theorists look.

It also yields a practical warning: any drug, mutation, or disease process that alters GABA-A kinetics - anesthetics, alcohol, benzodiazepines, developmental channel variants - is expected to retune the brain's fast clocks, a thread taken up in the chapters on anesthesia and disease.

8.2.2 Interneuron diversity and the mapping of cell type to rhythm

The final widening of the principle is that inhibition is not one thing: the cortex and hippocampus contain a zoo of inhibitory interneuron types, and they map with striking specificity onto different rhythms and different phases within each rhythm.

The definitive early cartography came from Thomas Klausberger and Peter Somogyi, who recorded identified interneurons in the hippocampus of living animals and, in a landmark 2008 synthesis in Science, described at least 21 distinct interneuron classes in the CA1 region alone, each with its own targeting territory on the pyramidal cell and its own timing during network rhythms.

The division of labor is spatial and temporal at once.

Parvalbumin basket cells clamp the pyramidal cell body, the site of final spike generation, making them ideal fast gatekeepers - and they fire phase-locked within gamma cycles and on a preferred phase of theta, consistent with their role as the gamma clock established above.

Axo-axonic cells, also parvalbumin-expressing, target the axon initial segment, the very trigger zone of the spike, wielding veto power at the last possible point.

Somatostatin-expressing cells, including the oriens-lacunosum-moleculare cells of the hippocampus, target the distal dendrites where inputs arrive, and their firing is strongly theta-modulated while barely locked to gamma - dendrite-controlling, slower-rhythm specialists rather than fast-clock components.

Cholecystokinin-expressing basket cells, a chemically distinct basket type modulated by mood-related and cannabinoid signaling, fire on a different theta phase than their parvalbumin counterparts, as if two shifts of workers shared the same station at different hours.

Later cortical work sharpened the frequency mapping: a 2017 study by Guang Chen, Bernardo Rudy, and colleagues, among others, found that parvalbumin cells preferentially support gamma while somatostatin cells contribute to slower beta-band and low-gamma rhythms, suggesting that the cortex's palette of frequencies is built from its palette of inhibitory time constants and targeting geometries.

The synthesis of this chapter can now be said in one breath: the brain's fast clocks are inhibitory circuits, their tempos are set by inhibitory decay times, their phases are divided among specialized inhibitory cell types acting on different parts of the pyramidal cell at different moments - and the pyramidal cells, the only cells loud enough for our distant electrodes to hear, broadcast a rhythm that is largely the sound of being timed.

For the konnectome reader, the engineering translation is direct and will recur: inhibition here plays the role of a clock distribution network with multiple clock domains, where each interneuron class is a distinct clock line with its own period and duty cycle, gating when the compute elements may write their outputs - and the design test of Part Thirteen will ask, honestly, when an explicit digital scheduler does this job better than a decaying chemical curfew does.

CHAPTER 9. THE MATHEMATICS OF SYNCHRONY (APPARATUS)

Before we can judge whether brain waves do work, we need the mathematics that explains how millions of separate cells can tick together in the first place.

This chapter is the apparatus for that question, and it asks nothing of you but patience: every idea here can be built from the everyday picture of clocks, pendulums, and crowds falling into step.

A neuron, for the newcomer, is a brain cell that sends brief electrical pulses called spikes to other neurons across tiny connection points called synapses.

An oscillator is anything that repeats a cycle on its own, like a metronome or a dripping tap, and the mathematics of synchrony is the study of what happens when many such repeaters can nudge each other.

The payoff of this chapter is a small set of tools - the phase-response curve, the Kuramoto model, and the neural mass model - that turn the vague phrase "the neurons synchronise" into equations with testable consequences.

9.1 Coupled-oscillator theory

The first family of tools treats each rhythmic neuron, or each small rhythmic circuit, as a single self-sustaining oscillator, and asks how one oscillator shifts another when they are connected.

The strategy is deliberately humble: instead of tracking every ion channel, it compresses each oscillator down to a single number, its phase, meaning where in its cycle it currently is, the way a clock face compresses the whole mechanism down to the position of a hand.

9.1.1 Limit cycles and the phase-response curve

A limit cycle is the mathematician's name for a stable, self-restoring rhythm: if you disturb the system a little, it returns to the same loop, the way a grandfather clock nudged by a passing truck settles back into its familiar tick.

A regularly spiking neuron is a limit-cycle oscillator in exactly this sense, because its voltage traces the same closed loop cycle after cycle.

The phase-response curve, abbreviated PRC, is the single most useful summary of such an oscillator, and it answers one plain question: if a brief input arrives at a particular moment in the cycle, does the next spike come earlier or later, and by how much.

Imagine pushing a child on a swing: a push given as the swing moves away from you speeds the cycle up, a push given as it comes toward you slows it down, and the phase-response curve is simply the full table of "push at this moment, get this much speed-up or slow-down."

Experimenters measure it directly by injecting tiny current pulses into a spiking neuron at many different points in its cycle and recording how the timing of the next spike shifts, a method formalised in neuroscience through the work of theorists including Arthur Winfree in the 1970s and Bard Ermentrout from the 1980s onward.

Neurons come in two broad flavours of curve: a Type I phase-response curve, in which a small excitatory input always advances the next spike no matter when it arrives, and a Type II curve, in which the same input advances the spike at some phases but delays it at others.

That difference matters enormously for synchrony, because theory and experiment agree that Type II neurons, which can both push and pull, lock into synchrony with each other far more readily than Type I neurons, which can only push.

The shape of a few millivolts' worth of curve, measured in a single cell, thus predicts whether a whole network of such cells will fall into step, which is exactly the kind of bridge from cell to wave that this book needs.

9.1.2 Weakly coupled oscillator theory and the effect of conduction delay

The theory of weakly coupled oscillators, developed in neuroscience largely by Ermentrout and Nancy Kopell in the 1980s and 1990s, makes a simplifying bet: if the connections between oscillators are weak, each oscillator stays close to its own limit cycle, and all that the coupling can do is gently shift phases.

Under that bet, the whole interaction between two neurons reduces to a beautifully simple recipe: combine the input one neuron sends with the phase-response curve of the neuron receiving it, and you can predict whether the pair will lock in step, lock in opposite phase, or drift.

The bet buys enormous clarity, because networks of thousands of cells become tractable pencil-and-paper problems, and it produced one of the field's most counterintuitive results: under many conditions, mutual inhibition, meaning connections that suppress the partner, synchronises neurons better than mutual excitation does.

That result, associated with work by Ermentrout, Kopell, Xiao-Jing Wang, John Rinzel, and others in the 1990s, is a cornerstone of the standard model of the gamma rhythm, in which a population of inhibitory interneurons - neurons whose job is to quiet their neighbours - acts as the network's metronome.

Now add the complication the brain cannot avoid: conduction delay, the time a signal takes to travel down a nerve fibre, which ranges from well under a millisecond between neighbouring cells to tens of milliseconds between distant brain regions.

Delay changes everything, because a nudge computed for "now" arrives at a different phase of the receiver's cycle, and modelling studies show that as delays grow, stable zero-lag synchrony - everyone ticking at exactly the same moment - becomes harder to sustain, and phase-lagged or antiphase patterns take over.

Detailed analyses of pulse-coupled oscillators with delays, such as the work of Carmen Canavier and colleagues in the 2000s and 2010s, show that even the direction of the effect can flip: with short delays, inhibitory coupling can favour synchrony where excitatory coupling disrupts it.

Hold on to this point, because it becomes one of the book's three recurring objections: when two brain regions are separated by delays of 5 to 30 milliseconds, and a gamma cycle lasts only 12 to 30 milliseconds, the claim that they share a meaningful common phase must clear a high mathematical bar.

9.1.3 The Kuramoto model and the synchronisation transition

The Kuramoto model, proposed by the Japanese physicist Yoshiki Kuramoto in 1975, is the single most famous mathematical picture of synchrony, and it is worth learning in plain words because it appears everywhere from firefly swarms to power grids to brain models.

Picture a large crowd of runners on a circular track, each with a slightly different natural speed, which stands for the fact that no two neurons or circuits have exactly the same preferred frequency.

Now add one rule: each runner looks at the whole crowd and adjusts speed slightly toward the pack, speeding up if behind the average position, slowing down if ahead, with the strength of that adjustment called the coupling.

Kuramoto's model is exactly this - a set of phase oscillators with different natural frequencies, each pulled toward the average phase of all the others - and he solved it exactly in the limit of very many oscillators.

The famous result is the synchronisation transition: when the coupling is below a critical value, the runners drift apart and the crowd stays smeared around the track, but the moment coupling crosses that threshold, a cluster of runners abruptly condenses into a moving pack, and the pack grows as coupling grows.

Synchrony, in other words, is not gradual bookkeeping but a phase transition, the same mathematical kind of event as water freezing: nothing, nothing, nothing, then suddenly order.

The model gives us the order parameter, a single number between 0 and 1 that measures how tightly the crowd is bunched, with 0 meaning complete disorder and 1 meaning perfect lockstep, and this number is now a standard measurement applied to real brain recordings.

The critical coupling depends on the spread of natural frequencies: the more alike the oscillators are, the less coupling is needed to synchronise them, which translates into a biological prediction that circuits with well-matched time constants synchronise cheaply.

Modern extensions add realism - networks with brain-like wiring, conduction delays, and noise - and studies running the Kuramoto model on measured human connectomes, such as work by Géza Ódor and colleagues in 2019, find that brain-like wiring pushes the system into a broad regime of partial, flexible synchrony rather than the all-or-nothing transition of the original model.

That is a satisfying result, because a brain locked at order parameter 1 would be having a seizure, and a brain stuck at 0 could bind nothing: the interesting computation, if there is any, lives in the tunable middle.

9.2 Population and field models

The second family of tools gives up on tracking individual neurons altogether and instead writes equations for populations: what fraction of the excitatory cells in this patch of tissue is active right now, and what fraction of the inhibitory cells.

These are called neural mass models when they treat a patch as a single lump, and neural field models when they spread the equations continuously across the cortical sheet, and they matter here because they are the standard machines for explaining how measured brain waves arise.

9.2.1 The Wilson-Cowan excitation-inhibition oscillator

In 1972 Hugh Wilson and Jack Cowan published a pair of equations that became the founding document of population modelling, describing the interplay of two lumped variables: the average activity of an excitatory population and the average activity of an inhibitory population.

The intuition is a chase: excitatory cells excite each other and also excite the inhibitory cells, activity therefore climbs, the inhibitory cells then switch on and suppress everyone, activity collapses, the inhibition fades because its drive is gone, and the excitatory cells recover and begin the climb again.

That loop - excitation rising, inhibition catching up, both falling, repeat - is a natural oscillator, and the Wilson-Cowan model shows with just two equations that rhythm is not something a circuit needs special pacemaker parts for, but something any balanced excitatory-inhibitory pair will do in the right parameter range.

The period of the rhythm is set by the time constants of the two populations, meaning how quickly each responds, which is the mathematical form of a claim this corpus makes repeatedly: a rhythm is a time constant made visible.

The model also shows the other regimes that the same circuit can occupy - a quiet steady state, a saturated high state, and the oscillation between them - and the switches between regimes as inputs change are a first sketch of how a brain state change can retune a rhythm.

9.2.2 The Jansen-Rit column and the alpha rhythm

In 1995 Ben Jansen and Vincent Rit, building on earlier modelling by Fernando Lopes da Silva in the 1970s, published a neural mass model of a single cortical column - a millimetre-scale vertical slab of cortex - with three interacting populations: pyramidal cells, meaning the large excitatory neurons whose summed currents dominate the electroencephalography (EEG) signal, plus one excitatory and one inhibitory population of interneurons feeding back onto them.

The model's claim to fame is specific and checkable: with physiologically plausible time constants, its simulated output oscillates at roughly 8 to 12 cycles per second and looks strikingly like the human alpha rhythm, the prominent wave recorded over the back of the head when a person relaxes with eyes closed.

It also produces realistic evoked potentials, the stereotyped voltage ripples that follow a sensory stimulus, from the same equations, which was an early argument that ongoing rhythm and evoked response are two behaviours of one underlying circuit.

Driven harder, the same model slips into large slow spike-like oscillations resembling epileptic discharge, and this double life - alpha in one parameter region, seizure-like activity in a neighbouring one - made Jansen-Rit and its descendants, such as the Wendling model of 2002, standard tools in epilepsy modelling.

For the beginner the lesson is the modesty of the machinery: nothing in the model knows about attention or consciousness, and yet the most famous human brain wave falls out of three populations and their delays, which is quiet evidence for the substrate reading of alpha's mere existence, whatever one concludes later about its uses.

9.2.3 Next-generation neural mass models and neural field theory

Classical neural mass models have a known dishonesty: they assume the population's average activity is all that matters, and they simply posit the equation linking average input to average output rather than deriving it from spiking cells.

In 2015 Ernest Montbrió, Diego Pazó, and Alex Roxin published an exact fix, deriving from an infinite network of simple spiking neurons a closed pair of equations for the population's firing rate and its average voltage, with no averaging fudge.

These so-called next-generation neural mass models carry a crucial extra dial that the old models lacked: the degree of synchrony inside the population is tracked, not assumed, so the model can distinguish a population that is loud because every cell fires in lockstep from one that is equally loud but asynchronous.

That distinction is precisely the engine-versus-exhaust distinction of this book expressed in equations, and next-generation models are now the preferred tool for studying events where within-population synchrony itself changes, such as the alpha blocking that occurs when the eyes open.

Neural field theory, developed by Shun-ichi Amari in 1977 and extended by researchers including Paul Nunez, Peter Robinson, and Jack Cowan, takes the final step and treats the cortex as a continuous excitable sheet, with equations resembling those for waves on a drumhead.

Field models predict global standing-wave patterns whose frequencies depend on the size of the head and the conduction speed of the long fibres beneath the cortex, offering an alternative account of alpha as a global resonance of the whole sheet rather than a local circuit's tick, a live and unresolved contrast we will meet again in section 9.3.2.

9.3 The dynamical vocabulary

This section collects the remaining words of the trade - entrainment, Arnold tongues, resonance, standing and traveling waves - so that the rest of the book can use them without pausing.

Each term earns its place because a later chapter's dispute turns on it.

9.3.1 Entrainment, Arnold tongues, and oscillations as band-pass filters

Entrainment means one oscillator locking its timing to a rhythmic input, the way you fall into step with a drumbeat, and it is the mechanism claimed whenever a scientist says the brain "locks onto" speech rhythms or flickering light or an applied electrical current.

The mathematics says entrainment is conditional, and the condition is drawn as an Arnold tongue, named after the Russian mathematician Vladimir Arnold: on a map with the input's frequency along one axis and its strength along the other, the region where locking succeeds forms a triangle, narrow at the bottom and widening upward.

Read the triangle plainly: a weak rhythmic input can only capture an oscillator whose natural frequency is already very close to its own, while a strong input can drag an oscillator from further away.

This gives experimenters a signature to demand: genuine entrainment should get easier as the driving strength rises and as the driving frequency approaches the brain's own preferred frequency, and a 2021 study by Marcello Massimini's and Flavio Fröhlich's field, specifically Fröhlich and colleagues, mapped exactly such an Arnold tongue for alpha oscillations entrained by transcranial alternating current stimulation in animals.

The same mathematics explains why an oscillating circuit behaves as a band-pass filter, meaning a device that responds strongly to inputs near its own frequency and weakly to inputs far from it, a property demonstrated in single neurons as subthreshold resonance by Bruno Hutcheon and Yosef Yarom in a much-cited 2000 review.

The vocabulary matters because a recurring dispute in Part Ten is whether the brain truly entrains to speech or merely produces a fresh evoked response to each syllable, and the Arnold tongue is one of the few tools that can tell those apart.

9.3.2 Standing synchrony against traveling waves

The textbook picture of a brain wave is standing synchrony: a whole territory rising and falling together, in phase, like a stadium crowd all standing at once.

High-density recordings have increasingly shown something else: traveling waves, in which the peak of the oscillation sweeps across the cortex like a stadium wave moving around the stands, with measured speeds in cortex commonly in the range of roughly 0.1 to 10 metres per second depending on scale.

The 2018 review by Lyle Muller, Frédéric Chavane, John Reynolds, and Terrence Sejnowski in Nature Reviews Neuroscience assembled the evidence that traveling waves appear in sensory, motor, and cognitive systems, and in sleep rhythms too: human slow oscillations were shown by Marcello Massimini and colleagues in 2004 to sweep from front to back of the head as traveling waves.

Human intracranial work by Joshua Jacobs and colleagues, including a study by Honghui Zhang and Jacobs published in 2018 and later work in 2024, found that theta and alpha rhythms in individual patients are very often traveling waves, and that their direction of travel changes with memory encoding and recall.

The distinction is not cosmetic, because a standing rhythm offers a shared clock, while a traveling wave offers something different: a moving window of excitability that visits each location at a slightly different time, which could serve to sequence or scan rather than to bind.

It is also a methodological warning: two electrodes sitting on a traveling wave will show a systematic phase lag that is easily misread as one region driving another, a trap that belongs to Chapter 11's catalogue but is born here, in the mathematics.



================================================================================

PART FIVE. THE METHODS OF ANALYSIS

================================================================================

CHAPTER 10. DECOMPOSING THE SIGNAL (APPARATUS)

A raw brain recording is a single wiggly line, and every claim in this book - that alpha fell, that gamma rose, that theta carried a memory - depends on decomposing that wiggle into parts.

This chapter explains how the decomposition is done, what it silently assumes, and where it misleads, because a reader who cannot see the assumptions cannot judge the claims.

The honest headline, established over the last decade, is that a large fraction of published brain-wave findings measured the background hiss of the brain rather than its rhythms, and the correction for that error is now a required part of the trade.

10.1 Spectral analysis

Spectral analysis is the general name for asking how much of the signal's energy sits at each frequency, producing the power spectrum, a graph with frequency along the bottom and power - the squared size of the oscillation - up the side.

Everything in this section descends from one mathematical idea, Fourier analysis, and every refinement exists to patch one of its two chronic problems: brain signals do not hold still, and short recordings give noisy estimates.

10.1.1 Fourier analysis and its stationarity assumption

Joseph Fourier's insight, published in 1822 in his theory of heat, is that any wiggly line can be rebuilt exactly as a sum of pure sine waves - smooth, endless, perfectly regular oscillations - of different frequencies, sizes, and alignments.

The Fourier transform is the recipe that finds those ingredient sine waves, and the fast Fourier transform, the efficient algorithm published by James Cooley and John Tukey in 1965, is why every EEG software package computes spectra in milliseconds.

The catch is the stationarity assumption: the decomposition describes the signal as if its rhythmic content were the same from the first second of the recording to the last, the way a recipe lists ingredients without saying when each was added.

Brain signals violate this constantly, since a burst of alpha lasting one second inside a one-minute recording is real and important, yet the plain Fourier spectrum of the whole minute smears that burst into a small bump, indistinguishable from a weak rhythm present throughout.

The violation matters for interpretation as much as for measurement: work by researchers including Maureen Jones, Stephanie Jones and colleagues on the beta rhythm from 2016 onward showed that what averaged spectra present as sustained beta oscillations is, in single moments, mostly brief punchy events lasting one to three cycles, so the smooth "rhythm" of the textbook may be an artefact of the averaging.

10.1.2 Time-frequency analysis and the uncertainty trade-off

Time-frequency analysis patches the stationarity problem by chopping the recording into short windows and computing a spectrum for each, producing a picture called a spectrogram with time along the bottom, frequency up the side, and power as colour.

The standard tools are the short-time Fourier transform, which uses fixed-length windows, and the wavelet transform, which uses a small wave template - commonly the Morlet wavelet - stretched for low frequencies and compressed for high ones, so that each frequency is measured with a window matched to its own cycle length.

Every such method pays an unavoidable tax, the time-frequency uncertainty principle, which is the same mathematics as Heisenberg's uncertainty principle in physics: sharpening your knowledge of when something happened necessarily blurs your knowledge of its exact frequency, and the reverse.

Concretely, a wavelet tuned to 6 cycles at 6 hertz - hertz meaning cycles per second - spans about one full second, so a theta event it reports at time zero actually reflects signal up to half a second before and after, and any claim about precise event timing at low frequencies must respect that blur.

The practical discipline is to choose the trade-off to fit the question - short windows to time a gamma burst, long windows to resolve whether a peak sits at 9 or at 11 hertz - and to distrust any figure whose stated precision beats the uncertainty limit, because that precision is cosmetic.

10.1.3 Multitaper estimation

A spectrum computed from a single short window is noisy, jumping wildly from one frequency bin to the next, and the multitaper method, introduced by David Thomson in 1982, is the field's standard cure.

A taper is a smooth weighting function that fades the data in and out at the window's edges, preventing the sharp edges from splattering false power across the spectrum, an artefact called leakage.

Thomson's move was to use not one taper but a whole family of them - the Slepian tapers, mathematically optimal shapes derived by David Slepian - each giving a statistically independent look at the same data, and to average the resulting spectra.

Averaging several independent looks shrinks the noise roughly in proportion to the number of tapers used, at the deliberate cost of smoothing the spectrum over a chosen bandwidth, a trade the analyst sets explicitly in advance.

The method entered systems neuroscience largely through Partha Mitra and Bijan Pesaran, whose 1999 paper on analysing dynamic brain imaging data made multitaper estimation the default for spike and field recordings, and it is the engine inside the widely used Chronux toolbox.

For the reader of later chapters the point is simple: when a study claims a subtle spectral difference - a gamma peak shifting by a few hertz between conditions - the believability of that claim rests on variance-controlled estimation of exactly this kind.

10.2 The aperiodic component

Here is the correction that reshaped the field in the last decade: most of the power in a brain recording is not rhythmic at all.

Plot any EEG power spectrum on logarithmic axes and you see a long descending slope - power falling steadily as frequency rises - with at most a few bumps riding on it, and only the bumps are oscillations.

The slope is called the aperiodic component, the one-over-frequency background, or the 1/f background, and treating it correctly turns out to change the conclusions of whole literatures.

10.2.1 The one-over-frequency background and its possible meaning

The name one-over-frequency describes the shape: power is roughly proportional to one divided by frequency raised to some exponent, so each doubling of frequency cuts power by a roughly constant factor, which is why the spectrum is a straight line on log-log axes.

For decades this background was dismissed as noise and subtracted away without comment, but it is produced by the brain itself, and its steepness - the aperiodic exponent - moves systematically with brain state, with age, with anaesthesia, and with disease.

A leading account of its origin, developed in work such as Biyu He's studies of scale-free brain activity around 2010 and a 2017 model by Richard Gao, Erik Peterson, and Bradley Voytek, ties the slope to the mix of synaptic currents: excitatory currents decay quickly and inhibitory currents slowly, so the balance between excitation and inhibition tilts the spectrum, with a steeper slope indicating relatively more inhibition and a flatter slope indicating relatively more excitation.

If that account holds, the despised background is a continuous readout of the cortex's excitation-inhibition balance, arguably a more fundamental physiological variable than any single rhythm, and studies have since used the exponent to track anaesthetic depth, development across childhood, and disorders including Parkinson's disease, where a 2023 study by Ahmet Kaymak, Alberto Mazzoni, and colleagues linked the subthalamic aperiodic exponent to the excitation-inhibition effects of treatment.

The honest caveat is that the mapping from slope to excitation-inhibition balance is model-derived and indirect, several biological factors can move the slope at once, and the field treats the exponent as a promising index rather than a settled measurement.

10.2.2 Separating periodic from aperiodic, and the traps of raw band power

The traditional measure in thousands of papers is raw band power: sum the power between fixed frequency limits, for example 4 to 8 hertz for theta, and call the result "theta power."

The trap is that raw band power mixes three different things - the height of any true oscillatory bump, the height of the aperiodic background under it, and the exact frequency of the bump relative to the fixed band edges - so a change in "theta power" can occur with no change in any theta rhythm at all.

The point was made unmissable by Thomas Donoghue, Bradley Voytek, and colleagues in a 2020 paper in Nature Neuroscience, which introduced the tool called specparam, earlier known as FOOOF, that fits the spectrum as an explicit sum of a sloped aperiodic curve plus a small number of Gaussian bumps for the true oscillations.

With the fit in hand, each reported effect can be assigned to its real source - a bump growing, a bump sliding in frequency, or the whole background tilting - and re-analyses using this decomposition have overturned findings, most prominently in ageing, where much of the famous slowing and weakening of rhythms in older adults turns out to be a flattening of the aperiodic slope misread as oscillatory change.

The trap generalises: ratios of band powers, such as the theta-beta ratio once promoted as an attention-deficit biomarker, are doubly exposed, because a single tilt of the background moves both numerator and denominator in opposite directions and manufactures an effect with no rhythm involved, one concrete reason that biomarker failed, as Chapter 11's statistics and the clinical chapters will take up.

The working rule this book adopts is strict: a claim about an oscillation is credible only if the analysis separated the oscillation from the background, and pre-2020 findings based on raw band power inherit an asterisk-free but real question mark.

10.2.3 What counts as an oscillation above the background

Once the background is fitted, a bracing question follows: is there any rhythm here at all.

The modern criterion is that an oscillation exists in a recording only if the spectrum shows a peak rising clearly above the fitted aperiodic curve, and by that criterion oscillations are not universal: many individuals, many brain regions, and many task states show no detectable peak in a given band.

Large-sample work applying specparam found, for example, that a substantial fraction of participants lack a distinct theta peak at rest, and that clear narrow-band gamma peaks in scalp EEG are the exception rather than the rule, appearing robustly mainly for particular stimuli such as high-contrast gratings.

Peak detection in the averaged spectrum is also not the end of the story, because a peak can come from occasional strong bursts rather than a sustained rhythm, so complementary time-domain tests exist: burst-detection methods that find individual events crossing a power threshold, the lagged coherence measure of rhythmicity introduced by Ole Jensen's group through Jan-Mathijs Schoffelen, and cycle-by-cycle analysis, introduced by Scott Cole and Bradley Voytek in 2019, which walks the raw signal one cycle at a time and asks whether consecutive cycles are regular enough to deserve the word oscillation.

The consequence for everything downstream is severe and clarifying: phase, the variable carrying most of the functional claims in this book, is only defined when a rhythm exists, and computing phase from a filtered signal that contains no true oscillation produces smooth, confident, meaningless numbers.

That is the first appearance of a rule the next chapter enforces relentlessly: the filter always returns a wave, whether or not the brain contains one.


CHAPTER 11. COUPLING, CONNECTIVITY, AND THEIR PITFALLS (APPARATUS)

The grandest claims for brain waves are claims about coupling: that two regions talk by aligning their phases, or that a slow rhythm carries a fast one like a wave carrying surfers.

This chapter presents the measures behind those claims and, with equal weight, the traps, because the coupling literature is the part of the field where honest methods work has retracted the most.

Nothing here is a counsel of despair: every trap has a published control, and the chapter ends with the statistical tools that let a careful study survive scrutiny.

11.1 Phase and coupling measures

The measures in this section all begin the same way: filter the signal around a frequency band, extract at every moment an instantaneous phase - where in its cycle the wave is - and an instantaneous amplitude, usually via a mathematical device called the Hilbert transform, and then ask whether phases at two places, or across many trials, line up.

The extraction step is uncontroversial machinery; every controversy lives in what the alignment is taken to mean.

11.1.1 Phase locking and inter-trial coherence

The phase-locking value, introduced to neuroscience by Jean-Philippe Lachaux, Eugenio Rodriguez, Jacques Martinerie, and Francisco Varela in 1999, measures whether the phase difference between two signals stays constant over time: imagine two clocks that may show different times but whose difference never changes, which suggests a connection between them.

It is computed by averaging the phase differences as directions on a circle, yielding a number from 0, meaning the difference wanders freely, to 1, meaning it is perfectly constant.

Inter-trial coherence applies the same idea across repetitions of an event rather than across time: if a stimulus resets the brain's oscillation, then at a fixed delay after each repetition the phase should be similar from trial to trial, and inter-trial coherence near 1 at 100 milliseconds after a flash says the flash reliably set the wave's position.

Both measures come with immediate cautions: a rise in inter-trial coherence cannot by itself distinguish a true phase reset of an ongoing oscillation from a fresh evoked response added on top of it, a distinction that haunts the entrainment debates of Part Ten, and the phase-locking value is biased upward by small sample sizes, so short recordings flatter the hypothesis.

And beneath both sits the rule from Chapter 10: phase is only meaningful where an oscillation exists, so a phase-locking analysis must first show the peak above the aperiodic background or it is measuring the alignment of fictions.

11.1.2 Coherence and the volume-conduction-robust measures

Coherence is the frequency-domain workhorse: for each frequency it measures, on a 0 to 1 scale, how consistently two signals maintain a fixed phase and amplitude relationship.

For scalp EEG and magnetoencephalography (MEG), the magnetic counterpart of EEG, coherence has a disqualifying vulnerability called volume conduction: electrical fields spread instantly through the head's tissues, so one deep source is seen simultaneously by many sensors, and any two such sensors show high coherence at zero time lag despite measuring a single source, not two connected ones.

The field's answer exploits the one honest signature available: volume conduction is instantaneous, so any coupling that involves a genuine time lag cannot be explained by it.

Guido Nolte and colleagues introduced the imaginary part of coherency in 2004, which simply discards the instantaneous, zero-lag portion of the coherence and keeps only the lagged portion; Cornelis Stam and colleagues introduced the phase lag index in 2007, which asks only whether one signal consistently leads or lags the other, ignoring by how much; and Martin Vinck and colleagues refined it in 2011 as the weighted phase lag index, which downweights the near-zero lags where noise flips the sign.

The robustness has a price that must be stated: these measures are blind to true zero-lag coupling, which genuinely exists in the brain - zero-lag synchrony between distant sites was reported by Roelfsema, Engel, König, and Singer in 1997 - so the conservative measures buy safety from false positives by accepting false negatives, and a small phase lag index does not mean no connection.

The practical reading rule for the whole literature is now simple: a scalp-level coupling claim that rests on plain coherence alone, without a volume-conduction-robust check or source-level analysis, should be treated as unproven.

11.1.3 Directed measures and their filtering traps

Correlation-style measures are symmetric, but the interesting claims are directional - region A drives region B - and the standard directional tool is Granger causality, adapted from the economist Clive Granger's 1969 work: A is said to Granger-cause B if the past of A improves the prediction of B beyond what the past of B alone achieves.

Frequency-resolved variants such as partial directed coherence and the directed transfer function extend the idea to ask at which rhythm the driving occurs, and spectral Granger measures underlie celebrated results, such as the finding by Andre Bastos, Pascal Fries, and colleagues in 2015 that signals flow up the visual hierarchy in the gamma band and down it in the alpha-beta band.

The traps here are sharp enough to have their own literature: filtering the data before Granger analysis can manufacture or destroy causality, because a filter smears each signal's past across time and corrupts exactly the prediction machinery the method relies on, a problem analysed by Luiz Baccalá, by Anil Seth and Lionel Barnett in 2011, and by Alard Roebroeck and others in the neuroimaging debate.

Three further traps deserve one sentence each: unequal noise levels in the two signals bias Granger causality toward saying the cleaner signal drives the noisier one; a third, unmeasured region driving both A and B at different delays produces confident false arrows between them; and a traveling wave passing under both electrodes, as flagged in section 9.3.2, produces a systematic lead-lag that reads as driving.

The mature position, which this book adopts, is that directed measures are hypothesis-generating descriptions of statistical precedence, valuable for building models, and are never by themselves the causal evidence that a COMPUTATION grade requires - that grade demands perturbation, not prediction.

11.2 Cross-frequency coupling

Cross-frequency coupling is the proposal that the brain's rhythms are not parallel channels but a nested hierarchy: a slow rhythm's phase organising a fast rhythm's amplitude, like a slow tide deciding when the fast surf is big.

It is the mechanistic heart of some of the field's most ambitious theories, including the theta-gamma memory code of Part Eight, and it is also the home of the book's third recurring objection, so this section teaches the measure and its failure modes with equal care.

11.2.1 Phase-amplitude coupling and the modulation index

Phase-amplitude coupling, abbreviated PAC, is the specific claim that the amplitude of a fast rhythm waxes and wanes locked to the phase of a slow one: in the classic hippocampal example, gamma bursts arrive preferentially on a particular part of each theta cycle.

The standard recipe extracts the slow rhythm's phase and the fast rhythm's amplitude, then asks whether amplitude is evenly spread around the slow cycle or piled up at a preferred phase.

The most widely used statistic is the modulation index of Adriano Tort and colleagues, published in 2010, which bins the fast amplitude by slow phase - say eighteen bins around the cycle - and measures how far the resulting distribution departs from perfectly flat, using a distance borrowed from information theory.

A flat distribution means no coupling, a single sharp pile means strong coupling, and the method's virtue is that it makes the claim visual: the tell-tale figure is a histogram of gamma amplitude around the theta cycle, bulging at the preferred phase.

Genuine, physiologically meaningful PAC exists - the theta-gamma nesting in the rodent hippocampus, first described in the lineage of work from John Lisman and Gyorgy Buzsáki's groups, is robust across laboratories - and PAC strength has been reported to track task demands in humans, most famously in a 2010 human intracranial study by Ryan Canolty and colleagues' lineage following Canolty's 2006 report of widespread theta-gamma coupling in human cortex.

The measure itself, however, is only as honest as the signals fed into it, which is the subject of the next section.

11.2.2 The spurious-coupling problem from wave shape and common drive

Here is the third recurring objection of this book, and it deserves a slow explanation because it looks like a technicality and is actually a landmine under a decade of findings.

The Fourier machinery of Chapter 10 describes every signal as a sum of sine waves, so when a real brain rhythm is non-sinusoidal - shaped like a sawtooth or a row of arches rather than a smooth sine, as hippocampal theta and the sensorimotor mu rhythm genuinely are - the mathematics is forced to represent that shape as the base frequency plus a series of harmonics, meaning exact multiples of it.

Those harmonics are not separate brain signals; they are the bookkeeping cost of describing a pointy wave with smooth ingredients.

But a PAC analysis cannot tell the difference: the harmonics of a sawtooth-shaped 8 hertz theta rhythm rise and fall in perfect lockstep with the theta cycle, so the analysis reports fast activity coupled to slow phase - textbook PAC - when in reality there is one rhythm with a pointy shape and no second rhythm at all.

This critique was developed in the mid-2010s, notably by Ole Jensen's and colleagues' methodological work, by a 2016 eNeuro study by Juan Aru, Jaan Aru, and colleagues titled a critical analysis of cross-frequency coupling, and by Scott Cole and Bradley Voytek's 2017 review on the importance of waveform shape, which reframed non-sinusoidal shape as a signal in its own right rather than a nuisance.

The second spur of spurious coupling is common drive: a sharp non-rhythmic event, such as a stimulus onset or an artefact, injects energy at all frequencies at once, so the slow and fast bands both inherit its timing and appear coupled, though each is merely coupled to the shared event.

The published controls are concrete, and a credible PAC study now shows its work: inspect the raw waveform for non-sinusoidal shape, check whether the "fast" activity sits at exact integer multiples of the slow frequency with rigidly constant phase relations, which fingerprints harmonics, compare against surrogate data built to preserve the wave shape while destroying the hypothesised coupling, and prefer broadband high-frequency activity, which harmonics cannot mimic, over a narrow fast band.

The verdict this book carries forward is calibrated, not cynical: hippocampal theta-gamma coupling survives the critique in well-controlled recordings, but a substantial share of reported cortical PAC, especially from scalp EEG, has never been re-examined under these controls, and any CONTESTED grade citing wave shape in later chapters is pointing at exactly this machinery.

11.3 Artefacts and statistics

The final section covers the unglamorous conditions of belief: the non-brain signals that imitate brain waves, the reconstruction methods that assign scalp signals to sources, and the statistics that separate a finding from a fluctuation.

Reviewers ask about these first, and so should readers.

11.3.1 Muscle, microsaccade, and filtering artefacts

The scalp is a noisy neighbourhood: the electrical activity of muscle, called electromyographic (EMG) activity, is broadband and strongest above roughly 20 hertz, sits directly on top of the EEG's beta and gamma bands, and a slight jaw clench or brow tension can dwarf cortical gamma at the electrodes.

The most instructive artefact story in the field concerns microsaccades, the tiny involuntary eye jumps made one to two times per second: each one produces a brief spike potential from the eye muscles that spreads across the scalp, and its energy lands squarely in the gamma band.

In 2008 Shlomit Yuval-Greenberg, Leon Deouell, and colleagues showed in Neuron that the widely reported broadband induced gamma response appearing 200 to 300 milliseconds after a visual stimulus - by then a staple finding linked to perception and binding - tracked the timing of microsaccades and was largely ocular muscle activity, not cortex.

The episode is this book's standing exhibit for humility: a reproducible, peer-reviewed, theory-confirming gamma effect, reported across multiple laboratories, turned out to come from the eyes, and scalp gamma claims ever since carry the burden of showing eye-tracking controls or intracranial confirmation.

Filtering adds its own manufactured shapes: sharp filters ring, producing oscillation-like ripples around abrupt events where the brain produced none, causal filters smear activity later in time and acausal filters smear it earlier, so an effect's onset time can be an artefact of the filter's direction, a set of traps catalogued in tutorial papers by Andreas Widmann and Erich Schröger in the 2010s and in Mike Cohen's standard 2014 textbook on neural time series analysis.

The rule of the section is that every claimed rhythm must survive the question "what else on or in the head oscillates at that frequency," and the honest answers include muscle, eyes, heartbeat, and the filter itself.

11.3.2 Source localisation and beamforming

Scalp sensors record blurred mixtures, and source localisation is the attempt to un-mix them: to say which places in the brain produced the recorded pattern.

The obstacle is fundamental and worth stating plainly: this inverse problem has no unique solution, because infinitely many different configurations of brain currents produce identical sensor readings, just as infinitely many arrangements of lamps behind a frosted window produce the same glow.

Every method therefore adds assumptions to pick one answer: dipole fitting assumes few focal sources, distributed methods such as minimum-norm estimation spread the explanation across the cortex as smoothly as possible, and beamforming - with the linearly constrained minimum variance beamformer as the standard variant - scans the brain location by location, building for each spot a spatial filter that passes signal from that spot while suppressing everything else.

Beamforming has become the workhorse for oscillation studies in MEG, but it carries a known blind spot: it assumes sources are uncorrelated with each other, so two regions oscillating in tight synchrony - precisely the phenomenon this book cares about - can suppress or distort each other in the reconstruction, a self-defeating property the analyst must check for.

The reader's calibration is this: source-level claims are estimates under assumptions, good ones when the assumptions are declared and tested, and the phrase "activity was localised to" always silently means "was estimated, under this method's assumptions, to arise from."

11.3.3 Cluster-based permutation, surrogates, and circular statistics

A time-frequency analysis produces tens of thousands of numbers - every time point at every frequency at every sensor - and testing each against chance guarantees false discoveries by sheer volume, the multiple-comparisons problem.

The field's standard remedy is the cluster-based permutation test of Eric Maris and Robert Oostenveld, published in 2007: find connected clumps of adjacent time-frequency-sensor points that all show an effect, measure each clump's total strength, then shuffle the condition labels many times to learn how big such clumps get by luck alone, and keep only the clumps that beat the shuffled world.

It is honest, assumption-light, and now near-universal, with one frequently forgotten limit stated by its own authors: the test licenses the conclusion that some effect exists, not precise claims about exactly when it starts or which sensor carries it, because the cluster's edges are not individually tested.

Surrogate data testing generalises the shuffling idea to single recordings: build artificial signals that preserve everything about the data except the hypothesised structure - for coupling analyses, by cutting the phase or amplitude series at a random point and swapping the pieces, which preserves each signal's shape and spectrum while destroying their alignment - and demand that the real coupling value beat the surrogate distribution.

The choice of surrogate is where rigour lives or dies, because a surrogate that destroys too much, such as fully randomising phases and thereby also destroying the non-sinusoidal wave shape, resurrects exactly the spurious PAC of section 11.2.2 and blesses it as significant.

Finally, phase is a circular quantity - 359 degrees and 1 degree are neighbours, not opposites - so ordinary averages and t-tests are simply wrong for it, and the correct tools come from circular statistics: the Rayleigh test for whether phases cluster rather than spread uniformly, and circular-linear correlations for whether phase predicts an outcome, as systematised for neuroscience in Philipp Berens's 2009 CircStat toolbox paper.

With the apparatus now complete - generation in Chapter 9, decomposition in Chapter 10, coupling and its courts of appeal here - the book can return to the brain itself, beginning with the states that set which rhythms play at all.



================================================================================

PART SIX. RHYTHMS AS THE BRAIN CHANGES STATE

================================================================================

CHAPTER 12. AROUSAL AND THE WAKING SPECTRUM (CORE)

Every rhythm described so far plays against a backdrop that the brain itself controls: the level of arousal, the difference between alert, drowsy, and asleep.

This chapter explains the machinery that sets that backdrop - a set of small, deep cell groups that act less like thinkers than like a power-management system - and reads the waking EEG as a continuous gauge of where the system currently sits.

It is a CORE chapter because none of the functional claims of later parts can be evaluated without knowing that the spectrum shifts wholesale when the state does, so that an uncontrolled change in arousal can imitate almost any cognitive effect.

12.1 The ascending systems

The cerebral cortex, the brain's folded outer sheet where perception and thought are computed, does not wake itself.

It is driven into the waking mode by signals ascending from the brainstem, the stalk connecting brain to spinal cord, and from the hypothalamus, a small structure above it that regulates bodily states, and this section traces how the modern, distributed picture of that machinery replaced the original single-system story.

12.1.1 From the reticular activating system to the distributed modern view

The founding experiment is Giuseppe Moruzzi and Horace Magoun's 1949 study: electrically stimulating the reticular formation, the diffuse core of the brainstem, in anaesthetised cats instantly switched the cortical EEG from large slow waves to the low-voltage fast pattern of waking, while damage to the same core produced an unwakeable, coma-like state.

Their conclusion named an organ, the ascending reticular activating system: a nonspecific arousal engine in the brainstem core, without which the cortex, whatever sensory input it received, would not maintain the waking state.

The naming was right about the function and wrong about the anatomy, because later work, especially from the 1980s onward, showed that the potent arousal projections arise not from an undifferentiated reticular net but from a handful of small, chemically specific nuclei near the junction of the pons and midbrain, each broadcasting its own transmitter through widespread axons.
