The pulsing has a measurable fingerprint: fast gamma activity, and with it stimulus processing, is squeezed into a preferred phase of the alpha cycle, a nesting called cross-frequency coupling, meaning the coupling of a fast rhythm's strength to a slow rhythm's phase.

Behaviour follows the phase: Busch, Dubois, and VanRullen in 2009, and Mathewson and colleagues the same year, reported that whether a threshold-level flash is consciously seen depends partly on the alpha phase at which it arrives, with detection swinging by roughly 10 to 20 percent between the best and worst phases.

Honesty requires flagging the standing objection here, one of this book's recurring three: alpha waves are notoriously non-sinusoidal, meaning their waveform is asymmetric rather than a smooth textbook wave, and Scott Cole and Bradley Voytek showed in 2017 that such asymmetric shapes can generate spurious cross-frequency coupling in the analysis even when no genuine nesting exists.

Some, though not all, of the pulsed-inhibition evidence has had to be re-examined under that objection, and phase-of-alpha perception effects have had notable replication failures, including work by Ruzzoli and colleagues in 2019.

18.1.3 The spatial-attention read-out

Whatever its mechanism, lateralised alpha is one of the most reliable read-outs in human cognitive neuroscience.

Michael Worden and colleagues first showed in 2000 that directing attention to one side produces the alpha increase over the ignoring hemisphere's visual cortex, and Gregor Thut and colleagues in 2006 showed that the size of this lateralisation predicts reaction times before the stimulus even appears.

The effect is robust enough to be decoded in real time, which has made alpha lateralisation a workhorse for brain-computer interfaces and for basic attention research, and versions of it appear in hearing and touch, not only vision.

A causal wrinkle deserves mention because this book weighs causality heavily: studies using rhythmic transcranial magnetic stimulation, brief magnetic pulses applied through the skull in an alpha-paced train, by Vincenzo Romei, Thut, and colleagues around 2010, reported that imposing alpha rhythmically on one hemisphere impaired detection of stimuli in the opposite visual field, as the inhibition account predicts.

These experiments have their own confound debates, including sensory side-effects of the stimulation, so they lift the account without settling it.

18.2 Rhythmic sampling

The second half of the chapter zooms out from one region's off-switch to a stranger claim about attention as a whole: that even when attention feels continuous, it is sampling the world in discrete beats.

18.2.1 Attention as a periodic spotlight at theta rhythm

Theta, in this literature, means rhythms of roughly 3 to 8 cycles per second.

The founding behavioural result is by Ayelet Landau and Pascal Fries in Current Biology in 2012: after a brief flash drew attention, detection of faint targets at two locations did not decline smoothly but oscillated over time, at about 4 cycles per second per location, and in antiphase between the two locations - when one location was at its best, the other was at its worst.

The picture is a spotlight that does not shine steadily but sweeps, sampling one location, then the other, roughly eight times per second in total.

Ian Fiebelkorn, Sabine Kastner, and colleagues built the neural half of the story: recording in the monkey fronto-parietal attention network while measuring perception, they showed in 2018 that detection at an attended location waxes and wanes with the phase of theta activity in these attention-controlling regions, and that different theta phases favour either holding the current focus or shifting to a new one.

Their 2019 synthesis in Trends in Cognitive Sciences, A Rhythmic Theory of Attention, proposes that attention intrinsically alternates between sampling and shifting states several times per second, which would explain why exploration never fully stops even during concentration.

This claim, too, is contested at full strength: a 2022 reanalysis by Geoffrey Brookshire argued that the standard statistical methods for detecting behavioural rhythms confuse aperiodic, meaning non-rhythmic, temporal structure with true oscillation, and re-examined datasets found weaker rhythmic evidence than originally claimed.

Fiebelkorn's 2023 reply argued that the neural evidence, phase-linked perception measured directly against brain rhythms, stands even where purely behavioural rhythmicity is statistically fragile, and independent 2023 work using concurrent recording continued to find theta-linked sampling.

The fair summary is that rhythmic sampling is a live, actively disputed hypothesis with serious evidence on both sides, not a settled fact.

18.2.2 Two rhythms: alpha excitability windows against theta selection

The two halves of this chapter describe two different rhythms doing two different jobs, and keeping them distinct prevents a common confusion.

Alpha, at 8 to 13 cycles per second, is proposed to set local excitability: it decides, region by region and roughly ten times per second, how open each patch of cortex is.

Theta, at 3 to 8 cycles per second, is proposed to schedule selection: it decides, a few times per second, where the fronto-parietal control system points the spotlight, and whether to hold or shift.

The proposed relationship is hierarchical, in the spirit of Charles Schroeder and Peter Lakatos's influential 2009 framework of rhythmic attentional sampling: the slower theta cycle of the control system modulates, and is interleaved with, the faster alpha and gamma dynamics in sensory cortex, so that each theta-paced visit to a location opens a train of alpha-paced excitability windows there.

In engineering terms, offered gently ahead of the full translation chapters: theta behaves like a scheduler's time slice, the fixed slot of processor time an operating system gives each task before switching, and alpha behaves like clock gating within the slice, disabling the components not in use.

The both-sides rule applies as always: an explicit digital scheduler with a task list and timestamps does this job with none of the ambiguity of coupled rhythms, and the konnectome design test asks whether the rhythmic version's advantages, chiefly that arbitration happens with no central list at all, are worth its imprecision.

18.3 The grading

Alpha earns the most interesting grade in this part of the book, because its evidence includes something the binding story never had: perturbation results pointing the right way.

18.3.1 Alpha as scheduled inhibition. Grade: CONTESTED, leaning COMPUTATION

The supporting evidence: alpha power rises specifically over task-irrelevant cortex under top-down control; high pre-stimulus alpha predicts misses; single-neuron firing drops as alpha rises; and rhythmic stimulation imposing alpha impairs perception on the predicted side.

The objections, at full strength: the non-sinusoidal waveform objection undermines part of the pulsed-inhibition and cross-frequency evidence; phase-of-alpha perception effects have failed to replicate in several careful attempts; the rhythmic stimulation results carry sensory-confound worries; and correlational alpha increases could in principle follow, rather than cause, the withdrawal of processing.

Why the lean toward COMPUTATION rather than toward SUBSTRATE: unlike gamma in chapter 16, alpha's story survives its objections in weakened rather than inverted form - the objections attack the pulsed fine structure and some phase effects, but the core claims, regional and directional inhibition that predicts behaviour and can be pushed causally, have independent converging support.

What would settle it upward is the same kind of experiment as always: a perturbation that changes alpha specifically, without sensory side-effects and without changing overall activity, that reliably re-routes processing as the gating account predicts.

Until then, CONTESTED, leaning COMPUTATION, is the honest reading, and it makes alpha the strongest CORE-chapter candidate in this part of the book for eventual promotion.


CHAPTER 19. FREQUENCY BY DIRECTION: THE PREDICTIVE-CODING MAPPING (CORE)

The last chapter of this part asks whether the brain's frequency bands have jobs assigned by direction of travel: fast rhythms carrying messages up the processing hierarchy, slow rhythms carrying messages down.

If true, this would be a discovery of rare elegance, because it would tie the taxonomy of brain waves to the deepest current theory of what the cortex computes.

If false, it would be a cautionary tale about how elegant mappings seduce.

The evidence, as of this writing, contains genuine support and genuine exceptions, and the chapter ends on the grade the outline demands: CONTESTED.

19.1 The proposal

Predictive coding is the theory that the cortex works as a prediction machine: higher areas continuously send predictions of expected input down to lower areas, lower areas compare predictions against actual input, and only the mismatch, called prediction error, is sent back up.

On this theory the cortex has two distinct message types flowing in opposite directions, and the hypothesis of this chapter, articulated most fully by Andre Bastos, Karl Friston, Pascal Fries, and colleagues in a 2012 Neuron paper titled Canonical Microcircuits for Predictive Coding, is that the two message types are carried on different frequency bands.

19.1.1 Gamma carrying feedforward, alpha and beta carrying feedback

Feedforward means the direction from sensory input toward higher areas, for example from primary visual cortex, called V1, to the higher visual area V4; feedback means the reverse.

The claim: feedforward influence travels predominantly in the gamma band, roughly 30 to 80 cycles per second, while feedback influence travels predominantly in the alpha and beta bands, roughly 8 to 30 cycles per second.

The flagship evidence is a pair of 2014 to 2016 studies.

Bastos, Fries, and colleagues in Neuron in 2015 recorded simultaneously from eight visual areas in monkeys and measured directed influence, meaning statistical estimates of which area's signal predicts which other's, in each frequency band.

Gamma influence ran almost exclusively up the anatomical hierarchy, and alpha-beta influence ran predominantly down it, so cleanly that the authors could reconstruct the anatomical ordering of the areas from the frequency-band asymmetries alone.

Timothée van Kerkoerle, Pieter Roelfsema, and colleagues in 2014 showed the same division within and between V1 and V4, and added a causal touch: microstimulation, injecting tiny currents, in the higher area produced alpha-like effects in the lower area, while stimulating the lower area produced gamma-like effects in the higher one.

Georgios Michalareas, Fries, and colleagues extended the pattern to humans in 2016 using magnetoencephalography (MEG), the technique of measuring the brain's tiny magnetic fields from outside the head, finding the same gamma-up, alpha-beta-down asymmetry across the human visual hierarchy.

19.1.2 The laminar signature by cortical layer

The cortex is a layered sheet, conventionally divided into six layers, and the mapping has an anatomical alibi that makes it more than numerology.

Feedforward anatomical projections originate mainly in the superficial layers, layers 2 and 3, and terminate in the middle layer, layer 4; feedback projections originate mainly in the deep layers, 5 and 6, and skip layer 4.

The physiological finding, reported across studies by Bastos, by van Kerkoerle, and by work in the laboratory of Earl Miller, is that gamma rhythms are strongest in the superficial layers while alpha and beta rhythms are strongest in the deep layers, aligning each band with the layers that anatomically send its proposed direction of traffic.

Bastos, Miller, and colleagues in 2018 found this laminar division across the frontal and parietal cortex of monkeys performing working-memory tasks, suggesting a general cortical motif rather than a visual-system quirk.

The honest asterisks: the laminar separation is statistical rather than absolute, gamma exists in deep layers and beta in superficial ones at lower strength; and laminar recordings come from a limited set of areas, tasks, and species, mostly monkeys.

19.1.3 Predictions in the slow feedback rhythms, errors in the fast feedforward rhythm

The functional half of the mapping assigns content, not just direction: predictions ride the slow bands down, prediction errors ride gamma up.

Three lines support it.

First, expectation experiments: work by Peter Kok, Floris de Lange, and colleagues in the 2010s found that expected stimuli evoke reduced feedforward responses, consistent with smaller prediction errors, while unexpected stimuli boost gamma-band responses in lower areas, as an error signal should.

Second, the mismatch literature: Yukiko Kikuchi, Christopher Petkov, and colleagues in 2017 reported in monkey auditory cortex that violations of learned sound sequences enhanced gamma in the feedforward direction while predictable structure enhanced beta feedback.

Third, computational fit: in predictive-coding models, error units are proposed to sit in superficial layers and prediction units in deep layers, matching the laminar band signature of the previous sub-section.

The objections are equally concrete.

Gamma in visual cortex is famously strongest for large, high-contrast, regular stimuli, such as gratings, which are among the most predictable stimuli that exist, the opposite of what a pure error signal should prefer; this point has been pressed by Martin Vinck and colleagues, who in 2015 and later proposed nearly the inverse coding for gamma, gamma as a signature of predictable, efficiently encoded input.

The Ray and Maunsell frequency-drift objection from chapter 16 applies again: a band whose frequency moves with contrast is an awkward fixed channel.

And directed-influence statistics themselves inherit the conduction-delay and signal-mixing objections, since estimates of who leads whom in phase can be distorted by delays, common input, and differences in signal-to-noise between areas.

19.2 Beta and the status quo

Within the mapping, the beta band, roughly 13 to 30 cycles per second, has attracted its own functional story, one strong enough to deserve its own section: beta as the signal that the current state of the system should be maintained.

19.2.1 Beta as the signal to maintain the current state

The status-quo hypothesis was articulated by Andreas Engel and Pascal Fries in a 2010 review: beta activity signals that the current sensorimotor or cognitive set should be maintained, and it should therefore rise when maintenance is intended and fall when change is prepared.

The motor system supplies the cleanest evidence.

Beta is prominent over motor cortex at rest, drops sharply just before and during voluntary movement, an effect called desynchronisation, and rebounds after the movement ends.

Steady holding, such as maintaining a constant grip, sustains beta, and beta is abnormally elevated in Parkinson's disease, a movement disorder marked by difficulty initiating change, where excessive beta in deep motor circuits correlates with slowness and rigidity and is reduced by effective treatment, whether medication or deep brain stimulation, the therapy of implanted electrodes delivering current to deep structures.

The Parkinson's link matters for grading across this book because it is one of the few places where a rhythm is tracked, targeted, and reduced with clinical benefit, and adaptive deep brain stimulation triggered by beta bursts reached regulatory approval in the mid 2020s.

Even here, causal purists note that stimulation does many things besides reducing beta, so beta may be an excellent biomarker, a measurable indicator of a state, without being the mechanism of the state.

In predictive-coding terms, the status-quo function and the feedback function rhyme: a prediction is, in a sense, an instruction to keep treating the world as already modelled, and Spitzer and Haegens proposed in a 2017 synthesis that beta serves the endogenous reactivation and maintenance of content, unifying the motor and cognitive stories.

19.2.2 Beta bursts for the reactivation of held content

The sustained-hum picture of beta received a major correction in the 2010s, and it changes how all earlier beta findings must be read.

When examined trial by trial rather than averaged, beta does not hum; it arrives in brief bursts, typically lasting on the order of 50 to 150 milliseconds.

Mikael Lundqvist, Earl Miller, and colleagues showed in Neuron in 2016, recording in monkey prefrontal cortex, the region behind the forehead central to working memory, that working-memory activity itself is bursty: gamma bursts accompany the encoding and read-out of held items, while beta bursts dominate the delay periods in between and give way to gamma at read-out, as if beta held the network in storage mode and gamma reopened the content.

Their 2018 follow-up in Nature Communications tied the timing of beta-burst decreases to the volitional control of when memories are read out.

In parallel, Shin, Law, Tsutsui, Moore, and Jones showed in 2017, in humans, mice, and monkeys, that the rate of pre-stimulus beta bursts over sensory cortex, not average beta power, predicts whether a faint touch will be missed, and Hannah Sherman, Stephanie Jones, and colleagues in 2016 proposed a specific circuit mechanism in which a burst is generated by near-simultaneous input volleys to deep and superficial layers.

The burst view supports the maintenance story in updated form, beta bursts as discrete clamp events rather than a continuous clamp, and in this book's engineering vocabulary the natural reading is batch write-back: content is held, protected from overwriting during beta-dominated intervals, and committed or read out in the gamma windows between bursts.

The both-sides rule again: an explicit memory system with write-enable flags and timestamps needs no such rhythm, and the design question carried to Part Fourteen is whether burst-gated maintenance buys robustness in a system, like the brain or the konnectome, that lacks a global controller to set the flags.

19.3 The grading

The mapping of this chapter is the most architecturally ambitious claim in Part Four, tying band, layer, direction, and message type into one scheme, and ambition raises the evidential bar.

19.3.1 The frequency-to-hierarchy mapping. Grade: CONTESTED

The supporting evidence, at full strength: three independent laboratories, two species, and two recording technologies converge on gamma-up, alpha-beta-down directed influence; the laminar signature matches the anatomy of feedforward and feedback projections; microstimulation reproduces the asymmetry causally in at least one preparation; and the beta maintenance story coheres with the feedback role.

The objections, at full strength: gamma behaves like a marker of strong, often highly predictable local drive, and the Vinck line of work argues its content role may be close to the opposite of prediction error; gamma frequency drifts with stimulus properties, undermining any fixed channel assignment; directed-influence measures are vulnerable to conduction delays, common input, and signal-to-noise asymmetries, one of this book's three recurring objections; the laminar and directional separations are statistical tendencies, not clean channels; and the mapping has been tested mostly in visual and frontoparietal cortex of monkeys and in human vision, so its claimed generality outruns its sampling.

No experiment has yet forced predictions and errors onto swapped bands, or selectively silenced one band in one direction, and shown the predicted specific cognitive failure.

The grade is therefore CONTESTED, without a lean: unlike chapter 16, no alternative account has absorbed the positive evidence, and unlike chapter 18, no perturbation result yet points decisively the right way.

The reader should leave this part of the book with the pattern the four chapters share.

In every case a rhythm correlates beautifully with a function; in every case the three recurring objections - frequency drift with stimulus, phase ambiguity under conduction delays, and spurious coupling from non-sinusoidal waveforms - carve the claim back to its defensible core; and in every case the missing experiment is the same, a specific perturbation of the rhythm with a specific cognitive consequence.

That shared missing experiment is why the grades in this part cluster at CONTESTED, and why the clean COMPUTATION cases elsewhere in this book, the sharp-wave ripple and parvalbumin-driven gamma, matter so much: they show the bar can be met.

CHAPTER 20. SPEECH AND LANGUAGE TRACKING (CORE)

Spoken language arrives as a stream of sound, and yet somewhere between the ear and understanding, that stream must be chopped into pieces - syllables, words, phrases - before meaning can be extracted.

This chapter asks whether the brain uses its own internal rhythms as the chopping mechanism, locking slow brain waves onto the rhythm of speech itself so that each wave cycle scoops up roughly one syllable.

It is one of the most attractive ideas in the entire field, and also one of the most fiercely contested, so it makes an ideal training ground for the kind of careful grading this book practices.

20.1 The proposal

The proposal begins from a striking coincidence of timescales.

Across the world's languages, syllables arrive at roughly four to eight per second, which is exactly the frequency range of the theta rhythm, the brain wave that cycles four to eight times per second.

A brain wave, for the newcomer, is a rhythmic rise and fall in the summed electrical activity of many brain cells, measurable from the scalp with electroencephalography (EEG), a technique that records the brain's electrical activity through sensors placed on the head, or with magnetoencephalography (MEG), a related technique that records the tiny magnetic fields the same currents produce.

The proposal, developed most influentially by Anne-Lise Giraud and David Poeppel in a much cited 2012 paper in Nature Neuroscience, is that this match is no coincidence: the auditory cortex, the hearing part of the brain's outer layer, uses its theta rhythm as a built-in syllable-sized ruler for slicing continuous speech.

20.1.1 Cortex tracking the speech envelope at syllable and phoneme timescales

The first piece of the proposal is an observation that is not itself in dispute.

Speech has an envelope, meaning the slow rise and fall of overall loudness as syllables come and go, like the outline you would draw over the tops of the peaks in a sound recording.

When a person listens to speech, the slow electrical activity of their auditory cortex follows this envelope with remarkable fidelity, a phenomenon called cortical tracking of the speech envelope.

This tracking is strongest at two timescales: a delta-band timescale of roughly one to four cycles per second, matching the pace of words and phrases, and a theta-band timescale of roughly four to eight cycles per second, matching the pace of syllables.

The delta band and theta band are simply names for frequency ranges of brain activity, delta being the slowest at roughly 0.5 to 4 cycles per second and theta the next band up at roughly 4 to 8 cycles per second.

Crucially, the strength of this tracking predicts comprehension: studies such as Peelle, Gross, and Davis in 2013, and many since, found that when speech is degraded or buried in noise, listeners who track the envelope better understand more, and work by Etard and Reichenbach in 2019 showed that theta-band tracking follows the clarity of the sound while delta-band tracking follows whether the listener actually comprehends it.

Faster activity in the gamma band, meaning rhythms above roughly 30 cycles per second, appears to follow the finer grain of speech, down toward the timescale of phonemes, the individual speech sounds like the p and a in pat, which flit past at rates of 30 or more per second.

20.1.2 Theta phase resetting to syllable onsets, then gamma encoding the detail

The second piece of the proposal turns the observation into a machine.

In the Giraud and Poeppel model, sharp acoustic edges in speech, especially the burst of energy at a syllable's onset, reset the phase of the theta rhythm, where phase means the position within the wave's cycle, like the position of a clock hand within one sweep.

Resetting the phase means yanking the wave to a fixed starting position, so that from that moment the theta cycle is aligned with the syllable.

Once aligned, each theta cycle acts as a parsing frame, a temporal container that says this stretch of sound belongs together as one syllable.

Within each theta frame, the model proposes that faster gamma activity encodes the fine detail, sampling the sound at the phoneme timescale, so that a slow rhythm sets the packet boundaries while a fast rhythm writes the contents.

The theta cycle also modulates the excitability of the neurons, the brain's individual signaling cells, so that they are more responsive at one phase of the cycle than another, which would naturally chunk the neural response into syllable-sized bites.

For an engineer, this is a familiar design: a slow framing clock that delimits packets and a fast sampling clock that digitizes the payload inside each packet, which is part of why this proposal has been so attractive.

20.2 The debate

Here the book's honesty rule takes over, because everything just described has a rival explanation that requires no oscillations at all.

The debate is not about whether cortical activity follows speech - it plainly does - but about what kind of machinery does the following, and whether the rhythm is a worker or a passenger.

20.2.1 Genuine entrainment against a train of evoked responses

Entrainment, in the strict sense, means that a genuine self-sustaining internal oscillation - a rhythm the brain generates on its own - adjusts its phase to lock onto an external rhythm, the way two pendulum clocks on the same shelf gradually fall into step.

The rival account says that what looks like entrainment is really just a train of evoked responses: the brain produces a stereotyped burst of activity every time it hears a sharp sound, and a sequence of syllables therefore produces a sequence of bursts that, once overlapped and summed, looks exactly like a wave locked to the speech.

On this account there is no internal oscillator at all, just a row of separate echoes, the way a row of separate splashes from skipping stones can look like a continuous wave on the water.

The two accounts make different predictions, and the tests have not been kind to the strong entrainment story.

A genuine oscillator should keep ringing briefly after the sound stops, and should show a phase lag that stays roughly constant across frequencies, whereas a chain of evoked responses should show a phase lag that grows linearly with frequency, the signature of a fixed time delay.

Work by Zou and colleagues in 2021 found that theta-band tracking of the speech envelope shows precisely the linear phase property expected from evoked responses, and a pointed 2023 study by Oganian and colleagues in the Journal of Neuroscience concluded from magnetoencephalography that phase alignment to the envelope reflects evoked responses to acoustic edges, not oscillatory entrainment, because slowed-down speech produced transient responses at the edges rather than a continuously locked rhythm.

Defenders of entrainment reply that some findings do fit an oscillator, including sustained rhythmic aftereffects in some paradigms, perceptual rhythms that continue after a rhythmic sound ends, and the fact that speech comprehension degrades sharply when speech is artificially compressed beyond roughly nine syllables per second, as if a parser with a maximum frame rate had been outrun, an observation going back to work by Ghitza and Greenberg in 2009.

The methodological literature now often uses the deliberately neutral phrase speech tracking instead of entrainment, a terminological retreat that is itself evidence of how live the dispute remains.

20.2.2 Tracking of abstract structure beyond the acoustics

The strongest card held by the tracking side is that the brain follows structure that is not in the sound at all.

In a landmark 2016 study in Nature Neuroscience, Nai Ding, Lucia Melloni, Hang Zhang, Xing Tian, and David Poeppel played listeners sequences of monosyllabic words spoken at a perfectly regular rate, constructed so that pairs of words formed phrases and pairs of phrases formed sentences, with no acoustic cue whatsoever marking the phrase or sentence boundaries.

Magnetoencephalography revealed cortical rhythms at the syllable rate, at the phrase rate, and at the sentence rate simultaneously, and the phrase-rate and sentence-rate responses appeared only in listeners who knew the language, so they could not be echoes of the acoustics.

This showed that the cortex tracks internally constructed linguistic units, a hierarchy built by the listener's own grammar, not just loudness contours.

The finding has replicated and extended, including to language learning, but its interpretation is itself debated: critics such as Frank and Yang in 2018 showed that some of the low-frequency structure could arise from the statistics of the words themselves rather than from true grammatical parsing, so even this elegant result sits inside the controversy rather than above it.

20.3 The grading

We now apply the book's discipline: is this rhythm a computation the brain performs, a substrate by-product like engine noise, or a contested case where real evidence is undermined by a named objection?

20.3.1 Oscillatory speech parsing. Grade: CONTESTED, leaning COMPUTATION

The supporting evidence is genuinely strong: envelope tracking is robust, it predicts comprehension, syllable rates worldwide sit in the theta band, comprehension collapses when speech outruns the theta frame rate, and the brain tracks abstract linguistic structure that exists only in the listener's head.

The named objection is equally concrete: the evoked-response account, sharpened by the linear-phase findings of Zou and colleagues in 2021 and the acoustic-edge findings of Oganian and colleagues in 2023, can reproduce most of the tracking data without any internal oscillator, which means the central mechanism of the model has not been isolated.

There is also no clean causal experiment of the kind that earns the COMPUTATION grade elsewhere in this book, because selectively silencing the theta rhythm in a human auditory cortex without touching everything else is not yet possible, though transcranial alternating current stimulation studies that nudge theta phase and shift speech perception, such as work by Riecke and colleagues in 2018 and Zoefel and colleagues in 2018, point in the entrainment direction while carrying that method's own weak-field and peripheral-nerve caveats.

The grade is therefore CONTESTED, leaning COMPUTATION: the parsing story is the best available explanation for the whole pattern of evidence, especially the tracking of abstract structure and the frame-rate limit on comprehension, but an honest scorer must record that the passive echo account has not been eliminated.

In plain words, the brain certainly rides the rhythm of speech; whether it supplies a rhythm of its own to do the parsing is exactly what the field is still fighting about.



================================================================================

PART EIGHT. THE RHYTHMS OF MEMORY

================================================================================

CHAPTER 21. THETA AND THE CODING OF SEQUENCE (CORE)

We turn now to memory, and to the single most beautiful phenomenon in this entire book: a case where the timing of a brain cell's firing, relative to an ongoing wave, demonstrably carries information that its firing rate alone does not.

The setting is the hippocampus, a seahorse-shaped structure deep in the temporal lobe on each side of the brain, essential for forming new memories of events and for knowing where you are in space.

During movement and exploration, the hippocampus of a rat is dominated by a large, regular theta rhythm of roughly 6 to 10 cycles per second, and against that metronome the hippocampus writes something remarkable.

21.1 Phase coding and phase precession

A phase code is the idea that a neuron can signal information not just by how often it fires but by when within a wave cycle it fires, the way the meaning of a drumbeat could depend on where it lands within a bar of music.

For decades this was an elegant theory in search of a clean example; phase precession is that example, and it is the book's strongest single case for a phase code, so we will walk through it slowly.

21.1.1 Place-cell spikes advancing in theta phase across a field

A place cell is a neuron in the hippocampus that fires only when the animal is in one particular patch of its environment, called that cell's place field, a discovery made by John O'Keefe in 1971 that earned him a share of the 2014 Nobel Prize.

A spike, for the newcomer, is the brief electrical pulse a neuron fires to signal to other neurons.

In 1993, John O'Keefe and Michael Recce reported in the journal Hippocampus a discovery about the timing of those spikes: as a rat runs through a cell's place field, the cell's spikes arrive at progressively earlier phases of each successive theta cycle.

When the rat enters the field, the cell fires late in the theta cycle; by the middle of the field it fires near the middle of the cycle; by the exit it fires early in the cycle, the total advance spanning up to roughly 360 degrees, a full cycle, across the field.

The phenomenon is called phase precession, precession meaning a steady forward creep, and its crucial property is this: the phase of firing tracks the animal's position within the field more precisely than the firing rate does.

Two visits at different running speeds produce different spike counts but the same orderly phase-versus-position relationship, so a downstream listener that read only rate would know less than one that read phase, which is the operational definition of a phase code.

Phase precession has since been found in the rat entorhinal cortex, the hippocampus's main gateway, by Hafting, Fyhn, Bonnevie, Moser, and Moser in 2008, in the ventral striatum, in bats, and, in a 2021 study in the journal Cell by Qasim, Fried, and Jacobs using recordings from neurosurgical patients, in the human hippocampus and entorhinal cortex as well.

The bat result, from Eliav and colleagues in the Ulanovsky laboratory in 2018, is philosophically important: bat hippocampal field potentials lack a continuous theta oscillation, yet the cells still show phase coding relative to irregular, nonrhythmic fluctuations, which shows that the phase code is more fundamental than the clean sinusoidal clock it was discovered against.

21.1.2 The compression of behavioural sequences into a plasticity window

Now consider not one place cell but a chain of them whose fields tile a route: field A, then B, then C.

At the pace of behavior, the rat crosses these fields over several seconds, but because each cell precesses, within any single theta cycle of roughly 125 milliseconds the cells fire in the order A then B then C, separated by tens of milliseconds - a miniature, fast-forward replay of the journey inside every wave beat.

This is called theta sequence compression, and its significance was spelled out by William Skaggs, Bruce McNaughton, Matthew Wilson, and Carol Barnes in an influential 1996 paper: the compression squeezes a behavioral sequence lasting seconds down into the tens-of-milliseconds window where synapses can actually learn.

A synapse is the junction where one neuron passes signals to the next, and its strength can grow when the sending cell fires just before the receiving cell, a rule called spike-timing-dependent plasticity whose window is only a few tens of milliseconds wide.

Events separated by seconds in the world are invisible to that rule, but the same events compressed into a theta cycle land squarely inside it, so phase precession looks like a purpose-built adapter between the slow timescale of experience and the fast timescale of synaptic learning, letting the brain wire up the order of events, A before B before C, and not merely the events themselves.

Later work showed these theta sequences can also sweep ahead of the animal toward goals, as in Wikenheiser and Redish in 2015, suggesting the same compression machinery serves planning and imagination, not only recording.

21.1.3 The grading: phase precession as the strongest single case for a phase code. Grade: COMPUTATION for the phenomenon, CONTESTED for its downstream use

This grade has two halves, and the split is the whole point.

For the phenomenon itself, the grade is COMPUTATION: phase precession is robust, replicated across species including humans, measurable on single passes through a field as shown by Schmidt, Kempter, and colleagues in 2009, and it demonstrably carries information about position and sequence that firing rate does not, so the claim that phase carries a code here is as solid as anything in systems neuroscience.

It even survives the loss of the clean oscillation, as the bat work of Eliav and colleagues in 2018 showed, which answers the usual objection that phase codes are artifacts of pretty sinusoids.

For the downstream use, the grade is CONTESTED, and the objection must be stated plainly: no experiment has yet shown that any downstream brain region actually reads the phase code, or that erasing precession while sparing everything else impairs sequence learning.

The compression-into-the-plasticity-window story is a hypothesis about function, and it has rivals: precession might instead be a signature of memory retrieval or of internal sweep dynamics, a by-product of how place fields are generated rather than a message intended for a reader, and reviews such as Jaramillo and Kempter in 2017 explicitly list enabling sequence learning, reflecting retrieval, and neither as open possibilities.

Encouraging correlational evidence exists - Zheng, Jacobs, and colleagues reported in Nature Human Behaviour in 2024 that the strength of human phase precession during encoding of film clips predicted later memory success, beyond what firing rates predicted - but correlation with memory is not proof of a consumed code.

So the honest summary is: the brain writes in phase here, beyond reasonable doubt; whether anything reads it is still open.

21.2 Theta and encoding against retrieval

The same theta cycle may solve a second, quite different problem: keeping the act of storing a new memory from colliding with the act of recalling an old one.

Writing and reading into the same memory network at the same instant risks corruption, because retrieval activity could be mistaken for new experience and stored again, so the proposal is that the hippocampus time-shares, devoting one phase of each theta cycle to writing and the opposite phase to reading.

21.2.1 The separate-phases model and the acetylcholine gate

The model was developed by Michael Hasselmo, Clara Bodelon, and Bradley Wyble in a 2002 paper in Neural Computation.

It exploits an anatomical fact: the hippocampal area called CA1 receives two competing inputs, one from the entorhinal cortex carrying fresh sensory information about the present, and one from area CA3, the hippocampus's internal association network, carrying retrieved patterns from the past.

These two inputs arrive preferentially at opposite phases of theta, so the model assigns encoding of new input to the theta peak, when entorhinal drive is strongest and synapses are most ready to strengthen, and retrieval from CA3 to the theta trough, when the internal input dominates.

Layered on top of this fast within-cycle switch is a slow chemical gate: acetylcholine, a neuromodulator, meaning a chemical that broadly adjusts how circuits behave, is released when the world is novel and attention is high, and it selectively suppresses the internal CA3 pathway while sparing the external entorhinal one, tilting the whole system toward encoding, as Hasselmo argued in earlier modeling from the 1990s onward.

The model has earned real experimental support: Douchamps, Jeewajee, Blundell, Burgess, and Lever showed in 2013 in the Journal of Neuroscience that in rats exploring novelty, place-cell firing shifts toward the encoding phase, and that scopolamine, a drug that blocks acetylcholine receptors, pushes firing toward the retrieval phase, exactly as predicted.

More strikingly, Siegle and Wilson in 2014 used optogenetics, a technique that switches genetically tagged neurons on or off with light, to inhibit hippocampal cells at specific theta phases in behaving mice, and found that inhibition timed to one phase preferentially improved encoding while inhibition at the opposite phase improved retrieval - a genuinely causal, phase-specific manipulation, which is rare and precious in this literature.

21.2.2 The human evidence for competing memories at distinct phases

Does the human brain also timetable memory operations within its theta cycle?

Direct evidence has begun to arrive from electroencephalography and from electrodes implanted in patients for epilepsy surgery.

Kerren, Linde-Domingo, Hanslmayr, and Wimber showed in Current Biology in 2018 that when people recall learned word-picture associations, the neural reactivation of the target memory arrives clocked to a preferred phase of a roughly 7 to 8 cycle-per-second theta rhythm, with phase consistency appearing about 300 milliseconds before the moment of maximal reactivation.

The sharper result came in 2022, when Kerren, van Bree, Griffiths, and Wimber reported in eLife that when two memories compete - an old association and a newer one sharing the same cue, the classic setup for interference - the two memories are reactivated at distinct phases of the human hippocampal theta rhythm, with the currently relevant memory and its rival separated in phase rather than in time-averaged strength.

That is precisely what a time-sharing scheduler would look like from the outside: rivals for the same wire assigned different slots within the cycle.

The caution to record is that these are correlational decoding studies in small clinical populations, phase estimation in humans faces the alignment and non-sinusoidal waveform problems described in Chapter 23, and a causal phase-specific manipulation like Siegle and Wilson's has no human counterpart yet, so the human half of this story is promising rather than settled.

No Grade line is stated in the outline for this section, and we resist the temptation to award one informally; the rat causal work is strong, the human work is young.


CHAPTER 22. THE THETA-GAMMA NEURAL CODE AND WORKING MEMORY (CORE)

Hold a phone number in mind for the ten seconds between hearing it and dialing it, and you are using working memory, the mind's scratchpad for information held briefly and actively.

Its most famous property is its tiny capacity - a handful of items, classically summarized by George Miller in 1956 as the magical number seven plus or minus two, and revised downward by Nelson Cowan in 2001 to about four for genuinely independent chunks.

This chapter presents a bold proposal that explains both the holding and the limit with a single piece of rhythmic machinery, and then a modern debate that questions the very foundation the proposal stands on.

22.1 The nested code

The proposal builds on a physiological observation from Chapter 23's territory: in the hippocampus and elsewhere, fast gamma waves of roughly 30 to 100 cycles per second do not run free but ride on the slower theta wave, several gamma cycles nesting inside each theta cycle, a coupling first described in the rat hippocampus by Anna Bragin, Gyorgy Buzsaki, and colleagues in 1995.

The nested code turns that nesting into a data structure.

22.1.1 One item per gamma cycle within a theta cycle

In 1995, John Lisman and Marco Idiart proposed in the journal Science that each item held in working memory is represented by the group of neurons that fire together within one gamma cycle, and that the several items currently in mind are activated one after another, each in its own gamma cycle, at successive phases of each theta cycle.

Picture a slowly rotating carousel with a fixed number of seats: the theta cycle is one full rotation, each gamma cycle is a seat, and each remembered item sits in its own seat, replayed in order once per rotation, roughly seven rotations per second.

The scheme elegantly explains how multiple items can share one network without blending into each other - they are separated in time within the cycle, not in space - and it automatically preserves their order, since each item keeps its serial position as a phase position.

An engineer will recognize this instantly as time-division multiplexing, the technique by which many telephone conversations share one wire by taking strict turns in numbered time slots; the theta cycle is the frame and the gamma cycles are the slots, and this analogy is developed as a design pattern in Part Fourteen.

Supportive evidence includes the finding by Heusser, Poeppel, Ezzyat, and Davachi in Nature Neuroscience in 2016 that human memory for the order of items depends on distinct items being coupled to distinct theta phases, and single-unit work in patients by Bahramisharif, Jensen, Jacobs, and Lisman in 2018 showing letter-selective cortical sites activating serially at different theta-alpha phases during maintenance.

22.1.2 The capacity limit as the gamma-per-theta ratio, a mechanism for the magic number

The model's boldest stroke is that it derives the capacity limit from arithmetic.

If each item needs one gamma cycle and all items must fit within one theta cycle, then capacity equals the number of gamma cycles per theta cycle: with theta at about 7 cycles per second, a cycle lasts about 140 milliseconds, and with gamma at about 40 cycles per second, each gamma cycle lasts 25 milliseconds, so roughly five to seven items fit - landing exactly in the Miller range with no free parameters.

The theory therefore predicts that individuals with slower theta or faster gamma should hold more, and Kaminski, Brzezicka, and Wrobel reported in 2011 a positive correlation between individual theta-to-gamma cycle-length ratios and short-term memory capacity, while Wolinski, Cooper, Sauseng, and Romei in 2018 used transcranial alternating current stimulation to impose slower 4-cycle-per-second theta on the parietal cortex and found improved visual working memory capacity relative to faster 7-cycle-per-second stimulation, a causal nudge in the predicted direction.

Honesty requires the ledger's other column: a 2021 replication attempt by Pavlov and Kotchoubey, pointedly titled with a question mark, failed to reproduce the correlation between theta-gamma ratio and capacity in a larger sample, and the transcranial stimulation work carries the standing objections, detailed elsewhere in this book, that its scalp-applied fields are weak by the time they reach cortex and that peripheral nerve stimulation is an unexcluded confound.

22.2 The maintenance debate

Beneath the slot-code proposal lies a more basic question that the field genuinely does not agree on: what does the brain physically do during the silent seconds while you hold that phone number?

This must be taught as a live controversy, because it is one - three serious answers coexist in the current literature, each with strong evidence and strong advocates.

22.2.1 Persistent activity against activity-silent against bursty working memory

The classical answer is persistent activity: neurons in the prefrontal cortex, the front part of the brain's outer layer associated with planning and control, fire continuously throughout the delay, actively holding the item the way you keep a beach ball aloft by constant tapping.

This picture rests on genuinely landmark recordings - Fuster and Alexander in 1971 and Goldman-Rakic's laboratory through the 1980s, notably Funahashi, Bruce, and Goldman-Rakic in 1989, found individual monkey prefrontal neurons firing steadily through memory delays, tuned to the remembered location - and modern defenders, prominently Christos Constantinidis and colleagues in a 2018 position paper, argue it remains the core mechanism.

The second answer is activity-silent working memory, articulated by Mark Stokes in an influential 2015 paper in Trends in Cognitive Sciences: the item is held not in ongoing spikes but in short-lived changes in synaptic strengths, invisible in the firing record, the way a foam mattress silently holds the imprint of a hand.

A specific physical mechanism exists - synaptic facilitation lasting hundreds of milliseconds to seconds, modeled as a working memory store by Mongillo, Barak, and Tsodyks in 2008 - and striking human evidence came from Rose, Postle, and colleagues in Science in 2016, who used a pulse of transcranial magnetic stimulation, a magnetic pulse to the scalp that briefly stirs the cortex below, to ping the network during a delay and saw an item that had vanished from the decodable neural signal reappear, as if the pulse had read out a hidden synaptic trace.

The third answer, from Mikael Lundqvist, Earl Miller, and colleagues in Neuron in 2016 based on multi-electrode recordings in monkey prefrontal cortex, is bursty working memory: on any single trial, neurons do not fire persistently but in brief, sparse bursts accompanied by transient gamma oscillations, with beta oscillations, rhythms of roughly 15 to 30 cycles per second, dominating between bursts and acting as a hold-and-suppress control signal, and with the synaptic mechanism of the activity-silent account bridging the gaps between bursts.

In the compromise picture that Miller, Lundqvist, and Bastos sketched in their 2018 Working Memory 2.0 review, spiking bursts periodically refresh a synaptic trace, so the memory is neither purely active nor purely silent but a rhythm of the two, and some in the field read the theta-gamma slot code of this chapter as the natural timetable for those refreshes.

The debate remains genuinely open: defenders of persistence point to robust delay activity in many tasks and note that some information really does stay decodable throughout; skeptics reply that attended items can be held with no decodable activity at all; and the sides still dispute how much of the disagreement is about brain areas, task demands, or analysis choices.

22.2.2 The trial-averaging illusion of sustained firing

Why did half a century of recordings show smooth persistent firing if single trials contain sparse bursts?

The answer is an averaging artifact, and it is worth understanding because it is a warning that applies to the whole field.

Neuroscientists traditionally averaged a neuron's response over many repetitions of a task to wash out noise, but averaging destroys timing: if a neuron emits brief bursts at unpredictable moments that differ from trial to trial, the average across trials is a smooth, sustained hump, even though no single trial ever contained sustained firing.

It is like photographing a firefly meadow with a long exposure: the plate shows steady glow everywhere, though at no instant was the meadow steadily lit.

Lundqvist, Miller, and colleagues made exactly this point quantitatively in their 2016 paper and their 2018 follow-up in the Journal of Neuroscience, showing that prefrontal delay activity that appears persistent in trial averages resolves into discrete gamma-associated bursts on single trials.

The persistent-activity camp responds that burstiness in single trials does not prove the average is meaningless - some neurons do fire with high continuity, and populations can maintain a stable code even while individual members flicker - so even the illusion claim is itself contested, which is the honest note to end on.

The methodological moral stands regardless: claims about sustained neural processes must be checked on single trials, because averaging can manufacture persistence out of intermittency.

22.3 The grading

With both the elegant proposal and the unsettled foundation on the table, we can grade the theta-gamma working memory code.

22.3.1 The theta-gamma slot code. Grade: CONTESTED, with a strong engineering analogue carried to Part Fourteen

The supporting evidence: theta-gamma nesting is a real and robust physiological phenomenon; the capacity arithmetic lands on the psychological magic number with no tuning; item-to-phase separation has been observed in human recordings by Heusser and colleagues in 2016 and Bahramisharif and colleagues in 2018; slowing theta by stimulation nudged capacity in the predicted direction in Wolinski and colleagues in 2018.

The objections, each named: the correlation between theta-gamma ratio and capacity failed a direct replication attempt by Pavlov and Kotchoubey in 2021; the stimulation evidence inherits the weak-field and peripheral-nerve confounds of transcranial alternating current stimulation; phase-amplitude coupling measurements that support nesting are vulnerable to the non-sinusoidal waveform artifact detailed in Chapter 23; and the entire maintenance debate of section 22.2 means the field has not even settled what is being maintained, let alone whether it is maintained in gamma slots.

No experiment has selectively deleted the gamma slotting while sparing everything else and watched capacity change, so the model has never faced the decisive causal test that would earn COMPUTATION.

The grade is therefore CONTESTED: a beautiful, quantitative, repeatedly suggestive theory that has survived thirty years without being either confirmed or killed.

Yet the design idea is too good to discard, which is why the outline carries it forward: whatever the hippocampus turns out to do, time-division multiplexing - a slow frame clock whose fast sub-slots hold ordered items - is a proven engineering solution to exactly the problem working memory solves, and Part Fourteen builds the konnectome analogue explicitly, while also applying the design test of asking where an explicit indexed buffer with timestamps and a scheduler would beat the wave-like version outright.

CHAPTER 23. CROSS-FREQUENCY COUPLING AND MULTIPLEXING (CORE)

The last two chapters kept meeting the same structural idea from different directions: a slow rhythm and a fast rhythm working together, the slow one framing and the fast one filling.

This chapter names that idea, generalizes it, and then turns on it the harshest methodological spotlight in the book, because cross-frequency coupling is simultaneously one of the most cited and one of the most artifact-prone concepts in modern neuroscience.

23.1 Coupling as a computation

Cross-frequency coupling means any systematic relationship between brain rhythms of different speeds.

The variety that dominates the literature is phase-amplitude coupling, in which the phase of a slow rhythm - where the slow wave is in its cycle - controls the amplitude, meaning the momentary strength, of a fast rhythm, so that fast activity bursts at one particular part of each slow cycle and falls quiet elsewhere.

The signature empirical paper is by Ryan Canolty and colleagues in Science in 2006, who recorded electrocorticography, meaning electrodes placed directly on the brain surface of neurosurgical patients, and found that the power of high gamma activity at 80 to 150 cycles per second was locked to the phase of the theta rhythm at 4 to 8 cycles per second across the human neocortex, with the coupling pattern shifting from task to task.

23.1.1 The slow rhythm parsing, the fast rhythm encoding

The proposed computation generalizes the speech story of Chapter 20 and the memory story of Chapter 22 into a single principle, articulated in reviews by Canolty and Knight in 2010 and by Ole Jensen and colleagues: slow rhythms parse and fast rhythms encode.

Slow rhythms are the natural carriers of large-scale coordination, because their long cycles span the conduction delays between distant brain regions and because they track slow structure in the world, like syllables, footsteps, and sniffs.

Fast rhythms are the natural carriers of local detail, because gamma-speed activity reflects tight interactions among nearby neurons and can change from millisecond to millisecond.

Phase-amplitude coupling wires the two together: the slow phase acts as a frame marker saying now, in this window, and the fast burst inside the window carries the content, exactly as a postal system separates the envelope's delivery schedule from the letter inside.

In engineering language this is multiplexing, sending multiple streams over shared hardware by giving each stream its own slot or channel; a related laboratory example is the rodent olfactory system, where each sniff cycle frames a fresh gamma-borne odor analysis, and the hippocampal theta-gamma nesting of Bragin and colleagues in 1995 remains the anatomically best understood case.

Schroeder and Lakatos added in 2009 the active sensing twist: the brain can align its slow rhythms to predictable events in the world, so that the high-excitability phase of the cycle arrives exactly when the important input does, making coupling a tool of attention as well as of packaging.

23.1.2 Gamma frequency routing information by the low-frequency phase

A further refinement proposes that the slow phase does not merely gate one fast stream but selects among several.

Recordings in rat hippocampal area CA1 by Colgin, Moser, and colleagues in Nature in 2009 found two distinct gamma rhythms at distinct theta phases: slow gamma at roughly 25 to 55 cycles per second, coherent with area CA3, the internal memory store, and fast gamma at roughly 65 to 140 cycles per second, coherent with the entorhinal cortex, the gateway for fresh sensory input.

Because the two gammas occupy different theta phases and different frequency lanes, the theta cycle in effect routes the conversation, connecting CA1 to its memory on one part of the cycle and to the outside world on another - a rhythmic switchboard, and a natural physiological cousin of the encoding-versus-retrieval scheduling of Chapter 21.

The idea connects to the broader communication-through-coherence framework of Pascal Fries, treated in its own chapter, in which matching rhythms open channels between regions and mismatched rhythms close them.

The caution, even before the statistics arrive: later large-scale analyses, including work from the Buzsaki laboratory by Schomburg and colleagues in 2014, showed that the slow and fast gamma sub-bands overlap, vary along the anatomy, and are less cleanly separable than the switchboard cartoon suggests, and the routing interpretation leans on coherence measures that face the same conduction-delay and volume-conduction alignment problems that haunt all cross-regional phase claims.

23.2 The Achilles heel

Everything above depends on measuring, from a wiggly voltage trace, that the phase of one rhythm controls the amplitude of another, and this measurement turns out to be treacherously easy to get wrong.

This section is the book's most concentrated dose of methodological skepticism, and it exists because the field's own best practitioners wrote the warnings.

23.2.1 The statistical pitfalls that inflate the evidence

The central pitfall is the non-sinusoidal waveform artifact, the third of the book's three recurring objections.

The analysis pipeline for phase-amplitude coupling assumes, usually silently, that the slow rhythm is a smooth sinusoid, because the mathematical tool that extracts phase, the filter followed by the Hilbert transform, is only cleanly interpretable for sinusoid-like signals.

Real brain rhythms are not sinusoids: the rodent theta wave is famously sawtooth-shaped, and the human motor cortex mu rhythm is arch-shaped, as Scott Cole and Bradley Voytek documented in their 2017 review on the importance of waveform shape.

A sharp-edged, asymmetric wave, when passed through filters, mathematically spawns harmonics, meaning spurious faster components locked by construction to particular phases of the slow wave, so the analysis reports phase-amplitude coupling even though only one rhythm, oddly shaped, actually exists.

Demonstrations that this generates convincing fake coupling and even fake directionality were published by Kramer, Tort, and Kopell in 2008, by Gerber, Sadeh, and colleagues in 2016, and by Lozano-Soldevilla, ter Huurne, and Oostenveld in 2016, and the definitive checklist of this and further pitfalls - sharp transients, nonstationarity, too little data, edge effects from filtering, and inappropriate surrogate statistics - was assembled by Juhan Aru, Jaan Aru, Viola Priesemann, Michael Wibral, and colleagues in a widely cited 2015 paper in Current Opinion in Neurobiology titled Untangling cross-frequency coupling in neuroscience.

A second pitfall is that any repeated sharp event, such as a train of evoked responses - the same rival we met in the speech chapter - produces broadband power locked to the event rhythm, which a coupling analysis dutifully reports as phase-amplitude coupling.

None of this proves that cross-frequency coupling is unreal; hippocampal theta-gamma nesting, for instance, survives inspection of raw traces, where genuinely distinct gamma wiggles ride visibly on the theta wave.

It proves something narrower and more corrosive: a positive coupling statistic, by itself, is weak evidence, and every claimed instance must be re-examined with waveform-aware methods - such as the cycle-by-cycle analysis introduced by Cole and Voytek in 2019 - before it can be believed.

23.2.2 The grading: cross-frequency coupling as a mechanism. Grade: CONTESTED

The supporting evidence: phase-amplitude coupling has been reported across species, brain regions, and tasks; the hippocampal theta-gamma case rests on directly visible nested rhythms with known circuitry from Bragin and colleagues in 1995 and phase-segregated gamma inputs from Colgin and colleagues in 2009; coupling strength changes with task demands and predicts performance in studies from Canolty and colleagues in 2006 onward; and the framework unifies speech parsing, working memory slots, and attentional sampling under one principle, which is genuine theoretical power.

The named objection: non-sinusoidal wave shapes create spurious cross-frequency coupling, per Aru and colleagues in 2015 and Cole and Voytek in 2017, and an unquantified fraction of the published coupling literature predates the checklists and has never been re-audited, so the evidence base is systematically inflated by an unknown amount.

Beyond measurement, the mechanism claim faces the deeper gap that recurs throughout this book: even where coupling is real, showing that the brain computes with it - that the slow phase causally routes or packages information and that downstream circuits read the packaging - requires phase-specific causal manipulation, and outside a few animal paradigms such experiments barely exist.

The grade is therefore CONTESTED: real physiology in the best cases, inflated statistics in the average case, and a mechanism story that remains a well-motivated hypothesis rather than a demonstrated computation.

For the konnectome build, the translation is nonetheless carried forward with both hands: multiplexing by nested clocks is sound engineering with a proven biological inspiration, and the design test asks, for every use, whether an explicit mechanism - a timestamped message queue, a scheduler with numbered slots, a routing table - does the job more transparently than a phase relationship, which in software it usually does; the wave-like version earns its keep only where global lightweight coordination without central bookkeeping is the requirement.


================================================================================

PART NINE. TRAVELING WAVES AND SPATIAL ORGANISATION

================================================================================

CHAPTER 24. TRAVELING WAVES (CORE)

For most of this book we have talked about brain rhythms as if they beat in place, like a metronome ticking on a shelf.

This chapter corrects that picture.

Many brain rhythms do not stand still: they sweep across the surface of the brain the way a stadium wave sweeps around a crowd, with each section of fans standing a moment after their neighbours.

A neuron, to remind the newcomer, is a brain cell that sends brief electrical pulses called spikes, and it talks to other neurons across tiny junctions called synapses.

When millions of neurons wax and wane together, their summed electrical activity forms the rhythms that electroencephalography (EEG), the recording of the brain's electrical activity through sensors on the scalp, has measured since the 1920s.

The discovery that these rhythms travel, rather than merely pulse, has changed how scientists think about what they might be for.

24.1 The phenomenology

Phenomenology is simply the careful description of what is observed, before any theory about what it means.

Here we describe what a traveling brain wave looks like in a recording, how fast it moves, and which of the familiar rhythms turn out to travel.

24.1.1 Phase gradients revealing propagation rather than standing synchrony

Imagine two clocks ticking at the same speed but with one always a fraction of a second behind the other.

The rhythm is shared, but the timing is offset, and that offset is called a phase difference - phase being the position within one cycle of a repeating wave, like the position of a clock hand within one sweep of the dial.

If you place a grid of electrodes, which are small electrical sensors, on the brain and see the same rhythm at every electrode but with the phase shifting smoothly and systematically from one side of the grid to the other, you are looking at a phase gradient.

A phase gradient is the fingerprint of a wave that is moving: the peak of the oscillation arrives at each location a little later than at the location before it, exactly as the crest of an ocean wave arrives at each swimmer down the beach in turn.

For decades, researchers who averaged their recordings across electrodes treated these offsets as noise and unintentionally painted the picture of a standing, zero-lag synchrony, meaning everyone oscillating in perfect lockstep.

Careful reanalysis showed that much of what looked like standing synchrony was propagation in disguise.

Honghui Zhang, Andrew Watrous, Ansh Patel, and Joshua Jacobs demonstrated this decisively in 2018, in the journal Neuron, using recordings taken directly from the brains of neurosurgical patients performing a memory task.

They found contiguous patches of cortex - cortex being the folded outer sheet of the brain where most higher processing happens - oscillating together at frequencies between 2 and 15 cycles per second, written as hertz (Hz), with clear spatial phase gradients marking them as traveling waves.

Crucially, the direction and consistency of the waves' travel correlated with how well the patients performed the memory task, which argues that the traveling is not an idle by-product.

24.1.2 The scales and speeds, and theta and alpha as traveling waves

Traveling waves appear at several spatial scales, from waves crossing a few millimetres of cortex within one brain area to waves sweeping across whole lobes.

The mesoscopic waves, meaning those within a single area, typically move at roughly 0.1 to 0.8 metres per second, which matches the conduction speed of the thin unmyelinated horizontal fibres that link neighbouring patches of cortex - myelin being the fatty insulation that speeds signals along a neuron's cable-like axon.

The large-scale waves seen across lobes move faster, often 1 to 10 metres per second, matching the myelinated long-range fibres.

The Zhang and Jacobs recordings put human cortical theta and alpha traveling waves at about 0.25 to 0.75 metres per second.

Two of the book's most familiar rhythms turn out to travel.

Theta, the roughly 4 to 8 Hz rhythm central to memory and navigation, was shown by Evgueniy Lubenov and Athanassios Siapas in 2009, in Nature, to sweep along the length of the rat hippocampus - the hippocampus being the seahorse-shaped structure deep in the temporal lobe that is essential for forming new memories.

Their recordings showed the theta wave propagating along the hippocampus's long axis, so that neurons at different positions experience systematically different theta phases at any instant, spanning in total more than half a cycle.

Honghui Zhang and Joshua Jacobs confirmed in 2015, in the Journal of Neuroscience, that theta travels the same way along the human hippocampus.

Alpha, the roughly 8 to 12 Hz rhythm that dominates the resting EEG at the back of the head, also travels: in humans it most often sweeps between the back and the front of the cortex, and studies since 2018 report that the dominant direction shifts with the brain's state and the task, for example tending front-to-back in quiet rest and back-to-front when a stimulus arrives or during memory encoding.

Even the slow oscillation of deep sleep travels: Marcello Massimini and colleagues showed in 2004 that each slow wave of human sleep typically originates toward the front of the brain and sweeps backward across the cortex at a few metres per second, like a slow tide crossing the whole scalp once or twice a second.

24.2 The proposed computations

Description alone does not tell us what a traveling wave is for.

This section lays out the leading proposals, drawn especially from the influential 2018 review by Lyle Muller, Frederic Chavane, John Reynolds, and Terrence Sejnowski in Nature Reviews Neuroscience, titled Cortical travelling waves: mechanisms and computational principles.

24.2.1 Organising spike timing across space

A rhythm that stands still can only tell every neuron the same time.

A rhythm that travels hands each location a slightly different time, and that turns space into a timing map.

As a wave passes, it transiently raises and lowers the excitability of the neurons under it, meaning how ready they are to fire, so neurons along the wave's path are nudged to spike in a spatial order.

In the hippocampus, the Lubenov and Siapas result means that place cells - neurons that fire when an animal occupies a particular location - at different positions along the structure are held at different theta phases, which could let the hippocampus write out information as an ordered spatial-temporal pattern rather than a single simultaneous chord.

In engineering terms, and for the konnectome build, this is close to a clock distribution network with deliberate skew: a clock signal is the shared timing pulse that steps a digital circuit forward, and skew is the designed difference in when that pulse reaches different parts of the chip, which engineers sometimes exploit to sequence operations.

The honest other side, per this book's design test, is that a digital system can achieve the same ordered hand-off with explicit timestamps or a Lamport logical clock, which is a simple counter scheme invented by Leslie Lamport in 1978 that orders events without any physical wave at all, and the explicit mechanism is exact where the wave is approximate.

24.2.2 Scanning, buffering the recent past, and resetting phase

Three further proposals deserve their own telling.

First, scanning: a wave that sweeps across a sensory map acts like a searchlight passing over a dark field, transiently boosting one part of the map after another, so the brain could sample its inputs in sequence rather than all at once.

Work associating alpha traveling waves with shifts of attention, and perceptual echo studies by Rufin VanRullen and colleagues showing an approximately 10 Hz wave that replays a visual stimulus across the cortex, fit this scanning picture.

Second, buffering the recent past: because a wave carries the imprint of an input away from its entry point, the spatial extent of the wave holds a short history of what just happened, like ripples on a pond still recording where the stone fell seconds ago.

A 2023 computational study by Anderson Keller and colleagues, titled Traveling Waves Encode the Recent Past and Enhance Sequence Learning, showed that artificial neural networks equipped with wave-like dynamics store recent inputs as a spatial pattern and learn sequences better, which is a rare case of the wave idea earning its keep inside working machine-learning code.

In engineering vocabulary this is a ring buffer implemented in space: a ring buffer is a fixed-size memory that continuously overwrites its oldest entry, and a traveling wave does the same by letting old activity decay at the far edge as new activity enters.

The both-sides rule again: an explicit ring buffer with a write pointer is cheaper, lossless, and easier to read out than a decaying wave, so the wave version is only attractive where hardware pointers are unavailable, which is precisely the brain's situation.

Third, resetting phase: a sudden sensory event can restart the wave from a known starting line, called a phase reset, which stamps a shared time zero across a whole region, much as a starter's pistol resets every runner's internal count.

24.3 The grading

This book grades every proposed rhythm function on causal evidence: COMPUTATION when perturbing the rhythm specifically changes the cognitive outcome, SUBSTRATE when the rhythm is engine noise, and CONTESTED when real evidence is undermined by a named objection.

24.3.1 Traveling waves as a spatial organiser. Grade: CONTESTED, ascendant and improving

The supporting evidence is genuinely strong for a contested grade.

The phenomenon itself is no longer in doubt: theta travels along the rodent and human hippocampus, alpha and theta travel across the human cortex, sleep slow waves travel front to back, and wave direction and consistency correlate with memory encoding, retrieval, attention, and perception in study after study.

The 2023 sequence-learning result shows the proposed computation actually working in an artificial system, which strengthens plausibility.

Now the objections, which keep the grade at CONTESTED.

The central one is the correlational gap: almost all human evidence shows waves accompanying good performance, not waves causing it, and nobody has yet cleanly created or redirected a traveling wave and shown the predicted specific change in cognition.

The measurement objections of this book also apply with full force here.

Phase is hard to align across regions given conduction delays, meaning the time signals take to travel along fibres, so an apparent smooth phase gradient can be mimicked by two or three separate local oscillators with fixed offsets rather than one true propagating wave, and distinguishing these cases in scalp EEG is genuinely difficult because the skull blurs the signal.

Non-sinusoidal wave shapes, meaning rhythms whose cycles are peaked or sawtoothed rather than smoothly curved, can create spurious gradients and spurious coupling in the analysis pipeline.

Why ascendant and improving: the field has answered part of the mimicry objection by moving to direct brain recordings with dense grids, by testing whether wave direction shifts lawfully with task phase, which fixed local oscillators should not do, and by early perturbation attempts using patterned electrical and magnetic stimulation designed to launch or steer waves.

The honest summary for the beginner: traveling waves are real, their correlations with cognition are real, the causal experiment that would promote them to COMPUTATION has not yet been done, and the trend line of evidence points upward.



================================================================================

PART TEN. DEVELOPMENT AND EVOLUTION OF THE RHYTHMS

================================================================================

CHAPTER 25. THE DEVELOPMENT OF BRAIN RHYTHMS (CORE)

A newborn's EEG does not look like a quieter version of an adult's.

It is a different landscape, with rhythms that exist nowhere in adult life and with adult rhythms entirely absent, and the road from one landscape to the other takes about twenty-five years to walk.

This chapter follows the rhythms from the womb to old age, and it meshes deliberately with this corpus's companion volume THE_NEUROSCIENCE_OF_DEVELOPMENTAL_PSYCHOLOGY, which tells the behavioural side of the same story.

25.1 The fetal and preterm brain

Recordings from premature infants in intensive care, made through soft scalp sensors, give science its only direct window on the electrical life of the human brain in the third trimester, the final three months of pregnancy.

What that window shows is startling.

25.1.1 The discontinuous early record and the spontaneous activity transients

The early preterm EEG is discontinuous, a pattern clinicians call trace discontinu: bursts of high-amplitude activity separated by stretches of near-electrical silence lasting many seconds.

In an adult, such a burst-suppression pattern signals deep anaesthesia or serious brain injury, but in a preterm infant it is the normal and healthy mode of operation.

The bursts are called spontaneous activity transients, often abbreviated SATs, a term developed by Sampsa Vanhatalo, Kai Kaila, and colleagues in Helsinki through the 2000s.

Spontaneous here means generated from within, not triggered by any outside event: the immature brain does not yet have organised senses to drive it, so it drives itself, producing internally generated volleys of activity that sweep through developing circuits.

The evidence says these transients are not idle noise but construction traffic: their occurrence correlates positively with subsequent brain growth, and simultaneous EEG and functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI), a scan that maps brain activity through blood flow, showed in a 2017 eLife study that a major hub generating these bursting events in the preterm brain is the insula, one of the most densely connected regions of the developing cortex.

As the infant approaches full term, the silent gaps shrink and the record knits itself into the continuous activity that will characterise the rest of life.

25.1.2 Delta brushes and their wiring role

The signature rhythm of the preterm brain is the delta brush, and it exists at no other time of life.

A delta brush is a slow delta wave, roughly 0.3 to 1.5 Hz, meaning one cycle every one to three seconds, with a rapid oscillation of roughly 8 to 25 Hz riding on top of it, so that in the recording the slow wave looks as if someone had drawn fine bristles along its crest, hence the name brush.

Delta brushes appear at about 28 to 30 weeks of postmenstrual age, meaning weeks counted from the mother's last menstrual period, reach their peak incidence at about 32 to 35 weeks, and disappear around term, between about 38 and 42 weeks.

Their proposed job is wiring.
