During exactly this window, the cortex is being connected up under the guidance of the subplate, a transient waiting-room layer of neurons beneath the cortex that receives incoming fibres, holds them, and helps them find their final targets before the subplate itself largely dissolves.

Rustem Khazipov, Petr Milh, and colleagues showed in rodents that the equivalent early oscillations are triggered by spontaneous muscle twitches, and a 2013 study by Fabrice Wallois's group in PLOS ONE showed that gentle sounds mimicking the womb environment drive delta brushes in the temporal, meaning hearing-related, cortex of premature infants, while other work shows touch driving them in the corresponding body-map cortex.

The interpretation is that the delta brush is the sound of activity-dependent wiring: each brush is a burst of coordinated firing that lets connections obey the rule often summarised as cells that fire together wire together, testing and strengthening the still-provisional cabling between senses, subplate, and cortex.

This is the first mesh point with THE_NEUROSCIENCE_OF_DEVELOPMENTAL_PSYCHOLOGY: where that volume describes the fetus learning the mother's voice and the newborn preferring it, this volume supplies the mechanism-level counterpart, sensory-driven delta brushes sculpting auditory cortex before birth.

25.2 Maturation across childhood and adolescence

After birth the story becomes one of rhythms speeding up, new rhythms appearing on schedule, and finally, in adolescence, a great quieting.

25.2.1 The speeding posterior rhythm and the emergence of spindles

The adult's famous alpha rhythm, the roughly 10 Hz wave over the back of the head that Hans Berger first recorded in the 1920s, is not present at birth.

Its ancestor, called the posterior dominant rhythm, first appears at about 3 to 4 months of age at a frequency of only about 3.5 to 4 Hz, technically in the delta range even though it behaves like alpha, appearing when the eyes close and vanishing when they open.

It then climbs steadily: about 4 Hz by 6 months, about 6 Hz by 12 months, about 7 Hz by 18 months, about 8 Hz in most children by age 3, and the adult value near 10 Hz by about age 10.

The best available explanation for the climb is the progressive myelination of the loops between the thalamus, the brain's central relay station, and the cortex: as the cabling gains insulation, signals complete each loop faster, and the resonant frequency of the circuit rises, just as shortening a pendulum makes it swing faster.

Sleep spindles, the brief 11 to 16 Hz bursts of light sleep that this book treats at length as memory-consolidation machinery, have their own strict timetable: they emerge at about 4 to 8 weeks after full-term birth and are robust and abundant by about 3 months.

Their arrival marks the coming online of the thalamocortical circuitry, the loop between thalamus and cortex, that generates them, and clinicians use their presence and symmetry as a health check on that circuitry.

The mesh with THE_NEUROSCIENCE_OF_DEVELOPMENTAL_PSYCHOLOGY is precise here and worth stating carefully: the developmental volume notes that spindle emergence at two to three months coincides with the infant's first consolidation of procedural and statistical learning during sleep, and this volume confirms the timing - spindles present by roughly 2 to 3 months - while adding the honest caveat that in early infancy the coupling of spindles to slow oscillations is loose, and studies by Ines Wilhelm, Kerstin Hoedlmoser, and colleagues show that precise slow-oscillation-spindle coupling continues to tighten across childhood and adolescence as spindles mature, so the adult consolidation machinery assembles gradually rather than switching on all at once.

25.2.2 The slow-wave decline of adolescence as a pruning marker

Between roughly age 11 and age 17, the deep slow-wave activity of sleep, the large 0.5 to 4 Hz waves of the deepest sleep stage, falls by well over half.

If you saw such a collapse in an adult you would worry, but in a teenager it is one of the most reliable maturational curves in all of neuroscience.

Irwin Feinberg proposed in 1982, and spent the following four decades confirming with longitudinal recordings, that the adolescent decline in slow-wave activity tracks synaptic pruning: the planned removal of a large fraction of the synapses that childhood overproduced, keeping the connections experience has validated and deleting the rest, the way a sculptor removes marble to reveal the figure.

The logic is straightforward: slow waves are generated by huge populations of cortical synapses discharging in near-unison, so the amplitude of the slow waves is a rough gauge of how much synaptic machinery the cortex carries, and as pruning thins the machinery the waves shrink.

Longitudinal studies by Feinberg and Ian Campbell from 2010 onward showed the decline is steep, consistent across children, begins over the back of the brain and finishes over the front, mirroring the back-to-front timetable of cortical grey-matter thinning seen in magnetic resonance imaging by Jay Giedd, Nitin Gogtay, and colleagues.

Feinberg also made a sobering clinical conjecture: that schizophrenia, which typically declares itself in late adolescence, may reflect this pruning process overshooting, a proposal that received genetic support in 2016 when Aswin Sekar, Steven McCarroll, and colleagues linked schizophrenia risk to variants of the complement component 4 (C4) gene, part of a molecular tagging system that marks synapses for removal.

This is the third mesh point with THE_NEUROSCIENCE_OF_DEVELOPMENTAL_PSYCHOLOGY: where that volume presents adolescence behaviourally, as the refinement of judgment and the peak age of onset for psychiatric illness, this volume supplies the electrophysiological marker, the slow-wave decline, that lets a scalp recording watch the pruning happen.

The grading discipline requires a caution: the slow-wave decline is graded here as a marker, a readout of pruning, not as a computation, because no experiment shows that changing the waves changes the pruning, and the wave-to-synapse link, though strongly supported, remains an inference.

25.3 The lifespan trajectory

Development does not stop at twenty, and neither does the story of the rhythms.

25.3.1 The individual alpha frequency across life

Each adult has a personal alpha peak, the individual alpha frequency, typically somewhere between 8 and 12 Hz, and it is stable enough within a person to function almost like a fingerprint, with twin studies showing it is strongly heritable.

Across the population it follows an arc: rising through childhood as described above, peaking near 10 to 11 Hz in the twenties, holding through midlife, and then slowing gradually in older age, with average declines of several tenths of a hertz per decade late in life.

Because a slower alpha means each cycle takes longer, and because much evidence ties one alpha cycle to one window of perceptual sampling, the slowing has been proposed as one thread in the general slowing of processing speed with age, though this remains a correlation, not a demonstrated cause.

A practical warning for the scientist reader: many clinical studies compare patients against fixed frequency bands, but a person whose alpha sits at 8.5 Hz will smear activity into the neighbouring theta band and be misread, so good practice measures each individual's own peak first.

25.3.2 The aging spectrum and the uncoupling of slow oscillation and spindle

Aging changes the whole spectrum, not just alpha.

Slow-wave activity in sleep continues its lifelong decline, spindles become fewer, smaller, and shorter, and the background spectrum flattens, meaning the balance between slow and fast activity shifts in a way that Bradley Voytek and colleagues have argued reflects rising neural noise rather than any single rhythm changing.

The most instructive aging finding for this book concerns timing rather than amount.

Randolph Helfrich, Matthew Walker, Robert Knight, and colleagues showed in 2018, in the journal Neuron, that in older adults the sleep spindle drifts out of its precise alignment with the peak of the slow oscillation: in young adults the spindle rides the crest of the slow wave with tight timing, and in older adults it arrives too early and too variably.

Crucially, the degree of this mistiming predicted how much of the previous evening's learning was lost overnight, and the mistiming itself was linked to atrophy, meaning tissue loss, in the medial prefrontal cortex, the front-midline region that generates the slow oscillation.

The lesson generalises and matters for the konnectome build: the aging brain's memory problem here is not a missing component but a failed rendezvous, two mechanisms each still present but no longer meeting on schedule, exactly the failure a clocked digital system suffers when clock skew grows until data and clock edge miss each other.

The both-sides rule: a digital system escapes this failure with explicit handshaking or timestamps, which never drift with the hardware's age, and that is a genuine advantage of the explicit mechanism over the phase-alignment mechanism the brain uses.

25.4 The corpus link

This corpus is written as a mesh, with each volume carrying the level of description it is best placed to carry.

25.4.1 The mesh with THE_NEUROSCIENCE_OF_DEVELOPMENTAL_PSYCHOLOGY

The division of labour between the two volumes is as follows, and the mesh points are chosen so the two books agree on every shared fact.

• The developmental psychology volume owns the behavioural milestones - fetal voice learning, infant statistical learning, adolescent risk-taking and judgment - and this volume owns their electrophysiological counterparts, the delta brush, the spindle timetable, and the adolescent slow-wave decline.

• The shared timing claims are: delta brushes present from about 28 to 30 weeks postmenstrual age and gone by term, sleep spindles emerging at about 4 to 8 weeks after term birth and robust by about 3 months, the posterior dominant rhythm appearing at 3 to 4 months and reaching about 10 Hz by about age 10, and the adolescent slow-wave decline running from about age 11 to about 17 along a back-to-front gradient.

• Where the developmental volume ties spindles at two to three months to the onset of sleep-dependent consolidation of infant learning, this volume endorses the timing and adds the mechanism-level caveat that slow-oscillation-spindle coupling precision continues maturing into adolescence, so early consolidation likely runs on partially assembled machinery.

The reader who wants the whole developmental story should read the two chapters side by side, behaviour in one hand and voltage in the other.


CHAPTER 26. RHYTHMS ACROSS SPECIES (CONTEXT)

A mouse brain weighs about half a gram, and a human brain weighs about 1400 grams, several thousand times more.

Almost everything about the two organs scales with that difference: neuron count, wiring length, surface area.

The rhythms do not.

That single stubborn fact is among the deepest clues this book has to offer about what brain waves are, and it bears directly on the question of building a synthetic mind.

26.1 The preserved architecture

The place to start is the 2013 review that framed the puzzle for the field.

26.1.1 The puzzle of conserved frequencies across a thousandfold range of brain size

In 2013, in the journal Neuron, Gyorgy Buzsaki, Nikos Logothetis, and Wolf Singer published Scaling Brain Size, Keeping Timing: Evolutionary Preservation of Brain Rhythms, surveying rhythms across mammals from mouse to human.

Their conclusion was blunt: despite the several-thousandfold increase of brain volume across mammalian evolution, the whole hierarchy of brain oscillations is preserved, from slow oscillations below 1 Hz through theta, alpha, beta, and gamma, up to ripples above 100 Hz.

Every known pattern of local field potential activity, oscillatory or intermittent, found in the human brain is present in the other mammals investigated, and not only the frequency bands but their durations, their temporal structure, their nested relationships, and their behavioural correlations are conserved.

A rat's hippocampal theta runs at roughly the same handful of hertz as a human's, and a mouse's gamma occupies roughly the same 30 to 80 Hz band as a monkey's, even though the circuits generating them differ enormously in size.

Why is this a puzzle at all?

Because timing should get harder as brains grow: a signal crossing a mouse brain travels a few millimetres, while a signal crossing a human brain may travel fifteen centimetres or more, and if conduction speed stayed fixed, every timing relationship the small brain relied on would fall apart in the large one, the way an orchestra would fall apart if the players were spread across a whole city but still tried to follow one conductor by ear.

Evolution evidently treated the timing, not the anatomy, as the thing that must not change.

26.1.2 Large axons preserving timing as brains scale

The solution the 2013 review identified is in the wiring diameter.

An axon is the neuron's output cable, and thicker, more heavily myelinated axons conduct faster.

As brains grew, evolution did not thicken all axons proportionally, which would have made big brains ruinously bulky and energetically expensive; instead, the distribution of axon calibres grew a longer tail, adding a small population of very large, very fast long-range axons alongside the unchanged multitude of thin, slow local ones.

Those few express cables let distant regions of a large brain exchange signals in roughly the same number of milliseconds a small brain needs, preserving the timing relationships that the conserved rhythms depend on, while the bulk of the wiring stayed cheap and slow.

The engineering analogy is exact enough to use: a chip designer facing clock skew across a growing chip does not fatten every wire, but lays a few wide, fast clock-distribution trunks so that the timing signal reaches all corners within tolerance, and evolution's fat axons are that trunk network.

26.2 The comparative sweep

With the puzzle stated, the evidence deserves a walk through the animal kingdom, because the reach of the conservation is itself the argument.

26.2.1 Theta and gamma across mammals

Hippocampal theta appears in every mammal examined, from mice and rats through bats, rabbits, cats, dogs, and monkeys to humans, always tied to movement, exploration, and memory.

There are honest wrinkles to report.

Rodent theta during running is a strong continuous 6 to 10 Hz rhythm, while human hippocampal theta measured in neurosurgical patients, including in work by Joshua Jacobs's group, appears in shorter bouts and includes lower-frequency components near 3 to 4 Hz, and echolocating bats sparked a genuine dispute when recordings showed their hippocampal theta appearing only in brief intermittent bouts rather than continuously, which some researchers read as a challenge to theta's universality and others read as the same mechanism running on a different duty cycle.

The book's discipline requires saying plainly: the conservation claim is about the presence and band of the rhythms, and it survives these wrinkles, but the exact behavioural deployment varies by species.

Gamma, the fast 30 to 90 Hz rhythm generated by the interplay of excitatory neurons and fast inhibitory interneurons - an interneuron being a locally connecting neuron whose job is to inhibit, meaning suppress, its neighbours - appears with the same generating circuit, the same parvalbumin-positive basket-cell mechanism, in every mammal examined, and rides nested inside theta in rodent and human alike.

26.2.2 Sleep rhythms in birds and olfactory oscillations in insects

The conservation reaches beyond mammals, which strengthens the case enormously, because birds and mammals last shared an ancestor over 300 million years ago, and insects are farther still.

Birds sleep in two alternating states closely resembling mammalian slow-wave sleep and rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, the dreaming stage with darting eyes, complete with large slow waves in the first state and wake-like activity in the second, despite birds lacking the layered cortex that generates mammalian slow waves; their equivalent structure, the hyperpallium, produces slow waves that even propagate as traveling waves, as shown in work by Niels Rattenborg's group.

Rattenborg and colleagues also showed in 2016, in Nature Communications, that frigatebirds sleep in mid-flight, one hemisphere at a time or both at once, in brief bouts, which tells us sleep rhythms are so essential that evolution engineers around flight rather than around sleep.

In insects, Gilles Laurent and colleagues showed in the 1990s that when a locust smells an odour, its antennal lobe, the insect's first olfactory processing station, produces an approximately 20 Hz oscillation that synchronises ensembles of projection neurons, with the odour's identity carried by which neurons join the synchronised ensemble at which moments.

The causal capstone came in 1997, when Mark Stopfer, Laurent, and colleagues showed in Nature that blocking the oscillation in honeybees with picrotoxin, a drug that blocks the inhibitory gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) receptors that pace the rhythm, abolished the synchronisation without silencing the neurons, and the bees specifically lost the ability to discriminate chemically similar odours while still managing coarse discriminations.

That experiment remains one of the cleanest demonstrations anywhere in neuroscience that a rhythm's timing, not just its neurons' firing, carries information the animal uses, and it did so in a brain of fewer than a million neurons.

26.2.3 Why the frequencies are the evolutionary invariant, which matters for a synthetic mind

Assemble the pattern.

Anatomy varies wildly across species; the frequencies barely move.

When evolution conserves something across a thousandfold change in scale and across 300 million years of divergence, while freely redesigning the hardware around it, the conserved thing is almost certainly load-bearing, the way the conserved genetic code matters more than the wildly varying bodies it builds.

Buzsaki, Logothetis, and Singer's argument is that the frequencies are set by things that did not change across evolution: the time constants of neurons' membranes and synapses, meaning how quickly their electrical states rise and decay, the decay time of inhibition, and the timescales of the body and world the brain must serve - the tempo of sniffing, of limb movement, of syllables.

The hierarchy of rhythms is, on this view, the brain's timing architecture: a nested set of clock domains, from slow global coordination to fast local processing, and evolution scaled the hardware while holding the timing specification fixed, paying for it in expensive fat axons.

Here is why this matters for the synthetic-mind question, stated as directly as the evidence allows.

First, it identifies what to copy: if you set out to build a konnectome-style synthetic mind by imitating the brain, the cross-species evidence says the thing to imitate is not the neuron count or the wiring diagram, which evolution treats as negotiable, but the timing architecture, the preserved hierarchy of nested timescales and the guarantee that distant modules can interact within a fixed millisecond budget.

Second, it frees the builder from biology's constraint: the brain holds its frequencies fixed because its components' time constants are fixed by chemistry, but silicon is millions of times faster, so a synthetic mind need not run at 8 Hz theta; what it must preserve, if the argument is right, is the ratios and the nesting, the relative structure of slow coordination enclosing fast computation, which could in principle be run at any absolute speed.

Third, the both-sides rule of this book applies at full strength: a digital system can implement a nested timing hierarchy explicitly, with clock domains, schedulers, and logical clocks, gaining exactness the brain lacks, and the open question, flagged honestly as open, is whether anything about the brain's analog, wave-based implementation of its timing hierarchy contributes computationally beyond what the explicit digital version captures.

The comparative evidence cannot settle that question, but it tells us precisely where the question lives, and that is why a chapter of animal recordings belongs in a book that ends with an engineering build.



================================================================================

PART ELEVEN. WHEN RHYTHMS GO WRONG: THE OSCILLOPATHIES

================================================================================

CHAPTER 27. EPILEPSY: PATHOLOGICAL SYNCHRONY (CORE)

Through this whole book, synchrony - many neurons oscillating together - has been the hero of the story, the mechanism proposed to bind, to route, to remember.

Epilepsy is the disease that shows the hero's dark side.

Epilepsy is a condition, affecting roughly one person in a hundred worldwide, in which networks of neurons periodically fall into runaway synchronised firing, producing seizures: episodes in which the excess synchrony hijacks behaviour, awareness, or both.

It is, in the vocabulary of this book, the first and defining oscillopathy, a disease of rhythm.

27.1 The pathological rhythms

Epileptic brains produce electrical signatures that healthy brains do not, and two of them teach the most.

27.1.1 The spike-and-wave of absence seizures

An absence seizure, once called petit mal, is epilepsy at its most deceptively gentle: a child, most often between about four and ten years old, simply stops - mid-sentence, mid-step - stares blankly for five to ten seconds, and then resumes as if nothing happened, unaware of the gap.

A child can have dozens or even hundreds of these episodes a day, and before diagnosis they are routinely mistaken for daydreaming.

The EEG during an absence is one of the most recognisable patterns in clinical medicine: generalised spike-and-wave discharges at approximately 3 Hz, meaning three times per second, appearing across the whole scalp at once, each cycle a sharp spike followed by a rounded slow wave, starting and stopping abruptly.

The generator is the same thalamocortical loop this book met in the sleep chapters, the reciprocal circuit between the thalamus and the cortex that produces sleep spindles, now captured by a pathological mode.

Decades of work, including rodent studies by Vincenzo Crunelli, John Huguenard, David McCormick, and colleagues, traced the mechanism to the loop's own machinery: thalamic neurons carry low-threshold calcium channels, called T-type channels, that let them fire rebound bursts after inhibition, and the thalamic reticular nucleus, a shell of inhibitory neurons wrapped around the thalamus, delivers that inhibition rhythmically, so the loop can lock into a self-sustaining cycle of burst, inhibition, rebound, burst.

The clinical pharmacology confirms the mechanism with unusual elegance: ethosuximide, a first-line drug specifically effective against absence seizures and little else, works by blocking those T-type calcium channels, and drugs that deepen the relevant inhibition can actually worsen absences.

The lesson for this book is pointed: the absence seizure is not an alien rhythm invading the brain but a healthy oscillator, the spindle-generating loop, pushed past a stability boundary, hypersynchrony as the failure mode of a mechanism that exists because moderate synchrony is useful.

27.1.2 High-frequency oscillations as a seizure-zone biomarker, and the honest caveat that they do not always beat spikes

For the roughly one third of patients whose seizures resist medication, surgery to remove the seizure-generating tissue can be curative, and the whole enterprise depends on answering one question: which tissue?

For a century the main electrical marker was the interictal spike, a brief sharp discharge appearing between seizures, interictal meaning between attacks, but spikes are imperfect guides, often appearing far beyond the tissue that actually launches seizures.

In the 2000s a better candidate emerged: high-frequency oscillations (HFOs), very fast ripples in recordings taken from electrodes placed inside the brain, conventionally divided into ripples at 80 to 250 Hz and fast ripples at 250 to 500 Hz.

Fast ripples in particular drew excitement, in work by Anatol Bragin, Jerome Engel, Jean Gotman, and colleagues, because unlike the physiological ripples of memory consolidation that this book celebrates elsewhere, fast ripples appear largely confined to diseased tissue.

Retrospective studies were encouraging: Julia Jacobs and colleagues reported in 2010 that patients in whom the HFO-generating tissue was removed had markedly better odds of freedom from seizures than patients in whom it was left behind.

Now the honest caveat, which the outline demands and the evidence requires.

When the idea was put to prospective tests, it stumbled: trials and multicentre studies in the late 2010s and 2020s, including a randomised trial by Maeike Zijlmans's group in the Netherlands, failed to show that surgery guided by HFOs beats surgery guided by conventional spikes, and analyses found that in a meaningful fraction of individual patients spikes actually localised the epileptic tissue as well as or better than HFOs.

The reasons are instructive rather than embarrassing: physiological ripples from normal memory processes contaminate the pathological signal and no algorithm yet separates them reliably, HFO rates vary from night to night in the same patient, and detection depends heavily on electrode placement and detector settings.

The current consensus position is deliberately modest: HFOs, especially fast ripples, are a genuinely informative biomarker that adds evidence, but they have not dethroned the humble spike, and a wise surgical team uses both.

This book grades the claim accordingly: HFOs as a superior localising biomarker is CONTESTED, with real supporting evidence and a named, replicated failure to beat the old standard in prospective tests.

27.2 The lesson

Every disease chapter in this book closes by asking what the pathology teaches about the healthy design.

27.2.1 Too much synchrony as a failure mode, which a synthetic mind must also fear

The deepest lesson of epilepsy is that synchrony is not a good in itself: it is a resource with a narrow healthy operating range, and the seizure shows what lies past the upper edge.

Consider what a seizure actually is, informationally: during full hypersynchrony, vast populations of neurons all say the same thing at the same time, and a system in which every element carries the same signal carries almost no information, however loudly it fires - a million-voice choir singing one note conveys less than a single sentence of conversation.

Consciousness and processing fail during a generalised seizure not because the brain falls silent but because it becomes too coordinated to compute.

The healthy brain, accordingly, spends real machinery holding itself below the boundary: fast inhibitory interneurons pace and cap runaway excitation, inhibitory and excitatory currents track each other in a tight balance physiologists call excitation-inhibition balance, and the fact that roughly one percent of humans nonetheless cross the boundary tells us how close to the edge the healthy operating point sits.

Theoretical work frames this as operating near criticality, the knife-edge between dying-out activity and runaway activity where information transmission is richest, a productive but still-debated idea, and epilepsy is what living near that edge costs.

Now the engineering half, for the konnectome build, stated per the design test with both sides.

A synthetic mind that uses wave-like coordination - shared clock signals, oscillatory gating, resonance between modules - inherits epilepsy's failure mode by construction: any system of coupled oscillators with adjustable coupling strengths has a region of parameter space where the oscillators lock into global synchrony, as the mathematics of coupled oscillators going back to Yoshiki Kuramoto's 1975 model makes precise, and engineers have already met this failure in power grids, where generator synchronisation runs away, and in bridges, most famously London's Millennium Bridge in 2000, which swayed dangerously when pedestrians' footfalls spontaneously synchronised.

So a konnectome that borrows the brain's oscillatory coordination must also borrow its safeguards: dedicated inhibition budgeted alongside excitation, monitoring for rising global coherence as an alarm signal, and circuit breakers that decouple modules when coherence exceeds a threshold, which is essentially what the vagus nerve stimulators and responsive neurostimulation implants used in human epilepsy do, detecting the onset of hypersynchrony and injecting disrupting stimulation within milliseconds.

The both-sides rule: a purely digital coordination scheme, with explicit schedulers and message passing and no shared oscillatory medium, simply cannot have a seizure, and that is a genuine and possibly decisive advantage of the explicit mechanism, purchased at the price of giving up whatever computational benefits the wave-like mechanisms of this book actually deliver.

Epilepsy is thus the strongest single argument on the digital side of this book's running ledger, and any honest design for a synthetic mind must weigh it there.

CHAPTER 28. PARKINSON'S DISEASE AND PATHOLOGICAL BETA (CORE)

If epilepsy showed us what happens when synchrony runs completely out of control, Parkinson's disease shows us something subtler and, in a way, more instructive.

Here a normal, everyday brain rhythm - the beta rhythm, a wave of electrical activity repeating roughly 13 to 35 times per second - does not vanish and does not explode.

It simply becomes too strong, too persistent, and too widely shared across a circuit, and movement grinds toward stillness.

Parkinson's disease is a condition in which brain cells that make the chemical messenger dopamine slowly die, leaving patients with slowness of movement, stiffness, and often tremor.

The story of its exaggerated beta rhythm is one of the clearest in this whole book, because it ends with something rare: a rhythm-based measurement that has passed from the laboratory into an approved medical device that listens to the wave and acts on it.

28.1 The exaggerated beta

Before we can appreciate what goes wrong, we need the normal picture.

Beta rhythm is the brain's ordinary accompaniment to holding steady: it rises when you maintain a posture or a plan, and it briefly drops just before and during a voluntary movement, as if the brain releases a brake.

In Parkinson's disease, that brake seems to get stuck on.

28.1.1 Synchronised anti-kinetic beta in the basal-ganglia loop

Deep beneath the wrinkled outer surface of the brain, the cortex, sits a cluster of interconnected structures called the basal ganglia, which act something like a gatekeeper for movement, helping to select which actions to release and which to suppress.

One small member of this cluster, the subthalamic nucleus, became accessible to direct recording in the 1990s and 2000s because surgeons implant electrodes there to treat Parkinson's disease.

A neuron, for the newcomer, is a brain cell that sends brief electrical pulses, and an electrode is simply a thin wire that can pick up the summed electrical activity of thousands of such cells at once.

When researchers such as Peter Brown and colleagues in London listened to the subthalamic nucleus of patients who were off their medication, starting with influential recordings around 2001, they heard beta activity in the 13 to 35 cycles per second range that was strikingly exaggerated: larger, more sustained, and more synchronised across the whole basal-ganglia loop than in comparable recordings from patients with other conditions.

Synchronised here means that huge populations of neurons were rising and falling together, locked to the same beat, instead of doing their own varied jobs.

The rhythm earned the label anti-kinetic, meaning against movement, because its strength tracks the symptoms: the more beta, the slower and stiffer the patient, and the level of beta correlates with the degree of dopamine loss in animal models of the disease.

A useful everyday picture is an office in which everyone stops their individual tasks to chant the same slogan in unison: the sound is loud and coordinated, but no actual work gets done.

An important refinement emerged in the 2010s from Peter Brown's group and from Simon Little and colleagues: pathological beta does not hum along continuously but comes in bursts, brief packets of elevated rhythm, and it is specifically the long bursts, lasting several hundred milliseconds or more, that correlate with worse movement, while short bursts appear in healthy circuits too.

That detail matters for everything that follows, because it suggests the disease is not the presence of beta but the failure to terminate it.

28.1.2 Dopamine and deep brain stimulation suppressing it

Two very different treatments for Parkinson's disease turn out to do the same thing to the beta rhythm, and that convergence is a large part of why researchers take the rhythm seriously.

The first treatment is the drug levodopa, which the brain converts into dopamine, the chemical messenger the disease has depleted.

When patients take levodopa, the exaggerated beta in the subthalamic nucleus shrinks within tens of minutes, and studies from the early 2000s onward, including work by Andrea Kühn, Peter Brown, and colleagues around 2006, showed that the amount of beta suppression correlates with the amount of clinical improvement in slowness and stiffness.

The second treatment is deep brain stimulation (DBS), a surgical therapy in which a thin electrode implanted in the subthalamic nucleus delivers continuous electrical pulses, typically at about 130 pulses per second, from a battery-powered device in the chest, somewhat like a pacemaker for the brain.

Deep brain stimulation also suppresses the exaggerated beta while it improves movement, and again the degree of beta suppression tracks the degree of benefit.

There is even a mirror-image experiment: stimulating the circuit at beta frequency itself, around 20 pulses per second, measurably slows movement in patients and boosts cortical beta, which is exactly what one would predict if the rhythm were anti-kinetic.

Honesty requires the standard caution, and it will echo through the rest of this book: correlation, even beautiful bidirectional correlation, is not yet proof that beta causes the slowness rather than merely accompanying whatever does.

The beta-frequency stimulation studies push toward cause, but the effect sizes on movement are modest, and some researchers hold that beta is the most visible symptom of a deeper derangement in how basal-ganglia neurons fire, not the culprit itself.

28.2 The closed-loop lesson

Whatever beta's ultimate causal status, the Parkinson's story delivered a lesson that no other rhythm in this book has yet matched: a brain wave became the control signal in an approved, commercially available feedback loop.

For the engineer, this is the difference between watching a gauge and wiring the gauge to the throttle.

28.2.1 Beta as a control signal for adaptive stimulation. Grade: COMPUTATION for the biomarker

Conventional deep brain stimulation is open loop, a control-engineering term meaning the device stimulates constantly at fixed settings, blind to what the brain is doing, the way an old air conditioner runs at one speed regardless of room temperature.

Adaptive deep brain stimulation closes the loop: the implanted electrode both stimulates and listens, measuring the patient's own beta level in real time and turning stimulation up when beta rises and down when it falls, like a thermostat.

The proof-of-principle experiment was published by Simon Little, Peter Brown, and colleagues in Annals of Neurology in 2013: in eight patients, stimulation triggered by the patient's own subthalamic beta improved movement scores more than conventional continuous stimulation did, while delivering less than half the electrical energy.

The idea then survived a decade of engineering and clinical testing, culminating in the Adaptive DBS Algorithm for Personalized Therapy in Parkinson's Disease (ADAPT-PD) trial led by Helen Bronte-Stewart of Stanford University, and on 24 February 2025 the United States Food and Drug Administration (FDA), the American medical regulator, approved the first adaptive deep brain stimulation system for Parkinson's disease, Medtronic's BrainSense Adaptive system, which uses sensed beta-band activity as its feedback signal.

Now to the grade, and to why it is worded so carefully.

The outline grades this story COMPUTATION for the biomarker, and the qualification is the whole point.

A biomarker is a measurable signal that reliably indicates a biological state, the way a fever indicates infection without being the infection.

What has been proven, to the standard of a randomised device trial and regulatory approval, is that beta power carries enough real-time information about the patient's motor state to steer therapy: reading the wave and acting on it works better, or at least as well with less energy and fewer side effects, than ignoring it.

That is a genuine computational use of the rhythm by us, the engineers, and it could not work if the rhythm did not faithfully encode the clinical state moment by moment.

What has not been proven is the stronger claim that beta is the mechanism of the slowness, the engine rather than a very well-calibrated gauge, and the honest reader should keep those two claims apart.

The grade is earned on the first claim and deliberately silent on the second.

For the konnectome builder, the translation is direct: this is telemetry-driven adaptive control, a system that monitors its own internal congestion signal and throttles an intervention against it, and the Parkinson's field showed that even an imperfectly understood internal signal can be good enough to close a loop around, provided it is cheap to measure and tightly correlated with the state you care about.


CHAPTER 29. SCHIZOPHRENIA AND THE GAMMA STORY (CORE)

Schizophrenia is a serious psychiatric illness affecting roughly one person in a hundred, marked by hallucinations, delusions, disorganised thought, and, less famously but more stubbornly, deep difficulties with attention, memory, and the coordination of thought.

If Parkinson's disease is the story of one rhythm turned up too loud, schizophrenia has long been told as the story of a rhythm turned down: gamma, the fast rhythm of roughly 30 to 80 cycles per second that earlier chapters tied to local circuits of fast inhibitory cells.

The gamma story of schizophrenia is one of the most complete mechanistic chains in psychiatry, running from a molecule to a cell type to a rhythm to a symptom, and that completeness is exactly why it deserves careful, honest telling.

29.1 The parvalbumin account

The account centres on one particular kind of brain cell, and it is worth building it up from the ground.

An interneuron is a locally connected neuron whose job is inhibition, meaning it releases a chemical messenger, gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), that briefly silences its neighbours, the way a conductor's raised hand silences an orchestra section.

Among interneurons, the fast-spiking cells that contain a calcium-binding protein called parvalbumin are the metronomes of the cortex: earlier in this book we saw that their rapid, rhythmic inhibition is what paces the gamma rhythm, a claim cemented by the optogenetic experiments of Jessica Cardin and colleagues and of Vikaas Sohal and colleagues, both published in 2009, in which driving parvalbumin cells with light generated gamma and enhanced information flow through the circuit.

29.1.1 Reduced gamma synchrony and the interneuron deficit

The clinical side of the account begins with electroencephalography (EEG), the technique of recording the brain's summed electrical waves from small metal discs on the scalp.

Across hundreds of studies since the 1990s, patients with schizophrenia show, on average, reduced gamma-band power and reduced gamma synchrony when performing tasks that demand perception, attention, or working memory, the mental notepad that holds information for a few seconds.

Kwang-Hyuk Lee, Peter Uhlhaas, Wolf Singer, and others documented these deficits across paradigms through the 2000s, and the deficits correlate with the cognitive symptoms, which are the symptoms current drugs help least.

The tissue side comes largely from post-mortem studies, examinations of donated brains after death, led over decades by David Lewis and colleagues at the University of Pittsburgh.

They found that in the cortex of people who had schizophrenia, parvalbumin interneurons are not missing but appear weakened: the cells make less of the enzyme that synthesises GABA, called glutamic acid decarboxylase 67, and less parvalbumin itself, particularly in the prefrontal cortex, the front region most tied to planning and working memory.

Put the two halves together and you get the parvalbumin account: the cortical metronomes are underpowered, so the gamma rhythm they pace is weak and poorly synchronised, so the fast coordination of neural activity that supports coherent perception and thought is degraded.

It is a beautiful chain, and the honest caveats belong right beside it.

Gamma measured at the scalp is a fragile quantity, easily contaminated by tiny muscle movements around the eyes and scalp, and some early reports of altered gamma had to be re-examined for exactly that reason.

Medication, smoking, and the general difficulty patients have with demanding tasks all complicate the comparison.

And the deficit is not perfectly uniform: some studies find elevated spontaneous background gamma alongside reduced task-evoked gamma, suggesting a noisier circuit rather than simply a quieter one.

The chain is strong, but every link has error bars.

29.1.2 The receptor-hypofunction and disinhibition model

Why would parvalbumin cells weaken in the first place?

The leading answer starts with a receptor, a molecular antenna on a cell's surface that responds to a chemical messenger.

The N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptor is a special antenna for glutamate, the brain's main excitatory messenger, and it has long been tied to schizophrenia by a striking pharmacological fact: drugs that block NMDA receptors, such as phencyclidine and ketamine, can produce in healthy volunteers a temporary state that resembles schizophrenia, including its cognitive symptoms.

The hypofunction model, developed by researchers including John Olney, Bita Moghaddam, John Lisman, and David Lewis from the 1990s onward, proposes that in schizophrenia the NMDA receptors specifically on parvalbumin interneurons are underactive.

Hypofunction simply means working below normal strength.

Here comes the elegant twist, called disinhibition: weaken the excitatory drive onto inhibitory cells, and the inhibitory cells fire less, so their targets, the excitatory pyramidal cells, are released from inhibition and fire more, but in a disorganised, poorly timed way.

The circuit becomes paradoxically both noisier and less rhythmic, which matches the observation of elevated background gamma with reduced task-driven gamma.

Supporting experiments include mouse work such as Korotkova and colleagues in 2010 and Carlén and colleagues, published in Molecular Psychiatry in 2011 and 2012, in which deleting NMDA receptors only from parvalbumin cells disrupted gamma rhythms and produced schizophrenia-like behavioural changes, and slice and model studies reviewed by Gonzalez-Burgos and Lewis in Schizophrenia Bulletin in 2012.

The honest counterweight: this remains a model, not a settled fact.

Genetic studies of schizophrenia point to hundreds of small risk factors, many involving glutamate signalling but few pointing uniquely at this one synapse, and mouse deletions are far blunter than whatever subtle developmental process operates in patients.

The model has earned its prominence by connecting drug effects, post-mortem findings, and rhythm deficits into one narrative, and it should be taught as the leading candidate narrative rather than the answer.

29.2 The translational assay

A mechanistic story becomes clinically useful when it yields an assay, a standardised measurement that can be run the same way in a mouse, a healthy volunteer, and a patient.

The gamma story of schizophrenia has produced one of psychiatry's best-known candidates, along with a companion from sleep.

29.2.1 The forty-hertz steady-state response and the spindle deficit

The auditory steady-state response (ASSR) is a simple, clever probe: play a sound that clicks or flutters at a fixed rate, and the auditory cortex, the hearing region of the brain, echoes the rhythm, producing an electrical wave at exactly the driving frequency that EEG can measure.

Healthy auditory cortex resonates best near 40 cycles per second, conventionally written 40 hertz, which is squarely in the gamma band, and the response is thought to depend on the same parvalbumin and GABA circuitry that paces spontaneous gamma.

The core finding, first reported by Kwon, McCarley, and colleagues in 1999 and confirmed by a meta-analysis, a formal statistical pooling of studies, by Thuné, Recasens, and Uhlhaas in JAMA Psychiatry in 2016: patients with schizophrenia show a reduced and less phase-locked 40-hertz response.

The deficit appears in first-degree relatives and in some individuals at elevated risk before full illness, and because the same click-train test can be run in rodents, it serves as a translational bridge for drug development.

Its limits are equally real: the reduction is a group-average effect with overlap between patients and healthy people, so it cannot diagnose an individual, and reduced 40-hertz responses also appear in bipolar disorder and other conditions, so it is a marker of a kind of circuit dysfunction rather than of schizophrenia specifically.

A 2025 Danish high-risk study even found comparable 40-hertz responses in children at familial risk and controls, a reminder that the developmental story is not simple.

The companion finding comes from sleep.

A sleep spindle is a brief burst of 11 to 16 cycles per second activity, lasting around a second, generated by a pacemaker circuit in the thalamus, the brain's central relay station, during light non-dreaming sleep; spindles were introduced earlier in this book as workhorses of overnight memory consolidation.

Fabio Ferrarelli, Giulio Tononi, and colleagues reported in 2007 and 2010 that patients with schizophrenia have markedly fewer and weaker sleep spindles, and later work found the deficit in young, unmedicated patients and, more weakly, in unaffected relatives, arguing it is not a drug effect.

The proposed thread connecting spindles to the rest of the story is again inhibitory timing: the thalamic pacemaker that makes spindles, the thalamic reticular nucleus, is itself rich in parvalbumin neurons, and mouse work published in Scientific Reports in 2019 showed that damping those neurons weakens spindles and produces other schizophrenia-like electrical signatures.

So the chapter closes with a disciplined summary: schizophrenia research owns psychiatry's most complete rhythm narrative, molecule to cell to wave to symptom, plus two measurable assays, the 40-hertz response and the spindle count.

None of it yet constitutes a diagnostic test or a proven cause, and the next chapter shows what happens when the field forgets that distinction.


CHAPTER 30. THE CONTESTED BIOMARKERS (CONTEXT)

Every field has a drawer of almosts, and this chapter opens it deliberately.

The rhythms in this chapter were each, at some point, announced as a practical clinical tool: a brain-wave ratio that would help diagnose attention problems, an asymmetry that would flag depression, a flickering light that might treat Alzheimer's disease, a grand theory uniting pain, ringing ears, and tremor under one rhythm.

Each announcement was based on real data, and each has since run into serious, well-documented trouble.

We tell these stories not to sneer but because a reader who knows how rhythm claims fail is far better equipped to judge the ones that might succeed.

30.1 The rhythms that did not translate

Translation, in medicine, means the journey from a laboratory finding to a tool that changes decisions in a clinic.

The two stories here got farther down that road than almost any other EEG measure, one all the way to regulatory clearance, which makes their stumbles all the more instructive.

30.1.1 The theta-beta ratio in attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder and its failure as a standalone test

Attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) is a common condition, usually identified in childhood, involving persistent difficulty with attention, impulse control, and restlessness.

The candidate biomarker was disarmingly simple: divide the power of slow theta waves, about 4 to 8 cycles per second, by the power of faster beta waves, about 13 to 30 cycles per second, measured at a single point on top of the head.

Children with ADHD, early studies from the 1990s and 2000s reported, tended to show a higher theta-beta ratio, as if their waking EEG were tilted toward drowsy slowness, and early pooled analyses claimed impressive separation between ADHD and typical children.

In 2013 the United States Food and Drug Administration cleared a device called the Neuropsychiatric EEG-Based Assessment Aid (NEBA), which measured exactly this ratio in patients aged six to seventeen as an aid, not a standalone test, to ADHD diagnosis, making it one of the first brain-wave measures ever cleared for a psychiatric purpose.

Then the foundation shifted.

A 2013 meta-analysis by Martijn Arns and colleagues, pooling studies across decades, found that the separation between groups had been shrinking year by year, with newer and larger studies finding much smaller or no differences, a classic warning sign that early results were inflated by small samples and publication bias, the tendency of exciting positive findings to get published while dull negative ones stay in drawers.

Only around 25 to 40 percent of children with ADHD show a genuinely elevated ratio in modern samples, and elevated ratios occur in typical children too.

In 2016 the American Academy of Neurology issued a formal practice advisory concluding the theta-beta ratio should not be used to confirm an ADHD diagnosis, warning that its misuse risked significant harm through misdiagnosis, and Arns and colleagues publicly argued the same year that the regulatory clearance had outrun the evidence.

The current, chastened consensus is that the ratio failed as a diagnostic test but may retain a narrower life as a prognostic marker, a signal about which patients might respond to which treatment, a hypothesis still under study.

The lesson to carry forward: a group-average difference, however statistically real, is not a diagnostic test, because diagnosis happens one individual at a time, in the overlap zone where distributions blur.

30.1.2 Frontal alpha asymmetry in depression and its reliability problem

The second story begins with a genuinely elegant idea from the 1980s and 1990s, developed prominently by Richard Davidson and colleagues.

Alpha waves, the roughly 8 to 12 cycles per second rhythm strongest when a brain region idles, can be read in reverse: more alpha over a region suggests less activity there.

Davidson's approach-withdrawal model proposed that the left frontal cortex leans toward approach, engagement, and positive mood while the right leans toward withdrawal and negative mood, so a person with relatively more left-frontal alpha, meaning a relatively underactive left frontal lobe, should be tilted toward depression.

Early studies found exactly that pattern in depressed patients and even in infants of depressed mothers, and frontal alpha asymmetry became one of the most cited biomarkers in affective neuroscience, spawning clinical claims and commercial neurofeedback offerings.

The reliability problem then arrived in three waves.

First, individual measurement proved unstable: a person's asymmetry score varies from session to session, with eye position, recording length, and electrode reference choices all shifting it, and a trait marker that will not hold still across a week is a poor flag for a lasting condition.

Second, large studies stopped finding the group difference: a meta-analysis by van der Vinne and colleagues published in NeuroImage: Clinical in 2017 pooled thousands of participants and found that after accounting for heterogeneity, the average difference between depressed and healthy groups was not significant, a paper pointedly subtitled fact or fiction.

Third, a multiverse analysis by Kołodziej and colleagues in 2020, a method that runs every reasonable combination of analysis choices to see whether a result survives, found no reliable relationship between frontal alpha asymmetry and depression across three studies under nearly any choice.

Later pooled analyses continue to find, at best, small and inconsistent effects.

The fair verdict is not that the original work was fraudulent but that a subtle, state-dependent signal was asked to serve as a stable individual diagnostic, a job it could not do, and the field took two decades to run the large, boring confirmation studies that would have revealed this earlier.

30.2 The contested therapy

The previous stories concerned measurement.

This one concerns treatment, it is still unfolding as this book is written, and it is the sharpest live test of this book's central question, because the therapy only makes sense if driving a rhythm from outside can causally change biology deep in the brain.

30.2.1 Forty-hertz sensory stimulation in Alzheimer's disease: the mouse promise and the mixed human replications

Alzheimer's disease is the most common cause of dementia, the progressive loss of memory and thinking, and is marked in the brain by accumulations of a sticky protein fragment called amyloid-beta and tangles of a protein called tau.

In December 2016, Li-Huei Tsai's laboratory at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, with Hannah Iaccarino and Annabelle Singer as lead authors, published a Nature paper that startled the field: exposing Alzheimer's-model mice to light flickering 40 times per second, one hour a day, entrained gamma-band activity in the visual cortex, activated microglia, the brain's resident cleanup cells, and reduced amyloid-beta levels.

Follow-up papers extended the effect to 40-hertz sound and to combined audiovisual stimulation, reported spread of the effects beyond sensory areas, protection of neurons, and improved memory in mice, and the approach was named gamma entrainment using sensory stimuli, abbreviated GENUS.

The promise was intoxicating: a non-invasive, drug-free, seemingly safe flicker that recruits the brain's own gamma machinery to clean itself.

Small human trials began, and early results were encouraging in tone: pilot and feasibility studies by Cognito Therapeutics, whose device delivers 40-hertz light and sound through a headset, reported in the OVERTURE trial and related publications through 2024 that treated patients showed slower brain shrinkage, preserved white matter, and less functional decline, with good safety.

A pivotal trial named HOPE, enrolling 670 patients, the largest device trial ever run solely in Alzheimer's disease, completed enrolment in 2025, with results still pending as this book is written.

Now the other side of the ledger, which is unusually heavy and must be reported in full.

Independent mouse replications have repeatedly failed to find the core biological effect.

A 2023 study in eNeuro exposed a standard Alzheimer's mouse model to weeks of 24, 40, or 80 hertz flicker and found no reduction in amyloid load and no change in microglia.

More pointedly, Mihály Soula, György Buzsáki, and colleagues published in Nature Neuroscience in 2023 a study titled, with unusual bluntness, Forty-hertz light stimulation does not entrain native gamma oscillations in Alzheimer's disease model mice: recording directly from neurons, they found the flicker produced only a weak rhythmic response in visual cortex, did not engage the brain's own gamma-generating circuits, barely reached the hippocampus, the deep memory structure, and did not reduce amyloid or activate microglia.

The distinction they pressed is one this book has equipped you to appreciate: a rhythmic sensory input produces a train of evoked responses, the brain echoing an external drumbeat, which is not the same thing as recruiting the brain's own internally generated gamma oscillation, and the therapeutic hypothesis rests on the latter.

In humans, an early 40-hertz light study by Ismail and colleagues in 2018 found no decrease in cortical amyloid, and a careful 2025 single-session study found that flicker entrains the EEG but does not improve perception, attention, or memory in healthy people.

Yet the picture refuses to collapse cleanly: some human intracranial recordings do show 40-hertz responses reaching the hippocampus in some individuals, some independent human pilot studies report hints of benefit on some measures, and systematic reviews in 2025 describe the evidence as genuinely mixed rather than negative.

The honest summary, and the only one this book will give: the mouse-to-mouse replication record is poor, the mechanistic foundation is under direct challenge from strong laboratories, the human clinical signals so far come mostly from small or company-run studies, and a large pivotal trial may yet settle the clinical question regardless of the mechanistic dispute.

An effective therapy with a wrong mechanism would still be a therapy; a beautiful mechanism without replication is not.

As of this writing, no one is entitled to certainty in either direction.

30.3 The unifying proposal

The chapter ends with the grandest claim of all: a single rhythmic disease mechanism proposed to underlie conditions as different as chronic pain, ringing in the ears, tremor, and depression.

Grand unifications deserve both admiration and heightened scrutiny, and this one has received far more of the former than the latter.

30.3.1 Thalamocortical dysrhythmia across pain, tinnitus, and tremor

In 1999, Rodolfo Llinás and colleagues at New York University published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences a magnetoencephalography study, magnetoencephalography (MEG) being the technique of measuring the tiny magnetic fields produced by the brain's electrical currents.

Comparing healthy people with a small group of patients suffering from neurogenic pain, tinnitus, which is the perception of ringing or hissing with no external sound, Parkinson's disease, or depression, they reported that the patients showed excess slow theta-range activity, around 4 to 8 cycles per second, along with abnormal coupling between that slow activity and faster rhythms.

Their proposed mechanism, named thalamocortical dysrhythmia, is genuinely elegant.

The thalamus, the brain's central relay station, contains neurons with two modes: a tonic mode of steady, faithful relaying, and a burst mode of rhythmic slow firing driven by special calcium channels, a mode that normally belongs to sleep.

The proposal holds that injury, sensory loss, or chemical imbalance can lock patches of thalamus into waking burst mode, dragging their connected cortical regions into slow theta rhythm; around the edge of each slowed patch, inhibition fails and fast gamma activity leaks out, the proposed edge effect, and that edge gamma is experienced as the positive symptom: pain with no injury, sound with no source, tremor, or intrusive negative mood.

Different symptoms, one mechanism, differing only in which patch of thalamus is affected.

The idea has real supporting threads: burst firing has been recorded in the thalamus of pain patients, some surgical interventions targeting the proposed circuit have been reported to help selected patients, and studies in tinnitus by Dirk De Ridder and colleagues, who published a theoretical update in 2015, have reported the predicted coupling patterns in some samples.

The critical ledger, however, is substantial.

The founding study rested on ten patients spread across four different diseases.

Direct tests of the predicted signatures have repeatedly disappointed: Zobay and Adjamian in 2015 found no difference in the predicted theta-gamma coupling between tinnitus patients and controls in a sample far larger than the original study, and the wider tinnitus EEG literature is marked by small samples and inconsistent findings.

The theory's breadth is also its weakness: a framework flexible enough to explain pain, tinnitus, tremor, and depression at once is hard to pin down to a decisive prediction that could refute it.

A quarter century on, thalamocortical dysrhythmia remains what it was: a fertile, influential hypothesis that has inspired treatments and organised thinking, but not a confirmed disease mechanism, and its history is a standing reminder that in rhythm science, explanatory reach and evidential weight are different things.



================================================================================

PART TWELVE. THE RECKONING: ENGINE OR EXHAUST

================================================================================

CHAPTER 31. THE EPIPHENOMENON DEBATE (CONTEXT)

Every chapter of this book has been quietly building toward the argument this chapter finally has in the open.

You have now seen rhythms that steer approved medical devices, rhythms that failed as diagnostic tests, rhythms that may or may not clean a diseased brain, and a grand rhythmic theory of disease still awaiting confirmation.

The question underneath all of it is the oldest and hardest in the field: do brain waves do anything?

This chapter states the strongest case on each side, explains the one experimental logic capable of deciding it, and then delivers, rhythm by rhythm, the most even-handed verdict this book can manage.

31.1 The framing

Arguments go better when the two positions are stated at full strength, so here they are.

Neither is a straw man; each is held, in some form, by serious and accomplished scientists.

31.1.1 By-product against mechanism, the exhaust and the engine

The word epiphenomenon means a side effect that accompanies a process without influencing it.

The classic analogy, used throughout this debate and throughout this book: an engine does the work of moving the car, and the exhaust is produced by that work, is perfectly correlated with it, tells a skilled mechanic a great deal about it, and moves nothing.

The exhaust position runs like this.

The brain's real currency is spikes, the brief electrical pulses individual neurons fire, and the patterns of connection that route them.

When millions of neurons near an electrode receive input at overlapping times, their summed currents produce a wave in the recording, so rhythms in the signal are an inevitable bookkeeping consequence of coordinated activity, and it would be a mistake to promote the bookkeeping into a cause.

Sceptics note that the boldest rhythm theories, such as binding-by-synchrony from Chapter 15, drew withering critiques, including Michael Shadlen and J. Anthony Movshon's 1999 argument that the hypothesis was neither computationally necessary nor empirically compelling, and Supratim Ray and John Maunsell's 2010 demonstration that gamma frequency drifts with stimulus contrast, awkward for any scheme that needs a stable carrier.

They can add that even Wolf Singer, the hypothesis's champion, published a substantially revised and more modest position in 2023.

The engine position answers with physics and physiology.

A rhythm is, concretely, a repeated alternation of excitability: whatever else a wave is, neurons at one phase of it are demonstrably more likely to fire than at another, so timing relative to the wave changes which inputs get through, and that is a mechanism, not an accident.

On this side stand the phenomena this book graded highest: rhythmic inhibition from parvalbumin cells measurably improves information transmission, hippocampal replay rides on a specific oscillatory event, and, as reported in the influential eLife review The gamma rhythm as a guardian of brain health, perturbing rhythms sometimes perturbs function.

There is also a modern middle position worth naming: field potentials could carry usable information for the observer, and even exert weak causal influence through the electrical fields themselves, an effect called ephaptic coupling, while still not being the brain's primary computational mechanism, a framing developed in a 2024 Trends in Cognitive Sciences framework paper on processes versus measurements.

31.1.2 The measurement-against-process distinction

That framework paper crystallised a distinction that dissolves much of the shouting, and it is worth a section of its own.

An oscillation in a recording is a measurement: a summary statistic of millions of cells, taken from outside, shaped by the geometry of the tissue and the position of the sensor.

The underlying process is the actual sequence of cellular events: which neurons fired, when, driven by what.

Much of the epiphenomenon debate, the paper argues, is two groups talking past each other: one asserting that the measurement causes nothing, which is close to trivially true, since a squiggle on a screen moves no molecule, and the other asserting that the rhythmic process being measured does causal work, which is an empirical claim that can be true for one rhythm and false for another.

The question do brain waves do anything is therefore ill-posed until it is rewritten as: does the rhythmic coordination of neural activity, of which this wave is the visible trace, causally contribute to this specific function?

Rewritten that way, the question stops being philosophy and becomes an experiment.

31.2 The test

Science settles causal questions by intervention, and the rhythm field has spent two decades converging on what an adequate intervention must look like.

The logic is strict, and most of the field's famous claims have never been put through it.

31.2.1 The logic: perturb the rhythm specifically and show a specific consequence

Correlation, however exquisite, cannot decide this debate, because engine and exhaust are correlated by definition.

The decisive form of evidence is the perturbation test, and it has three requirements, each doing essential work.

First, perturb the rhythm: change its power, frequency, or phase by direct intervention rather than waiting for it to vary on its own.

Second, specifically: the intervention must alter the rhythm without also directly altering the general excitability, firing rate, or health of the circuit, because otherwise any behavioural effect might flow through the side change, the way proving that a car's fan belt matters by removing the whole engine proves nothing about the fan belt.

Third, show a specific consequence: the predicted function, and ideally only that function, must change in the predicted direction, and a matched control perturbation, same energy, wrong frequency or wrong phase, must fail to produce the effect.

The frequency-matched and phase-matched controls are the heart of the logic: if a 40-hertz intervention helps but an 8-hertz intervention of equal strength helps equally, the rhythm as such has not been shown to matter.

The test can run in both directions, disruption and enhancement, and the cleanest programmes run both.

It is a demanding standard, and that is the point: rhythms are so tightly woven into brain activity that anything less rigorous cannot cut between engine and exhaust.

31.2.2 The tools ranked: light-activated proteins strongest, transcranial current weakest

The available tools differ enormously in how close they come to that standard, and ranking them honestly is half the verdict.

At the top sits optogenetics: the technique of installing light-activated proteins, borrowed from algae and other microbes, into chosen neurons so that flashes of light can drive or silence exactly those cells with millisecond precision.

It is the strongest tool because it alone offers cell-type specificity and phase-level timing: an experimenter can pace parvalbumin interneurons at a chosen frequency, or detect a ripple as it starts and abort it within milliseconds, as Gabrielle Girardeau, Michaël Zugaro, and colleagues did in 2009.

Its weaknesses are honest ones: it requires genetic modification and implanted hardware, so it is essentially confined to animals, and driving cells with light is itself somewhat artificial, so even optogenetic rhythm-pacing must show frequency specificity to be conclusive.

In the middle sit closed-loop electrical and sensory methods: implanted electrodes in patients, as in the Parkinson's chapter, and rhythmic sounds or flashes, which reach humans non-invasively but, as the Alzheimer's dispute showed, may produce trains of evoked responses rather than engage native oscillations.

At the bottom, for causal purposes, sits transcranial alternating current stimulation (tACS), the technique of passing a weak oscillating current between sponge electrodes on the scalp in the hope of nudging brain rhythms into step.

Its problems are now well documented.

Measurements in human cadavers and in living patients with implanted electrodes, published by György Buzsáki's and colleagues' groups around 2017 and 2018, showed that at standard intensities only a fraction of the applied current, producing electric fields of roughly one volt per metre or less, reaches the brain, likely too weak to entrain neurons directly in most people.

Worse, Boateng Asamoah, Myles Mc Laughlin, and colleagues showed in Nature Communications in 2019 that tACS can produce its rhythmic brain effects transcutaneously, meaning through the skin: the current rhythmically stimulates nerves in the scalp, and that peripheral rhythmic input entrains the brain indirectly, so an observed effect need not reflect the intended direct field at all.

Blocking the scalp nerves with anaesthetic reduced the entrainment, which is the experiment that made the confound undeniable.

None of this proves every tACS finding wrong, and the technique is improving through higher intensities and better controls, but it means the hundreds of published tACS studies claiming causal roles for rhythms carry, individually, very little weight in the reckoning that follows.

31.3 The verdict, rhythm by rhythm

Here, then, is the ledger this book has been keeping since its first chapter, settled as honestly as the evidence allows in the mid-2020s.

Three bins: passed a genuine perturbation test, failed or never faced one, and genuinely in between.

31.3.1 What has passed a causal test: the ripple, the parvalbumin gamma, the sleep coupling in part

The cleanest pass belongs to the hippocampal sharp-wave ripple, the brief 110 to 200 cycles per second event during rest and sleep that carries replayed memory sequences.

Girardeau, Zugaro, and colleagues in 2009, and independently Ego-Stengel and Wilson the same year, detected ripples as they occurred in sleeping rats and cut each one short with a precisely timed electrical pulse, leaving sleep otherwise intact, and the rats' memory for a recently learned spatial task was selectively impaired.

The logic is satisfied: a specific event was specifically perturbed, a specific predicted function suffered, and control stimulation outside ripples did not produce the deficit.

Later enhancement work, including Fernández-Ruiz and colleagues in 2019 showing that prolonging ripples improves memory, closed the loop in the other direction.

The second pass belongs to parvalbumin-paced gamma at the local-circuit scale.

Cardin, Moore, and colleagues in 2009 drove parvalbumin interneurons optogenetically across a range of frequencies and found the cortical circuit resonated selectively in the gamma band, and Sohal, Deisseroth, and colleagues in 2009 showed that this parvalbumin-driven gamma measurably enhanced information transmission through the circuit, with frequency specificity as the logic demands.

This establishes that gamma-band inhibitory timing is a real mechanism the cortex uses to structure local processing; it deliberately does not establish the grander binding-across-regions story.

The partial pass goes to the sleep rhythms of consolidation: the coupling of slow oscillations, spindles, and ripples.

Marshall, Born, and colleagues in 2006 boosted slow oscillations in sleeping humans with slow transcranial currents and improved overnight memory, a finding with mixed later replications; stronger evidence came from closed-loop experiments, such as Maingret and colleagues in 2016, in which artificially tightening the coupling between cortical slow waves, spindles, and hippocampal ripples in rodents improved memory, and from auditory closed-loop stimulation of human slow oscillations by Ngo, Born, and colleagues in 2013, which improved recall in the original study though later replications have again been mixed.

In part is the honest phrase: the rodent closed-loop work is strong, the human work is suggestive and inconsistent, and the field is still separating which element of the coupled cascade does the causal work.

31.3.2 What has not: fixed-frequency binding, much long-range coherence, much transcranial entrainment

The clearest failure is binding-by-synchrony in its strong, fixed-frequency form: the proposal that the brain marks features as belonging to one object by making their neurons oscillate in precise gamma synchrony.

It has not passed a perturbation test, and its correlational base absorbed three documented blows: Ray and Maunsell's 2010 finding that gamma frequency shifts with local stimulus contrast, so that two parts of one object can oscillate at different frequencies, making a shared carrier implausible; Shadlen and Movshon's 1999 computational and empirical critique; and the conduction-delay problem, the fact that signals take variable milliseconds to travel between regions, which makes precise phase alignment across distance hard to achieve and hard even to measure, since non-sinusoidal wave shapes, waves that are not smooth curves, generate spurious synchrony and spurious cross-frequency coupling in analyses.

Singer's own 2023 reformulation, which relocates the hypothesis's ambitions to flexible dynamic coordination rather than a fixed binding code, is the field's quiet acknowledgement of where the strong version stands.

Much long-range coherence research shares the same status: communication-through-coherence, Pascal Fries's influential proposal that regions exchange information by aligning their excitability cycles, retains supportive correlational evidence, including elegant attention studies, but specific causal tests at the long-range scale remain scarce, and every measurement is shadowed by the conduction-delay and volume-conduction objections, volume conduction being the spread of one source's signal to distant sensors, masquerading as coordination.

And much transcranial entrainment simply inherits the weakness of its tool: given the weak-field measurements and the peripheral-nerve confound described above, the many reports that tACS at alpha improves this or gamma improves that cannot, as a class, bear causal weight until replicated with the modern controls, and those replications have so far been sobering more often than vindicating.

Not proven, it must be said plainly, is not disproven: absence of a passed test leaves these hypotheses alive, but a quarter century is long enough that the burden of proof now sits firmly with the proponents.

31.3.3 The intermediate cases: alpha gating and phase precession

Two phenomena refuse to fit either bin, and their refusal is instructive.

Alpha gating is the proposal, associated with Ole Jensen, Wolfgang Klimesch, and others, that the 8 to 12 cycles per second alpha rhythm acts as an active brake, rising over cortical regions that should be ignored and thereby gating information flow, with perception waxing and waning along each alpha cycle.

The correlational evidence is abundant and replicated: alpha reliably rises over to-be-ignored regions, and pre-stimulus alpha power and phase predict whether a faint stimulus will be seen.

The causal record is genuinely mixed: some rhythmic sensory and magnetic stimulation studies report frequency-specific effects on perception consistent with a causal gate, others fail, the tACS branch of the evidence carries the confounds just described, and a persistent alternative reading holds that alpha is a consequence of attention shifting elsewhere rather than the mechanism that enforces it.

A fair judge says: too well replicated to dismiss as exhaust, not yet specifically perturbed cleanly enough to certify as engine.

Phase precession, discovered by John O'Keefe and Michael Recce in 1993, is the finding that as a rat crosses a place cell's patch of territory, the cell fires at progressively earlier phases of the hippocampal theta rhythm, so that position along a path is encoded in timing relative to the wave, and sequences of places are compressed into single theta cycles in a way ideally suited to teaching downstream synapses.

It is among the most beautiful and most replicated timing phenomena in neuroscience, has now been observed in human recordings, and is computation-shaped on its face: the phase carries information that firing rate alone does not.

Yet its causal status remains unresolved, because the perturbation test is nearly impossible to run cleanly: one cannot abolish phase precession without disturbing the theta rhythm and the hippocampal machinery around it, so whether downstream circuits actually read the phase code, or whether the precession is a gorgeous by-product of how sequences are generated, is still argued, notably by researchers who, like György Buzsáki, take rhythms seriously and still dispute which side of the line this phenomenon falls on.

So the chapter ends where an honest book must: not with a winner, but with a scoreboard.

A small number of rhythms, the ripple above all and parvalbumin gamma beside it, have passed the hardest test science knows how to run, and no fair reader can call them exhaust.

A larger number of famous claims have either failed the test or never faced it, and no fair reader can call them established.

The brain is neither a symphony nor a smokestack; it is a machine that has been caught, at least twice, using its waves to do real work, and caught many more times simply humming while it works.

The remaining chapters of this Part examine why the causal claims are so hard to make stick, and what the konnectome should build in light of a scoreboard this mixed.

CHAPTER 32. WHY THE CAUSAL CLAIMS ARE FRAGILE (CONTEXT)

Before we celebrate any brain rhythm as a cause of thinking, we owe ourselves a hard look at how such claims can go wrong.
