A brain rhythm is a repeating rise and fall of electrical activity produced by large groups of neurons - the brain's individual signalling cells - working in loose unison.

Scientists measure these rhythms with tools such as electroencephalography (EEG), which records tiny voltage changes through sensors resting on the scalp.

The trouble is that seeing a rhythm alongside a behaviour proves only correlation, meaning the two things happen together, not that one causes the other.

To prove cause, you must reach in and change the rhythm itself, and only the rhythm, and watch the behaviour change.

This chapter explains why that reach-in step is technically fragile, so that the causal-evidence grades used throughout this book - COMPUTATION for a rhythm proven to do work, SUBSTRATE for a by-product, CONTESTED for evidence undermined by a named objection - rest on honest ground.

32.1 The transcranial-stimulation confounds

The most popular way to perturb a human brain rhythm without surgery is transcranial alternating current stimulation (tACS), which passes a weak oscillating electrical current through electrodes stuck to the scalp.

The hope is that this outside rhythm will entrain the brain's inner rhythms, meaning it will pull them into step the way a metronome can pull a roomful of clapping hands into a shared beat.

Three problems, each documented in named studies, make many tACS results fragile.

32.1.1 The field too weak to entrain

The skull and scalp are poor conductors, so most of the applied current never reaches the brain.

Mihály Vöröslakos, György Buzsáki, and colleagues showed in 2018, using measurements inside the heads of human cadavers and live rats, that conventional tACS delivers an electric field of only about 1 volt per metre or less at the brain surface.

An electric field is simply the push a charge feels, and a volt per metre is the unit used to say how strong that push is across a given distance.

Their measurements in living animals suggested that fields need to be several times stronger, on the order of 4 to 5 volts per metre or more with conventional montages, to reliably shift the timing of neurons' firing, which led to their memorable estimate that much of the applied scalp current is lost before it arrives.

Defenders of tACS answer with laboratory-dish work by Davide Reato and colleagues in 2010 showing that fields as weak as 0.2 to 0.5 volts per metre can nudge the timing of an already-oscillating network, because a rhythm that is poised at its tipping point needs only a whisper to be steered.

Later work in awake monkeys, including studies by Matthew Krause, Christopher Pack, and colleagues published in 2019 in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, found that tACS at realistic intensities can indeed entrain the firing of single neurons in the living primate brain, and a 2020 follow-up in Science Advances found the effect grows with dose.

So the field-strength question is not settled in either direction, and that is exactly the point.

When a human tACS study claims to have changed memory or attention by entraining a rhythm, the reader cannot be certain the current ever moved a single neuron, and that uncertainty alone justifies caution.

32.1.2 The peripheral-nerve and retinal confounds

Even if the field inside the skull is too weak to matter, tACS still stimulates something - the skin.

Boateng Asamoah, Ahmad Khatoun, and Myles Mc Laughlin showed in 2019, in a paper in Nature Communications, that the rhythmic effects of tACS on the motor system can be produced by stimulation of peripheral nerves in the scalp skin, which then send rhythmic signals into the brain through ordinary sensory pathways.

A peripheral nerve is any nerve outside the brain and spinal cord, such as the nerves that carry touch and tingling sensations from your skin.

In their experiments, numbing the scalp with a local anaesthetic reduced the supposed brain-entrainment effect, while stimulating the skin alone, without any current path through the skull, reproduced it.

That is a devastating alternative explanation, because it means a tACS experiment can produce a genuine rhythmic change in the brain by an indirect sensory route rather than by the advertised direct entrainment of a cortical rhythm.

A related confound involves the retina, the light-sensing sheet at the back of the eye, which is exquisitely sensitive to electrical current.

Stimulation near the head can produce phosphenes, which are flickering ghost-lights seen without any light entering the eye, and a flicker at 10 cycles per second is itself a rhythmic sensory stimulus that can entrain visual-brain rhythms the ordinary way.

Participants who see flicker or feel skin tingling in the real condition but not the placebo condition are also no longer truly blinded, which contaminates the comparison.

32.1.3 The reproducibility problem

Beyond these physical confounds sits a plainer problem, which is that many tACS and related stimulation findings have simply been hard to repeat.

Effects are often small, sample sizes are often a few dozen people, stimulation montages and frequencies differ from laboratory to laboratory, and individual skulls and brains differ enough that the same current produces different fields in different heads.

Meta-analyses, which are studies that pool the results of many earlier studies, have repeatedly found mixed pictures for tACS effects on working memory and attention, with some pooled effects small but present and others vanishing when only the best-controlled studies are counted.

The honest summary, and the one this book adopts, is that tACS is a promising but unproven instrument, and any causal claim about a human brain rhythm that rests on tACS alone inherits that fragility.

This is why the clean COMPUTATION grades in this book come mainly from invasive animal work, such as optogenetics, a technique that uses light to switch genetically modified neurons on and off with millisecond precision, where the experimenter truly controls the rhythm at its source.

32.2 The recurring technical spine

Three technical objections recur so often in this book that they deserve to be gathered in one place, like a toolkit the reader can carry into every later chapter.

32.2.1 Frequency drift, phase-alignment across delays, and spurious coupling, gathered in one place

The first objection is frequency drift.

Gamma rhythms, the fast brain rhythms between roughly 30 and 80 cycles per second that were proposed to bind the features of an object together, turn out not to sit at one fixed frequency.

Supratim Ray and John Maunsell showed in 2010, recording from monkey visual cortex, that the gamma frequency shifts with the contrast of the visual stimulus, and that even nearby patches of cortex viewing different contrasts oscillate at different gamma frequencies.

Two oscillators at different frequencies cannot stay phase-aligned, the way two runners at different speeds cannot stay shoulder to shoulder, so a code that depends on stable gamma synchrony across regions is in trouble whenever the stimulus itself moves the frequency.

The second objection is phase alignment across conduction delays.

Signals between brain regions travel along axons, the cable-like output wires of neurons, at finite speeds, taking several to tens of milliseconds to cross the brain.

A gamma cycle at 40 cycles per second lasts only 25 milliseconds, so a delay of even 5 to 10 milliseconds is a large fraction of a cycle, and what counts as "in phase" at the sender may arrive badly out of phase at the receiver.

Any theory that requires precise phase relationships between distant regions must explain how the brain compensates for delays that differ from pathway to pathway, and most versions do not.

The third objection is spurious cross-frequency coupling from non-sinusoidal wave shapes.

Real brain rhythms are rarely smooth sine waves; they are often sawtooth-shaped or arch-shaped, like the hippocampal theta rhythm.

Mathematics decomposes any sharp-edged wave into a fundamental frequency plus harmonics, which are ghost rhythms at multiples of the true frequency, and those harmonics are automatically phase-locked to the fundamental.

Analyses that report coupling between a slow rhythm's phase and a fast rhythm's strength can therefore be fooled, detecting only the sharp shape of a single rhythm rather than a genuine conversation between two rhythms, a caution developed in detail by Scott Cole and Bradley Voytek in their 2017 work on brain-waveform shape.

Whenever a later chapter grades a claim CONTESTED, one or more of these three objections is usually the reason, and the reader now owns all three.



================================================================================

PART THIRTEEN. THE COMPUTATIONAL TRANSLATION

================================================================================

CHAPTER 33. OSCILLATIONS AS COMPUTATION IN THEORY (COMPUTATION)

Having armed ourselves with skepticism, we can now ask the constructive question: if brain rhythms do compute, what exactly would they be computing?

This chapter collects the main theoretical proposals, which fall into three families: rhythms as codes that carry information, rhythms as routers that direct traffic, and rhythms as gain controls that set sensitivity.

Each proposal is stated with its best supporting evidence, because Part Thirteen is where this book turns from grading biology to harvesting ideas for engineering.

33.1 The coding arguments

A neural code is any rule by which the activity of neurons stands for information about the world, the way patterns of dots stand for letters in Braille.

The oldest code is the rate code, in which a neuron says "more" by firing more spikes per second.

The proposals in this section claim that the timing of a spike relative to an ongoing rhythm carries additional information that the rate alone does not.

33.1.1 Phase-of-firing codes carrying information beyond rate

The flagship example is theta phase precession in the hippocampus, the seahorse-shaped structure deep in the brain that builds maps of space and stores new memories.

John O'Keefe and Michael Recce reported in 1993 that as a rat runs through the patch of space where a particular place cell fires - a place cell being a hippocampal neuron that activates only in one location - the cell's spikes arrive at progressively earlier phases of each successive cycle of the theta rhythm, the 4 to 12 cycle per second rhythm that dominates the rat hippocampus during movement.

The phase of firing therefore encodes how far through the place field the animal has travelled, which is information the firing rate alone does not carry, and later decoding studies confirmed that reading phase improves position estimates beyond what rate gives.

Phase coding is not confined to the hippocampus.

Work by Christoph Kayser, Nikos Logothetis, and colleagues in 2009 showed that in monkey auditory cortex, the phase of slow rhythms at which spikes occur carries information about natural sounds beyond the spike count.

The engineering translation is direct: a phase-of-firing code is a way of attaching a timestamp to a message at no extra bandwidth cost, because the message is the spike and the timestamp is its position within the shared cycle.

33.1.2 The discrete-sampling view of perception and attention

A second coding proposal claims that perception itself is not continuous like a film but discrete like a flip-book, with each frame corresponding to one cycle of a brain rhythm.

Rufin VanRullen, working in Toulouse, assembled much of the modern evidence, distinguishing a sensory sampling rhythm near the alpha band at roughly 10 cycles per second from an attentional sampling rhythm near the theta band at roughly 7 cycles per second.

Supporting findings include the demonstration by Niko Busch, Julien Dubois, and VanRullen in 2009, and independently by Kyle Mathewson and colleagues the same year, that whether a faint flash is consciously seen depends on the phase of the ongoing alpha rhythm at the moment the flash arrives.

Other work showed that when attention monitors several locations at once, detection ability rotates among them rhythmically, as if a spotlight were sweeping in circles several times per second, with influential studies by Laura Dugué, Ian Fiebelkorn, Sabine Kastner, and colleagues through the 2010s.

The effect sizes in these experiments are modest, typically shifting detection probability by around 10 percent between best and worst phase, and some large replication efforts have found weaker or inconsistent phase effects, so the discrete-sampling view remains actively debated rather than settled.

The theoretical prize, however, is large: a brain that samples discretely has, in engineering terms, a frame clock, and a frame clock is a familiar and powerful design element.

33.2 The routing and binding arguments

The second family of proposals says rhythms decide not what neurons say but who gets to talk to whom, turning oscillation into a routing fabric.

33.2.1 Communication through coherence as timing-gated routing

Pascal Fries proposed communication through coherence (CTC) in 2005 and refined it in 2015, and it is the most cited routing theory in systems neuroscience.

The idea rests on the fact that an oscillation in a receiving group of neurons rhythmically alternates windows of high excitability, when input is welcomed, with windows of low excitability, when input is ignored.

If a sending group times its output spikes to land inside the receiver's welcoming windows, its messages get through, and if its spikes land in the closed windows, the same messages are effectively muted.

Coherence - meaning a stable phase relationship between the sender's rhythm and the receiver's rhythm - therefore acts as a routing switch, selecting one conversation among many possible ones without changing any wiring.

Supporting evidence includes work by Thilo Womelsdorf, Fries, and colleagues in 2007 showing that the strength of interaction between neural groups depends on their momentary phase relation, and work by Georgia Gregoriou, Robert Desimone, and colleagues in 2009 showing that attention strengthens gamma-band coherence between frontal and visual areas.

The objections from Chapter 32 apply with full force - gamma frequency drifts with the stimulus, and conduction delays make cross-regional phase alignment hard - and CTC's grade in this book reflects that tension: a beautiful mechanism with solid supporting correlations and an unfinished causal case.

33.2.2 Multiplexing several codes on one channel by frequency and phase

Multiplexing is the engineering trick of sending several independent messages through one shared channel, the way many radio stations share one antenna by broadcasting at different frequencies.

Theoretical and experimental work suggests brains do something similar.

In the hippocampus, different gamma sub-bands appear to arrive at different phases of the theta cycle, with slower gamma near 25 to 55 cycles per second linked to input from area CA3 carrying stored memories and faster gamma near 60 to 100 cycles per second linked to input from the entorhinal cortex carrying current sensory information, a division reported by Laura Colgin, Edvard Moser, and colleagues in 2009.

If that picture is right, one anatomical cable carries two logical channels, separated by frequency and scheduled by theta phase, which is frequency-division and time-division multiplexing at once.

The same caution applies as always: non-sinusoidal theta can manufacture spurious gamma-band structure, and the crispness of the two-gamma story has been debated since, so the multiplexing claim is a strong hypothesis rather than a closed case.

33.3 The gain and reference arguments

The third family of proposals gives rhythms the humbler but still computational job of setting sensitivity and providing a shared reference for timing.

33.3.1 Phase setting momentary gain

Gain, in engineering, is the volume knob of a system: the same input produces a bigger or smaller output depending on the gain setting.

Because an oscillation is a rhythmic swing of a neural population between more excitable and less excitable states, phase is a gain knob that turns itself up and down many times per second.

Evidence includes the alpha-phase detection studies already described, and invasive recordings showing that the response of cortical neurons to identical stimuli varies systematically with the phase of ongoing slow rhythms at stimulus arrival, a line of work developed by Charles Schroeder and Peter Lakatos, who framed slow-rhythm phase as an instrument of active sensing.

Schroeder and Lakatos showed in 2008 and 2009 that when rhythmic streams of stimuli are attended, slow cortical rhythms shift their phase so that the excitable phase aligns with the expected arrival times of relevant events, effectively pointing the gain peaks at the future.

33.3.2 Phase resetting as a coordination signal

A phase reset happens when a sudden event - a flash, a sound, a decision - snaps an ongoing oscillation to a particular phase, the way a conductor's downbeat restarts an orchestra together.

The computational value is that a reset creates a shared time zero, a reference moment from which subsequent processing in many regions can be measured and coordinated.

After a reset, a phase-of-firing code becomes readable across regions, because every region agrees on where the cycle starts.

Evidence for stimulus-driven and attention-driven phase resets appears throughout sensory cortex, but a standing objection is that an apparent phase reset in an averaged recording can be mimicked by a simple added evoked response - a fresh burst of activity triggered by the event - riding on top of unchanged ongoing rhythms, a discrimination problem analysed carefully by Ali Mazaheri and Ole Jensen in 2006 and by others since.

Distinguishing a genuine reset of an ongoing clock from a superimposed transient remains one of the recurring measurement disputes of the field, and this book flags it wherever a reset claim carries weight.


CHAPTER 34. OSCILLATIONS IN ARTIFICIAL NEURAL NETWORKS (COMPUTATION)

The strongest evidence that oscillation can compute would be a machine that computes with oscillation, and such machines exist.

An artificial neural network is a computer program made of simple units, loosely inspired by neurons, that learn tasks by adjusting the strengths of their connections.

Ordinary artificial networks have no rhythms at all - each unit outputs a single number - so every system in this chapter had to add oscillation deliberately, and the interesting question is what the addition bought.

34.1 The classic synchrony networks

The first wave of oscillatory machines came directly out of neuroscience's binding debate in the 1980s and 1990s, and their goal was perceptual grouping: deciding which parts of an image belong to the same object.

34.1.1 Locally excitatory globally inhibitory oscillator networks for segmentation

The landmark system is LEGION, the Locally Excitatory Globally Inhibitory Oscillator Network, introduced by DeLiang Wang and David Terman in 1995 in IEEE Transactions on Neural Networks, with the mathematical analysis extended in Neural Computation in 1997.

Each picture element, or pixel, of an image is assigned a relaxation oscillator, a simple two-variable system that repeatedly charges up slowly and discharges quickly, like a dripping tap.

Neighbouring oscillators whose pixels have similar brightness are coupled excitatorily, meaning each pulls its neighbours toward firing with it, while a single global inhibitor - one unit connected to everything - pushes all oscillators apart.

The result, which Wang and Terman proved analytically through their selective-gating mechanism, is that oscillators belonging to one connected object rapidly synchronise with each other, while different objects settle into different phases of the shared cycle.

Read the network at one instant and you see one object lit up alone; wait a fraction of a cycle and the next object takes its turn.

Segmentation is thereby computed by time itself, with each object occupying its own time slot, and no separate labelling machinery is needed.

LEGION was applied through the late 1990s and 2000s to real medical and range images, and it remains the clearest existence proof that phase can serve as an object label.

Its historical fate is also instructive: convolutional networks trained on large datasets eventually outperformed it on raw segmentation accuracy, and LEGION survives today mainly as an influence, studied in the oscillatory-network revival described below, rather than as a deployed tool.

34.1.2 Phase labels for binding in early models

LEGION was the most complete member of a broader family.

Christoph von der Malsburg's correlation theory of brain function, circulated as an internal Max Planck Institute report in 1981, first proposed that the brain binds features into objects by the temporal correlation of their neurons' firing, and his 1986 work with Christian Schneider demonstrated a model that segregated mixed sounds by synchrony.

Through the 1990s, researchers including Reinhard Eckhorn, Peter König, Thomas Schillen, and others built simulations in which units representing the red of an apple and the round of an apple fired in synchrony, while units representing a different object fired at another phase, directly implementing binding by synchrony in software.

These models worked on small problems and made the theory concrete, but they shared a limitation that matters for the konnectome build: the number of objects that can be held apart is limited by how many distinguishable phase slots fit into one cycle given timing noise, an issue known as the capacity problem, with practical estimates in such models of roughly a handful of simultaneously separable objects.

That small capacity echoes, intriguingly, the small capacity of human working memory, and Chapter 33's multiplexing story returns here as the same mathematics.

34.2 The modern oscillatory networks

After two decades in which oscillation nearly vanished from machine learning, it returned in the 2020s through an unexpected door: the mathematics of stable training.

34.2.1 Oscillatory recurrent networks and the bounded-gradient result

A recurrent neural network (RNN) is a network with loops, so that its activity at one moment feeds back to influence the next moment, giving it a memory of the past.

RNNs are notoriously hard to train over long time spans because of the exploding and vanishing gradient problem: the learning signal, called a gradient, is multiplied through every time step, and repeated multiplication either blows up to uselessness or shrinks to nothing.

T. Konstantin Rusch and Siddhartha Mishra, working at the Swiss Federal Institute of Technology in Zurich, introduced the coupled oscillatory recurrent network (coRNN) at the International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR) in 2021, presented as an oral, building the network as a time-discretisation of second-order differential equations describing coupled damped oscillators, the same mathematics that describes weights on springs.

Because an oscillator's state is bounded - a swing can only swing so far - Rusch and Mishra proved precise mathematical bounds on the gradients of the hidden states, mitigating both explosion and vanishing, and coRNN matched or beat the standard long short-term memory (LSTM) architecture on benchmarks requiring memory over thousands of steps.

Their 2021 follow-up UnICORNN, an undamped independent controlled oscillatory RNN presented at the International Conference on Machine Learning, extended the result to sequences tens of thousands of steps long.

The lesson for this book is precise and surprising: the first rigorous payoff of oscillation in modern deep learning was not binding or routing but stability, the guarantee that information and learning signals survive long journeys through time - a genuine computational virtue of rhythm that neuroscience had barely articulated.

34.2.2 Kuramoto-based neurons and synchronisation-based deep learning

The Kuramoto model, published by the Japanese physicist Yoshiki Kuramoto in 1975, is the standard mathematical description of how coupled oscillators - flashing fireflies, swaying bridges, pacemaker cells - fall into synchrony, with each oscillator described only by its phase and pulled toward the phases of its neighbours.

Takeru Miyato, Sindy Löwe, Andreas Geiger, and Max Welling introduced Artificial Kuramoto Oscillatory Neurons (AKOrN) at ICLR 2025, where it was selected as an oral presentation, replacing each static unit in a deep network with an oscillator whose state is a rotating vector updated by a generalised Kuramoto rule.

Neurons that synchronise thereby bind together dynamically, and the authors reported benefits across unsupervised object discovery, robustness to adversarial attacks - inputs deliberately crafted to fool a network - calibrated uncertainty, and even constraint reasoning, where an AKOrN network solving Sudoku puzzles improved on out-of-distribution boards from 18 percent toward 90 percent accuracy when its oscillator dynamics were run longer and combined with energy-based voting at test time.

As of 2026 the code is public in the autonomousvision repository, follow-up work is extending the idea, and AKOrN stands as the most direct modern test of the claim that synchrony itself, not just oscillation, can be a working computational primitive in deep learning - a young result, one lineage of papers, and not yet an industry standard, which is the honest current status.

34.2.3 Rotating features and complex-valued binding for object discovery

A parallel line reached binding through complex numbers rather than explicit dynamics.

A complex number can be pictured as an arrow with a length and an angle, so a complex-valued neural activation can store two things at once: the strength of a feature in the arrow's length and a grouping tag in its angle, which is mathematically a phase.

Sindy Löwe, Phillip Lippe, and Max Welling built the Complex AutoEncoder in 2022 on exactly this principle, showing on simple images that features belonging to the same object learn to align their phases, with no object labels ever provided.

Löwe, Lippe, Francesco Locatello, and Welling then generalised the idea to Rotating Features - arrows in more than two dimensions - in a paper presented orally at the Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) in 2023, scaling continuous, distributed object binding from toy images to real-world images by applying the mechanism on top of features from pretrained vision networks.

This line matters for the konnectome argument because it shows the phase-tag idea from von der Malsburg's 1981 theory surviving translation into fully modern, gradient-trained deep learning, while also showing its competition: slot-based architectures, which bind by assigning objects to discrete memory slots - an explicit pointer-like mechanism - remain the mainstream approach to object discovery, and the field has not declared a winner.

34.3 The neuromorphic and spiking line

The third line abandons conventional deep learning and builds hardware and software from spikes, the brain's native currency.

34.3.1 Spike-timing codes, polychronisation, and oscillatory reservoirs

A spiking neural network communicates through discrete pulses in time rather than continuous numbers, and neuromorphic engineering builds chips - such as International Business Machines' TrueNorth in 2014 and Intel's Loihi in 2018 and Loihi 2 in 2021 - whose circuits emit and receive such pulses natively.

In these systems timing is not an analysis afterthought but the medium itself.

Eugene Izhikevich's polychronisation paper, published in Neural Computation in 2006, showed that when a simulated cortical network includes realistic axonal conduction delays and spike-timing-dependent plasticity - a learning rule that strengthens connections when one neuron fires just before another - the network spontaneously develops polychronous groups: sets of neurons that fire in reproducible time-locked patterns with millisecond precision, not simultaneously but in fixed relative delays, like a melody rather than a chord.

Because the number of such time-locked patterns can vastly exceed the number of neurons, Izhikevich argued that delays give a network far more representational room than synchrony alone, a direct upgrade of the phase-label idea into a delay-pattern code.

Hélène Paugam-Moisy, Régis Martinez, and Samy Bengio connected polychronisation to reservoir computing in 2008 in Neurocomputing - a reservoir being a fixed random recurrent network whose rich dynamics are simply read out by a trained output layer - showing that learned delays and polychronous activity improved classification in such systems.

The line remains active: mixed-signal hardware implementations of polychronous networks appeared in Frontiers in Neuroscience in 2014, and 2025 to 2026 preprints on polychronous wave computing and delay-based spiking motif detection continue to develop timing-native computation, though the honest status report is that spiking and oscillatory hardware still trails conventional deep learning on most benchmarks and earns its keep mainly where energy efficiency and event-driven sensing matter.

For konnectome, this line is the proof that delay, phase, and rhythm can be first-class engineering materials rather than biological quirks to abstract away.


CHAPTER 35. THE ENGINEERING VOCABULARY OF A SHARED RHYTHM (COMPUTATION)

Human engineers, facing the problem of coordinating millions of fast components, independently invented almost every trick this book has attributed to brain rhythms, and they wrote the manual.

This chapter teaches that manual from scratch - clocks, clock domains, phase-locked loops, multiplexing, and logical clocks - so that the translation chapters of Part Fourteen can use these words precisely.

No electronics background is assumed; everything is built from the idea of components that must agree on when things happen.

35.1 The clock analogy

Inside every ordinary computer chip is a clock signal: a voltage that flips between low and high at a fixed rate, billions of times per second in a modern processor, where one gigahertz means one billion cycles per second.

The clock does not carry data.

Its only job is to say now - to define the instants at which every storage element on the chip simultaneously captures the value sitting at its input.

35.1.1 The clock signal and synchronous against asynchronous logic

A design that uses such a shared clock is called synchronous logic, and the discipline it buys is enormous.

Between one clock tick and the next, signals may race through the chip's logic gates along paths of different lengths, arriving at different moments, glitching and settling - and none of that matters, because nothing is captured until the next tick, by which time every path has settled.

The clock converts messy continuous time into a clean sequence of discrete steps, which is exactly the service Chapter 33's discrete-sampling theories propose that alpha and theta rhythms perform for perception.

The price is threefold: every path must settle within one clock period, so the slowest path sets the speed of the whole chip; the clock must be distributed to millions of storage elements at nearly the same instant, an engineering problem called clock skew; and driving that clock network consumes a large fraction of the chip's power, which is why designers use clock gating, the practice of switching the clock off to idle regions - a trick this book has already compared to alpha rhythms shutting down unneeded cortex.

The alternative is asynchronous logic, in which there is no clock and each component signals its neighbours directly when its data is ready, using handshakes.

Asynchronous design saves clock power and lets each part run as fast as its data allows, but it is notoriously harder to design and verify, and it has remained a minority practice for decades - a trade-off worth remembering whenever one is tempted to declare either the clocked or the unclocked style obviously superior, in silicon or in brains.

35.1.2 Clock domains, their crossing, and metastability

A large modern chip does not have one clock; it has many.

The processor cores, the memory interface, and the input-output circuits each run at their own frequency, and each region sharing one clock is called a clock domain.

This style even has a name that could describe the brain: globally asynchronous, locally synchronous (GALS), meaning tight rhythmic discipline inside each island and looser, negotiated communication between islands.

The danger lives at the borders.

When a signal generated in one domain is captured by a storage element ticking to a different, unrelated clock, the capture will occasionally happen at the exact moment the signal is changing, and the storage element can then hang for a while in a state that is neither a clean zero nor a clean one.

This condition is called metastability, from the image of a ball balanced perfectly on a hilltop, undecided about which valley to roll into.

Metastability cannot be prevented, only made rare: designers insert synchronizers, typically two storage elements in a row, which give a wobbling signal an extra clock cycle to fall off its hilltop before anyone downstream trusts it, driving the failure rate down to perhaps once in many years.

The neuroscience resonance is direct: brain regions oscillate at their own frequencies, their conversations must cross rhythm boundaries, and Chapter 32's phase-alignment-across-delays objection is precisely the statement that the brain's clock-domain-crossing problem is unsolved in the theories on offer.

Engineering adds the sobering footnote that even in fully designed systems, domain crossing is a leading source of rare, hard-to-reproduce bugs.

35.2 The timing toolkit

Beyond the bare clock, engineers developed instruments for aligning rhythms and for sharing channels, and both have appeared in this book wearing biological costumes.

35.2.1 Phase-locked loops and clock recovery

A phase-locked loop (PLL) is a small feedback circuit that makes one oscillator follow another.

It has three parts: a phase detector that compares the timing of a reference rhythm with the timing of a local, adjustable oscillator; a filter that smooths the comparison; and a control input that speeds the local oscillator up when it lags and slows it down when it leads.

The loop continuously nudges the local oscillator until its phase locks to the reference, after which the two rhythms move as one, and the circuit - first described in essence by Henri de Bellescize in 1932 for radio reception - now sits in essentially every radio, processor, and network interface made.

PLLs earn their keep in two ways that matter here.

First, frequency synthesis: a chip receives one modest crystal reference, and PLLs multiply it up to the gigahertz clocks the cores need, keeping every derived clock locked to the same source.

Second, clock recovery: a serial data link sends only data bits, with no separate clock wire, and the receiver's PLL locks onto the timing of the transitions in the arriving data itself, rebuilding the sender's clock from the message stream so the bits can be read at the right instants.

Clock recovery is the engineering twin of neural entrainment: a listener's brain locking its slow rhythms to the syllable rate of a speaker, as in the speech-tracking work of Chapter 21, is recovering the sender's clock from the signal - and the same dispute applies in both fields, namely whether the receiver truly has a self-sustaining oscillator that locks, or is merely being shaken by each incoming transition, which is the genuine-entrainment-versus-evoked-response argument in silicon terms.

35.2.2 Time-division multiplexing

Time-division multiplexing (TDM) is the sharing of one channel by many users through a rotating schedule of time slots.

The classic telephone example is the T1 line, standardised by Bell Laboratories in the early 1960s, which carried 24 conversations on one wire pair by giving each conversation a recurring slot: the line carries a frame of 24 samples plus one framing bit, repeated 8000 times per second, and each listener's equipment extracts only its own slot.

Two requirements make TDM work, and both have appeared in this book's biology.

The first is a shared frame clock, so that sender and receiver agree on when the frame begins; without frame alignment, every slot is read as its neighbour, and the channel becomes nonsense.

The second is discipline, meaning each user transmits only in its own slot.

The proposed hippocampal arrangement of Chapter 33 - different gamma-band messages arriving at different phases of the theta cycle - is TDM in all but name, with theta as the frame clock and gamma bursts as slot occupants, and the LEGION network of Chapter 34 computes segmentation by spontaneously inventing TDM, giving each object its own slot in the oscillation cycle.

The design test this book applies to every such translation cuts both ways.

TDM shines when the channel is expensive and the traffic is steady and predictable.

But modern computer networks largely abandoned rigid TDM in favour of packet switching, in which each message is a self-contained packet carrying an explicit address and taking whatever transmission moment is free, because explicit addressing wastes no slots on silent users and adapts instantly to bursty traffic.

Wherever konnectome can afford to attach an explicit address or timestamp to every message, the packet beats the time slot, and the wave-like mechanism should be chosen only when messages are cheap, tiny, and relentless enough that per-message addressing is the greater waste.

35.3 The distributed-systems view

The final vocabulary lesson comes from distributed computing, the study of many computers cooperating over a network, because that field confronted the deepest version of the timing problem and solved it without any shared rhythm at all.

35.3.1 The global-clock problem

In a distributed system - thousands of servers in different buildings, or billions of neurons in different brain regions - there is no single clock everyone can see.

Each machine has its own physical clock, and physical clocks drift: cheap quartz oscillators wander apart by milliseconds per day, which is an eternity when events of interest are microseconds apart.

Synchronising them over a network is limited by the very message delays one is trying to measure, since a time report ages unpredictably in transit.

The consequence is fundamental rather than practical: in general one cannot decide, from timestamps alone, which of two events on different machines really happened first, and any design that assumes a perfect shared now is built on sand.

Engineering offers two escapes.

The first is to buy better physics - Google's Spanner database, described in 2012, equips data centres with atomic clocks and satellite time receivers so that global time is known within a small bounded uncertainty, and simply waits out the uncertainty interval before committing work.

The second escape, far more elegant and far cheaper, is to abandon physical time altogether, and it is the subject of the final section.

35.3.2 Logical and vector clocks and causal ordering, the digital alternative to phase

Leslie Lamport's 1978 paper, Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System, published in Communications of the Association for Computing Machinery, is among the most cited papers in computer science, and its central move is a redefinition: what matters is not when events happened but what could have caused what.

Lamport defined the happened-before relation: event A happened before event B if A and B are in the same process with A first, or if A is the sending of a message and B is its receipt, or if a chain of such steps links them.

He then gave each process a logical clock, which is nothing but a counter: increment it at every local event, stamp every outgoing message with its current value, and on receiving a message, jump the counter forward to just past the stamp if the stamp is ahead.

These Lamport timestamps guarantee that if A happened before B, then A's number is smaller than B's - causal order is never violated - at the cost that two unrelated events may still be numbered in either order, giving a partial order, with ties broken arbitrarily when a total order is needed.

Vector clocks, developed by Colin Fidge and Friedemann Mattern independently in 1988, extend the counter to a list with one entry per process, and buy the converse guarantee as well: by comparing two vectors one can tell not only that A preceded B but also when two events were causally unrelated - genuinely concurrent - which a single Lamport counter cannot reveal.

Here, at the end of the vocabulary, stands the sharpest version of the design test that Part Fourteen will apply rhythm by rhythm.

Phase is implicit time: cheap, continuous, carried for free on every signal, but fragile across delays, limited in the number of distinguishable slots, and unable to prove causal order.

The logical clock is explicit time: a few bytes of counter per message, utterly indifferent to transmission delays and drifting oscillators, and mathematically guaranteed to respect causality.

The brain, having no counters, may have been forced to build its coordination from phase; konnectome, having counters in abundance, is not - and every claim in the coming chapters that a brain rhythm should be imitated must first defeat the question of whether a timestamp, a scheduler, or a logical clock does the same job better.


================================================================================

PART FOURTEEN. THE TRANSLATION MAP FOR KONNECTOME

================================================================================

CHAPTER 36. EACH WAVE FUNCTION AS AN ENGINEERING ANALOGUE (COMPUTATION)

This chapter is the translation table that the whole book has been building toward.

In the earlier parts we met the brain's rhythms one by one - a brain wave being a repeating rise and fall in the summed electrical activity of many neurons, the brain's individual signalling cells, firing together - and we graded each claimed function on the causal-evidence ladder of COMPUTATION, SUBSTRATE, or CONTESTED.

Here we take each function that survived that grading, or that at least earned a serious hearing, and restate it in the vocabulary of a systems engineer, because konnectome, the synthetic mind this corpus is building, is an engineered system and can only borrow what can be stated in engineering terms.

The chapter carries the (COMPUTATION) tag because every analogue in it is anchored to a rhythm-function pairing for which perturbation evidence exists, most cleanly the hippocampal sharp-wave ripple, though we will be honest wherever an analogue leans on a pairing that earlier chapters graded CONTESTED.

The rule of the chapter is concreteness: every analogue must name a real mechanism from computing - a router, a buffer slot, a batch job, a mode register, a reset wavefront - and must say exactly which property of the rhythm maps onto which property of the mechanism.

36.1 Routing and gating

The first family of analogues concerns how a message gets from one part of a system to another part, and how a system refuses messages it does not want.

In computing this is the domain of routing, which means choosing the path a message takes, and gating, which means opening or closing a pathway so that a signal either passes or does not.

The brain, remarkably, appears to solve both problems without any addresses at all, using timing where an engineer would use a header field, and that is the translation this section works out.

36.1.1 Communication through coherence as timing-gated channel routing and slot arbitration

Communication through coherence is the hypothesis, stated by Pascal Fries in 2005 and restated with a decade of accumulated evidence in 2015, that two groups of neurons exchange information effectively only when their rhythms are aligned, so that the sending group's bursts arrive during the receiving group's windows of maximum excitability.

To unpack that for the newcomer: neurons that oscillate together are alternately easy and hard to excite, roughly the way a swinging door alternately opens and closes, and a message that arrives when the door is open gets through while the same message arriving a few thousandths of a second later bounces off.

Fries proposed that the brain exploits this by aligning the fast gamma rhythm - roughly 30 to 80 cycles per second - between a sender and its intended receiver, so that of several senders converging on one receiver, only the one whose rhythm is phase-aligned wins the receiver's attention.

The engineering restatement is a timing-gated channel: the physical wire between two modules always exists, but the channel is only effective when the two modules' clocks agree, exactly as in time-division multiplexing, the classic telephone-network technique in which many conversations share one wire by each being assigned a recurring time slot.

The receiver in this picture is performing slot arbitration, meaning the resolution of competition for a shared resource by assignment of time slots: whichever sender lands its volleys in the receiver's open slot owns the channel for that stretch, and attention, in this account, is the process that decides which sender gets the slot.

The supporting evidence includes recordings from awake monkeys by Thilo Womelsdorf, Fries, and colleagues in 2007 showing that the strength of interaction between neuronal groups depends on their phase relation, and work by Andre Bastos, Fries, and colleagues in 2015 showing that feedforward and feedback traffic in the visual system ride on different frequency bands.

The honesty owed here is that earlier chapters graded the strong binding-and-routing reading of gamma as CONTESTED, principally because gamma's frequency drifts with the visual stimulus itself, as Supratim Ray and John Maunsell showed in 2010, which makes it an unsteady carrier, and because conduction delays - the milliseconds a signal spends travelling between regions - make a single shared phase reference hard to maintain across distance.

So the translation to carry forward is not that the brain certainly routes by coherence, but that routing by timing alignment on a shared carrier is a coherent, buildable design, and that it is the design the communication-through-coherence literature says the brain may be using.

36.1.2 Alpha gating as scheduled inhibition, clock gating, and backpressure

The alpha rhythm - roughly 8 to 13 cycles per second, the large slow rhythm first recorded from the human scalp by Hans Berger in 1929 - grows stronger over brain regions that are about to be irrelevant to the task and weaker over regions that are about to be needed.

Ole Jensen and Ali Mazaheri formalised this in 2010 as gating by inhibition: alpha is pulsed inhibition, a suppressive influence delivered in bursts roughly every 100 milliseconds, that functionally switches off the regions where it is strong, so that information is routed by default to wherever alpha is low.

The first engineering analogue is clock gating, the standard power-saving technique in processor design, used since at least the Pentium 4 era, in which the clock signal - the shared square-wave pulse that steps every circuit forward - is simply withheld from blocks of circuitry that have no work to do this cycle, so they neither compute nor consume switching power.

Alpha over an idle cortical region looks strikingly like a gated-off clock domain: the region is not damaged and not disconnected, it is administratively paused, and releasing the alpha is the act of ungating it.

The second analogue is scheduled inhibition as a form of backpressure, which in networking means a downstream stage signalling an upstream stage to slow its sending because the downstream buffers are full: strong alpha imposed on a sensory region by frontal and parietal control areas is, in effect, downstream cognition telling an input channel that it will not be read for a while.

The evidence grade earned earlier applies: the correlation of alpha with suppression is robust across hundreds of studies, and pre-stimulus alpha predicts whether a stimulus will be perceived, but attempts to impose alpha causally with transcranial alternating current stimulation - weak electrical currents applied through the scalp - inherit that method's weak-field and peripheral-nerve confounds, so alpha gating sits at the strong end of CONTESTED rather than at COMPUTATION.

Even so, the analogue is exact enough to build from: an engineered mind can have clock-gated modules and backpressure signals whether or not the brain's alpha turns out to be the same thing.

36.2 Buffering and sequence

The second family of analogues concerns holding several items in mind at once and keeping them in order.

In computing this is the domain of buffers, meaning small regions of memory that hold data temporarily, and of serialisation, meaning the conversion of a set of items into an ordered stream.

The brain's claimed solution nests a fast rhythm inside a slow one and uses the fast cycles as numbered compartments, which is one of the most directly translatable ideas in all of systems neuroscience.

36.2.1 The theta-gamma code as a serialised, slot-indexed working-memory buffer

John Lisman and Marco Idiart proposed in a 1995 paper in Science that working memory - the mental scratchpad that holds a phone number while you dial it - stores its handful of items by replaying them, one item per gamma cycle, within each cycle of the slower theta rhythm, theta running at roughly 4 to 8 cycles per second and gamma at roughly 30 to 80.

Since roughly five to nine gamma cycles fit inside one theta cycle, the scheme predicted a working-memory capacity of about seven items, matching George Miller's famous 1956 estimate of seven plus or minus two, and later human evidence, including intracranial work reviewed by Lisman and Ole Jensen in their 2013 paper The Theta-Gamma Neural Code, and a 2016 study by Andrew Heusser and colleagues in Nature Neuroscience tying episodic sequence memory to theta-gamma phase coding, kept the idea alive and productive.

The engineering restatement is a slot-indexed ring buffer: a ring buffer is a small memory arranged in a circle so that a writer and a reader chase each other around it endlessly, and in the theta-gamma version the theta cycle is one full trip around the ring, each gamma cycle is one slot, and an item's identity is carried by which neurons fire while its position in the sequence is carried by which slot they fire in.

Stated that way, the buffer is serialised - the items are read out one at a time in a fixed order, once per theta cycle - and slot position is an implicit index, meaning the order information costs no extra storage because it is carried by timing rather than by a stored number.

The honest note is that the tidy seven-slots picture is an idealisation: measured gamma is bursty and its frequency drifts, the non-sinusoidal shape of theta can manufacture spurious cross-frequency coupling in the analysis - the third of this book's three recurring objections - and the capacity of human working memory is itself now argued to be nearer four items than seven.

The design idea survives the caveats, because a ring buffer with a timing-carried index is a real and cheap mechanism regardless of how faithfully the hippocampus implements it.

36.2.2 Phase precession as a within-frame encoding of order and fine position

Phase precession is the phenomenon, discovered by John O'Keefe and Michael Recce in 1993, in which a hippocampal place cell - a neuron in the hippocampus, the seahorse-shaped memory structure deep in the temporal lobe, that fires when an animal occupies one particular location - fires at a progressively earlier phase of the theta cycle as the animal moves through that location.

The consequence, confirmed in recordings by William Skaggs, Bruce McNaughton, and colleagues in 1996, is that within every single theta cycle the sequence of places just visited, occupied, and about to be visited is replayed in compressed order, past to future, as a sweep lasting a tenth of a second or so.

The engineering restatement is a within-frame encoding: if the theta cycle is a frame, in the sense of one repeating unit of a transmission, then phase within the frame is a sub-address that carries fine-grained order and position, so a downstream reader recovers not just where the animal is but the local trajectory, ordered, from a single frame.

This is timing used as a number: phase early in the frame means behind me, phase late in the frame means ahead of me, and the resolution is far finer than the firing rate alone could deliver in the same time window.

Phase precession is among the most secure phenomena in this book - it is robustly reproducible, it survives in many task variants, and Eva Pastalkova, Gyorgy Buzsaki, and colleagues showed in 2008 that similar theta sequencing appears even for internally generated sequences - which is why the translation chapters lean on it as the clearest existence proof that a brain really does carry information in phase.

36.3 Consolidation and state

The third family of analogues concerns moving memories from a fast temporary store to a slow durable one, and broadcasting what mode the whole system is in.

In computing these are the domains of write-back - copying data from a fast cache to slower main storage - and of state or mode registers, the small flags that tell every module what regime the machine is operating under.

36.3.1 The slow-oscillation, spindle, and ripple coupling as a scheduled batch write-back between a fast and a slow store

During deep sleep, three rhythms nest inside one another: the neocortical slow oscillation at under one cycle per second, the thalamic sleep spindle at roughly 12 to 15 cycles per second riding on the slow oscillation's active phase, and the hippocampal sharp-wave ripple at roughly 80 to 200 cycles per second nested in turn into the spindle's troughs, a hierarchy laid out in human data by work such as Bernhard Staresina and colleagues in 2015 and reviewed by Staresina in Nature Neuroscience in 2023.

The standard theory of systems consolidation, associated with Gyorgy Buzsaki's two-stage model and with Jan Born's laboratory, holds that during each ripple the hippocampus - the fast, small, quickly writable memory store - replays a compressed version of the day's experience into the neocortex - the slow, vast, durable store - precisely when the slow oscillation has opened the neocortex for writing.

The causal evidence here is the best in the book, which is why the pairing was graded COMPUTATION: Gabrielle Girardeau and colleagues in 2009 and Valerie Ego-Stengel and Matthew Wilson in 2010 showed that selectively cutting ripples short impairs spatial learning, Antonio Fernandez-Ruiz and colleagues in Science in 2019 showed that artificially prolonging ripples improves memory, Lisa Marshall and colleagues in 2006 improved human memory by driving the slow oscillation with weak electrical stimulation at 0.75 cycles per second, and Charles-Francois Latchoumane and colleagues in 2017 showed in mice that spindles boost memory only when driven in phase with the slow oscillation's active state.

The engineering restatement is a scheduled batch write-back: rather than writing every experience to durable storage the moment it happens, the system journals experience cheaply into the fast store all day, then, during a maintenance window when input is switched off - sleep - a scheduler batches the day's entries and copies them to the slow store, with the slow oscillation acting as the write-enable signal, the spindle as the burst clock that times each transfer, and the ripple as the actual data burst.

Every part of that sentence has a computing ancestor: databases batch their transaction logs, caches write back dirty lines when the bus is quiet, and operating systems run compaction jobs at night, so this is the analogue in the book that requires the least translation and enjoys the most causal support.

36.3.2 The global oscillatory state as a mode register carried in the medium

Across the day and night the brain's whole electrical landscape changes character: waking cortex shows low-amplitude, fast, desynchronised activity, deep sleep shows the huge slow oscillation, dreaming sleep shows a waking-like fast pattern, and these global states are switched by broadcast chemicals - neuromodulators such as acetylcholine and noradrenaline - released from small deep-brain nuclei.

The observable rhythm, in other words, tells every region at once which regime the system is in, and regions respond by changing what they do with the same inputs: the same hippocampal circuitry that ripples in quiet rest runs theta during movement, and the switch is visible to any neuron that can feel the field.

The engineering restatement is a mode register carried in the medium: a mode register in a processor is a small store whose bits tell all units what mode to operate in - user mode against supervisor mode, run against sleep - and the brain's version dispenses with the register and the wiring to read it by making the mode legible in the ambient signal itself, the way a factory's workers can tell from the sound of the machinery alone whether it is a production shift or a maintenance shift.

This is an analogue of a SUBSTRATE-graded fact rather than a COMPUTATION-graded one, and that is worth saying plainly: the global rhythm is partly a by-product of the neuromodulatory state rather than the cause of it, but a by-product that reliably encodes the mode is still usable as a signal, and engineers use exactly such incidental signals - supply-voltage levels, carrier tones - as cheap mode broadcasts.

36.3.3 Traveling waves as a propagating reset and context signal across a spatial array

Many brain rhythms are not synchronous flashes but travelling waves: the peak of the oscillation sweeps across the tissue like a stadium wave sweeping around a ground, a fact established for hippocampal theta by Evgueniy Lubenov and Athanassios Siapas in Nature in 2009 and reviewed across cortex by Lyle Muller, Frederic Chavane, John Reynolds, and Terrence Sejnowski in Nature Reviews Neuroscience in 2018.

These waves travel at a few centimetres to a few metres per second, they transiently raise and lower excitability as they pass, and recent human work, including a 2024 study in Nature Human Behaviour by Uma Mohan, Joshua Jacobs, and colleagues, finds that the direction of theta and alpha travelling waves changes with the memory operation being performed.

The engineering restatement is a propagating reset and context signal across a spatial array: in an array of processing elements, one useful primitive is a wavefront reset, a signal that sweeps across the array in a known direction at a known speed, re-initialising each element as it passes and thereby stamping each element with a position-dependent timing offset.

Such a wavefront does two jobs at once: it is a reset, returning elements to a known state in a staggered order, and it is a context signal, because an element can infer its position in the array, and the current direction of global processing, from when and from which direction the wave reached it.

The functional reading of cortical travelling waves is young and should be graded CONTESTED - the waves are indisputably real, but whether their direction and speed are used by the brain or merely reflect axonal conduction delays is still being argued - and the analogue is offered as a design worth testing, not a settled discovery being copied.


CHAPTER 37. WHERE A SYNTHETIC MIND NEEDS NO WAVE (COMPUTATION)

This chapter is the cold shower, and the book would be dishonest without it.

Every function that Chapter 36 translated into an engineering analogue can also be implemented by a plainly digital mechanism - a timestamp, a scheduler, a pointer - and in most of computing history the digital mechanism came first, works well, and is easier to reason about.

A synthetic mind is not obliged to inherit the brain's constraints, so before konnectome adopts any rhythm-like machinery it must look each digital alternative in the eye and admit where the alternative is simply better.

The chapter carries the (COMPUTATION) tag because its subject is precisely the computations of Chapter 36, re-implemented without waves, and its discipline is fairness: each alternative is stated at full strength, not as a straw man.

37.1 The digital alternatives

This section walks through the five main wave functions and, for each, names the explicit mechanism that computing already uses for the same job.

The pattern to watch for is that the wave carries information implicitly, in timing, while the digital alternative carries the same information explicitly, in a tag that travels with the data.

Explicit tags cost bandwidth and storage but are unambiguous, portable across any distance, and inspectable after the fact, and those three virtues are worth a great deal.

37.1.1 Explicit timestamps and sequence identifiers replacing phase

Everything that phase does in the theta-gamma buffer and in phase precession - carrying order, carrying position within a frame - can be done by attaching an explicit timestamp or sequence number to each message.

This is how virtually all distributed computing actually works: Leslie Lamport's 1978 paper Time, Clocks, and the Ordering of Events in a Distributed System showed that a system of simple counters - logical clocks, incremented on every event and carried on every message - suffices to reconstruct a consistent ordering of events across machines that share no physical clock at all.

The Transmission Control Protocol (TCP), the ordering backbone of the internet, numbers every byte it sends, and the receiver reassembles order from the numbers regardless of when the packets arrive, which is a strictly stronger guarantee than phase can offer, because a phase reference degrades with distance and delay while a sequence number is immune to both.

A timestamp is also durable: a memory written with a timestamp can be ordered against other memories years later, whereas information carried only in the live phase relation of two oscillators evaporates the moment the oscillation stops.

The honest cost accounting goes the other way too - a sequence number must be generated, transmitted, stored, and compared, item after item, while phase is generated once for a whole population by a shared oscillation - and Chapter 38 will collect that saving, but as a matter of capability the timestamp does everything phase does and more.

37.1.2 A scheduler or arbiter gating channels without a shared carrier

Everything that coherence gating does - deciding which sender's messages a receiver accepts - is done in engineered systems by a scheduler or an arbiter, a component whose whole job is to grant access to a shared resource according to an explicit policy.

An operating-system scheduler decides which program runs on the processor next; a bus arbiter decides which device may drive the shared wires this cycle; a network switch consults a forwarding table and sends each packet down exactly the port its address demands.

None of these mechanisms needs the sender and receiver to share a carrier rhythm: the arbitration decision is made in one place, on explicit metadata such as priority, address, or queue length, and it is exact - the losing sender is not merely attenuated, as in coherence gating, but cleanly excluded.

Explicit arbitration is also auditable, in that one can log every grant decision and later ask why a message was or was not delivered, a forensic property that a routing scheme diffusely implemented in phase alignment across millions of units cannot easily offer.

The fair statement of the trade is that an arbiter is a centralised component that must scale with the number of contenders and must itself be wired to all of them, while coherence gating is decentralised and rides on wiring that already exists, and konnectome must decide case by case which cost it would rather pay.

37.1.3 Content-addressed attention replacing coherence gating

Modern machine learning has already made this decision once, and it is instructive which way it went.

The attention mechanism of the transformer architecture, introduced by Ashish Vaswani and colleagues in the 2017 paper Attention Is All You Need, routes information by content: every element of the input emits a query vector and a key vector - lists of numbers describing what it wants and what it offers - and information flows from each element to each other element in proportion to how well key matches query.

This is content-addressed routing, the software cousin of a content-addressable memory, and it solves exactly the problem the communication-through-coherence hypothesis assigns to gamma - selecting, among many senders converging on a receiver, the relevant one - without any oscillation, any phase, or any shared carrier at all.

The transformer's success at language, vision, and reasoning is the strongest single piece of evidence in this book that a synthetic mind needs no wave for selective routing, because the selection is computed directly from the content of the messages rather than from their timing.

The costs are also explicit and well known: attention compares everything with everything, at a computational price that grows with the square of the number of elements, and it recomputes the entire routing pattern from scratch at every step, having no cheap standing rhythm to carry a routing decision forward in time - which is precisely the crack through which Chapter 38 will argue rhythm back in.

37.1.4 An explicit batch job replacing a state-triggered consolidation window

Everything the slow-oscillation, spindle, and ripple hierarchy does - deferring durable writes to a protected maintenance window - is done in software by an explicit batch job: a scheduled process, launched by a scheduler such as the venerable Unix cron utility, that runs the write-back when load is low.

Databases have refined this to a science: write-ahead logging journals every transaction immediately to a fast sequential log, and background processes called checkpointers later copy the logged state into the main store, at a moment chosen by explicit policy rather than by the arrival of a global brain state.

The digital version is superior on several counts at once: the window can be scheduled to the millisecond rather than awaited, the job can be verified - one can check after the batch that every record arrived - it can be retried on failure, and it can be run incrementally or in parallel, none of which the sleeping brain can obviously do.

A synthetic mind with a fast store and a slow store therefore does not need sleep, spindles, or ripples to consolidate: it needs a log, a checkpoint process, and a scheduler, all of which are decades-old, thoroughly understood machinery.

What the biological version contributes is not the mechanism but the argument for having one at all - the two-store architecture with deferred, replayed, prioritised write-back is a genuinely good design, graded COMPUTATION on the animal evidence - and konnectome should copy the architecture while feeling free to implement the trigger digitally.

37.1.5 Explicit pointers replacing phase tags for binding

The binding problem is the question of how a mind marks which features belong to which object - the red of the red square and the green of the green circle - when all of them are represented at once.

The binding-by-synchrony hypothesis, advanced by Christoph von der Malsburg in 1981 and by Wolf Singer and Charles Gray from 1989, proposed that features of the same object are bound by firing in synchrony within a gamma cycle, in effect using a shared phase as a temporary group tag.

Software solves the same problem with pointers: a pointer is simply a stored address, and an object is bound to its features by a record that holds explicit references to each of them, a mechanism so basic that every data structure in every program depends on it.

Pointers are exact, unlimited in number, arbitrarily nestable - a pointer can reference a structure that itself holds pointers, giving hierarchy for free - and indefinitely stable, while phase tags offer at most a handful of distinguishable phases per cycle, decay within a cycle or two, and nest only as far as one rhythm fits inside another.

The comparison is made sharper by the fate of the biological hypothesis itself, which this book graded CONTESTED at best: Michael Shadlen and William Newsome and later Shadlen and Anthony Movshon in 1999 argued the synchrony evidence was epiphenomenal, Ray and Maunsell in 2010 showed gamma frequency drifts with stimulus contrast, Bjorn Merker in 2013 pressed the case that gamma marks activation rather than binding, and by 2023 Singer himself had shifted toward treating synchrony as one ingredient in dynamic coordination rather than the binding code - so here the digital alternative is not merely available but is replacing a mechanism the brain may never have used as advertised.

37.2 Where the biology does not map

This section names three respects in which the biological facts, taken seriously, argue against literal transfer to a synthetic substrate.

The theme of all three is that brain rhythms are shaped by constraints - sluggish components, no shared clock line, a tight energy budget - that a machine simply does not have.

Copying an adaptation without the constraint that produced it is cargo-cult engineering, and this book has promised not to do that.

37.2.1 Rhythms as by-products of delay loops rather than designed clocks

No one laid down a clock line in the brain: its rhythms emerge because loops of excitation and inhibition have delays, and any feedback loop with delay tends to oscillate, as every engineer who has fought an unstable control system knows.

Gamma, on the standard pyramidal-interneuron and interneuron-network accounts, is the ringing of a fast loop between excitatory cells and inhibitory interneurons - an interneuron being a locally connecting neuron whose job is to inhibit its neighbours - with the period set by synaptic and membrane delays of a few milliseconds.

Thalamocortical spindles and the slow oscillation likewise fall out of the intrinsic ion-channel properties and loop delays of their circuits, which is why each rhythm sits in the frequency band its hardware imposes rather than at a frequency chosen for the computation.

The engineering lesson cuts against imitation: where a designed system wants a clock it specifies one, chooses its frequency freely, distributes it deliberately, and guarantees its stability, whereas a brain-style emergent oscillation has its frequency dictated by component physics, drifts with temperature, neuromodulation, and load, and was never guaranteed to anything.

This is the deepest sense in which several rhythms earned the SUBSTRATE grade: an oscillation that a circuit cannot help producing is evidence about the circuit, not necessarily a mechanism the computation relies on, and a synthetic mind that wants a rhythm should design one rather than arrange to be accidentally unstable.

37.2.2 The local, ambiguous phase reference against a defined logical clock

For phase to serve as a shared code, everyone using it must agree on where the cycle begins, and the brain has no such agreement.

Measured phase differs across the layers of a single cortical column, theta is a travelling wave along the hippocampus - Lubenov and Siapas measured phase shifts approaching half a cycle from one end to the other - and conduction delays between distant regions are large fractions of a gamma cycle, so the phrase the phase at which the two regions synchronised is, across any real distance, ill defined.

Worse, the second recurring objection of this book lives here: analyses that align phase across regions must first choose a reference, and different defensible choices yield different apparent phase relations, which is part of why so many cross-regional coherence findings resist replication.

Against this stands the defined logical clock of Lamport 1978, whose whole point is that it needs no physical simultaneity at all: order is defined by message causality - if event A could have influenced event B, A's counter is smaller - and the definition is exact at any distance, under any delay, for any number of participants.

A synthetic mind that spans machines, data centres, or merely long wires therefore already possesses, in logical clocks and their descendants such as vector clocks, a strictly cleaner solution to the very problem that makes biological phase ambiguous, and no wave should be adopted for cross-region ordering when a counter does it exactly.

37.2.3 The metabolic and wiring constraints that motivate biological rhythms and may not bind a synthetic substrate

The brain runs on roughly 20 watts, its neurons communicate through slow, expensive chemical synapses, and long-range axons - the wire-like fibres that carry spikes between regions - are so costly in volume and energy that the brain's wiring is famously sparse, with any one neuron connected to only a tiny fraction of the others.

Under those constraints, oscillation is a bargain three times over: rhythmic alternation between active and silent phases saves energy the way duty cycling saves a battery, a shared rhythm coordinates millions of units without any wiring dedicated to coordination, and timing can carry information that would otherwise require extra spikes, each spike costing measurable metabolic energy, a budget quantified in Peter Lennie's 2003 analysis of the cost of cortical computation.

A synthetic substrate faces none of these constraints in the same form: copper and optical links are fast and cheap to drive, broadcast of a small piece of metadata to every module is a solved problem, memory for explicit tags is effectively free at brain-relevant scales, and power, while a real engineering concern, is orders of magnitude less binding than 20 watts.

The inference discipline is the one this corpus applies everywhere: an adaptation is evidence about the constraint that produced it, so where the constraint does not transfer, the adaptation carries no automatic authority.

The chapter's conclusion is therefore plain: for ordering use timestamps, for routing decisions an arbiter or content-addressed attention, for binding use pointers, for consolidation use a scheduled batch job, and adopt nothing wave-like out of reverence - which sets up the final question of whether anything wave-like earns its place on merit.


CHAPTER 38. THE CASE FOR ADOPTING RHYTHM-LIKE MECHANISMS ANYWAY (COMPUTATION)

Chapter 37 established that a synthetic mind can do everything the waves do with timestamps, schedulers, and pointers, and that chapter was written to be unanswerable on capability.

This chapter argues the remaining question, which is not capability but cost: there are regimes - many cheap units, dense local interaction, coordination metadata threatening to swamp payload - where carrying information in a shared rhythm is simply cheaper than tagging everything explicitly.

The argument is made with engineering claims that can be checked, and it ends, as the book promised at the outset, with a design test stated plainly enough to be applied to any konnectome proposal.

The chapter keeps the (COMPUTATION) tag because each saving it claims is anchored to a mechanism from Chapter 36 whose biological version carries real perturbation evidence, and because the machine-learning results it cites are themselves experiments in which adding oscillatory dynamics changed a measured outcome.

38.1 The savings

This section itemises the three concrete economies a rhythm offers an engineered system.

Each is stated as a comparison against the explicit alternative from Chapter 37, because a saving only exists relative to the price of doing it the other way.

The common thread is amortisation: a rhythm is a piece of coordination infrastructure that is paid for once and then used simultaneously by every unit that can feel it.

38.1.1 A shared frame as cheaper coordination metadata than a full address

Consider a million units that must repeatedly agree on ordering and grouping, and count the metadata each scheme requires.

The explicit scheme attaches to every message a sequence number and a group tag, so the metadata cost grows with the number of messages, and at high message rates the tags can rival the payload - a familiar pathology in networking, where small-packet traffic can spend half its bandwidth on headers.

The shared-frame scheme establishes one rhythm and lets timing carry the metadata: an item's slot within the frame is its sequence position, as in the theta-gamma buffer of Section 36.2.1, and co-occurrence within a slot is its group tag, so the marginal metadata cost per message is zero once the rhythm exists.

This is exactly the economics of time-division multiplexing against packet switching, and telephony ran profitably on the time-division side of that trade for half a century: when traffic is regular and participants are known, slots are cheaper than headers, and when traffic is bursty and participants churn, headers win.

The saving is real but conditional, and the condition should be stated as sharply as the saving: a shared frame pays off only where the units are numerous, the interaction is sustained, and the ordering vocabulary needed is small - a handful of slots, not billions of addresses - which is, perhaps not coincidentally, a fair description of a cortical region.

38.1.2 Coherence gating as rewiring-free routing: one graph, many effective circuits

A fixed wiring diagram would seem to imply a fixed computation, and the deepest engineering content of the communication-through-coherence idea is that this is false.

If effective communication requires timing alignment, then the same anatomical graph supports many different effective circuits, selected moment to moment by which node pairs are currently in phase: routing is changed by re-tuning oscillators, which costs milliseconds and no hardware, rather than by rewiring, which biology cannot do quickly at all.

The explicit alternative - reprogramming a routing table or recomputing an attention pattern - is exact but must be computed and distributed by something, whereas phase-based selection is enacted by the same physical dynamics that carry the signals, with no separate control plane to build, power, and protect.

Anna Palmigiano, Theo Geisel, Fred Wolf, and Demian Battaglia showed in a 2017 modelling study in Nature Neuroscience that transient synchrony in oscillating networks yields exactly this: flexible, fast-switching information routing on a fixed graph, with the routing state readable from the phase pattern.
