A signal that tracks age, sleep depth, drug state, arousal, and development is not noise in any meaningful sense.

It is a slow-reporting instrument gauge, and the field had been ignoring it because it did not look like a wave.

55.2 The excitation-inhibition-balance reading

Why would a spectral slope track anything physiological at all?

The most influential answer came from Richard Gao, Erik Peterson, and Bradley Voytek in a 2017 paper in the journal NeuroImage.

Every cortical circuit runs on a balance between excitation, delivered by pyramidal neurons through the transmitter glutamate, and inhibition, delivered by interneurons through GABA, and this ratio, called excitation-inhibition balance, is one of the most important control knobs in the brain.

Excitatory synaptic currents are fast and spiky; inhibitory currents are slower and longer-lasting.

Gao and colleagues showed, first in computational models and then in real recordings from rat hippocampus and macaque cortex, that shifting the mix of these two current types tilts the aperiodic slope in a lawful way: more inhibition relative to excitation steepens the spectrum, and more excitation relative to inhibition flattens it.

The pharmacological checks line up neatly.

Propofol, which boosts GABA inhibition, steepens the slope; ketamine, which acts on the glutamate system, flattens it, as shown in analyses of anesthesia recordings including work building on Muthukumaraswamy and Liley's 2018 study.

A 2023 study in eLife by Ahmed and colleagues extended the reading to deep-brain recordings from the subthalamic nucleus in people with Parkinson's disease, finding that the aperiodic exponent there tracked the excitation-inhibition changes produced by treatment.

The honest caveat, in keeping with this book's discipline, is that the slope is an indirect gauge, not a meter wired to the synapses.

Many things besides synaptic balance can tilt a spectral slope, including the electrode type, the frequency range chosen for the fit, muscle and heart artifacts, and even cardiac contributions to supposedly cortical signals, as a 2025 eLife study on age-related 1/f changes pointed out.

The exponent is best treated the way a doctor treats body temperature: a genuinely informative summary of a hidden process, useful precisely because it is cheap and global, and never to be confused with a direct view of the mechanism.

55.3 Why separating periodic from aperiodic changed the field

The methodological earthquake came in 2020.

Thomas Donoghue, Bradley Voytek, and colleagues published a paper in Nature Neuroscience introducing an algorithm, originally called FOOOF for fitting oscillations and one-over-f, now renamed specparam for spectral parameterization, that decomposes any power spectrum into its aperiodic slope plus genuine periodic peaks sitting above that slope.

The point of the tool is a warning hiding in plain sight.

Suppose a study reports that a patient group has less alpha power than a control group.

The traditional method measures power inside a fixed frequency band, say 8 to 12 cycles per second, but that band contains both any true alpha peak and the slice of aperiodic background underneath it.

If the patient group merely has a flatter or lower aperiodic slope, band power changes even if the alpha rhythm itself is completely unchanged, and the study announces an oscillation finding that is actually a background finding.

The same confusion works in every direction: a shifted peak frequency can masquerade as a power change, and a change in slope can masquerade as a change in fast gamma rhythms, a trap especially relevant to the gamma literature this book graded so cautiously.

Once specparam and related tools spread through the field in the early 2020s, re-analyses showed that a sobering number of published band-power effects, in aging, in attention deficit hyperactivity disorder research, in schizophrenia research, and elsewhere, were partly or wholly aperiodic effects wearing an oscillation costume.

This did not make the old literature worthless, but it did mean that every claim of the form the rhythm changed now carries a burden: show that a true spectral peak exists and that the peak, not the background, is what moved.

For this book's grading project the lesson is structural.

The aperiodic component earns its APPARATUS tag because it is not itself a rhythm doing a computation; it is part of the measuring situation, a property of the canvas that every painted rhythm must be judged against.

Learning to subtract the canvas is one of the quiet reasons the field's causal claims are cleaner in 2026 than they were in 2010.


CHAPTER 56. THE MEASUREMENT-AGAINST-PROCESS PROBLEM IN DEPTH (CONTEXT)

This chapter confronts the question that has been waiting under every page of this book.

When we record a brain wave, are we looking at the machinery of thought, or at the machinery's shadow on the wall?

The distinction sounds philosophical, and it is, but it is also brutally practical: it decides which rhythms are worth building into a synthetic mind and which are merely worth monitoring, and it is the reason this book insists on grading every rhythm as COMPUTATION, SUBSTRATE, or CONTESTED instead of celebrating them all equally.

56.1 When the wave is the process and when it is the shadow

Begin with what a recorded wave physically is.

An electrode in or on the brain measures the local field potential, the summed voltage produced by ionic currents flowing across the membranes of thousands to millions of nearby neurons, dominated by the slow currents at synapses rather than by the spikes themselves, as Gyorgy Buzsaki, Costas Anastassiou, and Christof Koch laid out in an influential 2012 review in Nature Reviews Neuroscience.

The wave, in other words, is an aggregate: a stadium roar assembled from individual voices, recorded by a microphone hanging over the crowd.

Now the key conceptual move, made sharp in a 2024 philosophical analysis by David Silverstein and colleagues titled Decoupling Measurements and Processes: On the Epiphenomenon Debate Surrounding Brain Oscillations in Field Potentials.

The word oscillation is used in the literature for two different things, and sliding between them generates most of the confusion.

Sometimes it means the oscillation-as-measurement: the wiggly line on the screen, the field potential itself.

Sometimes it means the oscillation-as-process: the underlying rhythmic coordination of neuronal excitability, the fact that millions of neurons are being made more and less likely to fire on a shared schedule.

The wiggly line can be a shadow while the rhythmic coordination is the engine, and asking is the brain wave causal without saying which sense you mean is asking an unanswerable question.

So when is the wave the process?

There is one route by which the recorded field itself, the actual voltage in the extracellular fluid, feeds back on neurons: ephaptic coupling, from a Greek word meaning touching, in which the electric field generated by a population of neurons nudges the membrane voltage of neighboring neurons without any synapse.

Experiments by Flavio Frohlich and David McCormick in 2010 and by Anastassiou, Koch, and colleagues in 2011 showed that fields as weak as about half a volt per meter, comparable to the brain's own endogenous fields, can measurably entrain the timing of neuronal firing in brain slices, and later work showed that slow waves can propagate through tissue by this mechanism even when synapses are blocked.

Ephaptic effects are real, but they are gentle, biasing spike timing by milliseconds rather than commanding it, and most researchers treat them as a secondary reinforcing loop rather than the primary channel of neural communication.

For every other function claimed for brain waves, the causal work is done by the process, the synchronized synaptic bombardment and the rhythmic excitability windows it creates, and the recorded wave is the shadow that process throws onto our electrode.

The shadow can still be a superb scientific instrument, exactly as a sundial's shadow is a superb clock, but removing the shadow would not stop the sun.

56.2 The decoupling of measurement from mechanism

Once the two senses are separated, this book's three recurring objections can be seen as one objection wearing three costumes: each is a case where a property of the measurement was mistaken for a property of the mechanism.

First, gamma frequency drift.

Supratim Ray and John Maunsell showed in 2010 that the gamma frequency in visual cortex shifts with stimulus contrast, which is devastating if gamma-the-measurement is supposed to be a fixed carrier frequency for binding, but merely descriptive if gamma-the-process is a local excitatory-inhibitory negotiation whose tempo naturally tracks drive.

Second, phase alignment across regions.

Conduction delays, the milliseconds a signal spends traveling down an axon, the neuron's output cable, mean that a phase relationship measured between two distant electrodes does not straightforwardly reveal the timing relationship experienced by the neurons themselves, so a beautiful coherence number on the screen may correspond to no usable coordination in the tissue, one of the standing objections to strong forms of Pascal Fries's communication-through-coherence hypothesis.

Third, non-sinusoidal wave shape.

Real brain rhythms are sawtooth-shaped and asymmetric rather than smooth sine waves, and Scott Cole and Bradley Voytek showed in 2017 that standard analyses of such shapes mathematically generate spurious cross-frequency coupling, the appearance of a slow rhythm controlling a fast one, out of a single non-sinusoidal process.

In all three cases the fix is the same discipline: state your claim about the process, then ask whether the measurement actually pins that claim down, and accept that the answer is often not yet.

This is also why perturbation experiments carry the crown in this book's grading.

Optogenetics, the technique of making specific neurons controllable with light, lets an experimenter act on the process directly, silencing ripple events or driving parvalbumin interneurons at gamma tempo, and when such an intervention changes memory or perception, the causal claim finally attaches to the mechanism rather than to its shadow.

The rhythms this book graded COMPUTATION, the sharp-wave ripple above all, are precisely the ones where the process, not the wiggly line, has been grabbed and moved.

And the cautionary mirror image is transcranial alternating current stimulation, where a rhythmic current applied to the scalp was long assumed to entrain the process, until measurements in human cadavers and living subjects by Gyorgy Buzsaki's and colleagues' 2018 work showed that the field actually reaching neurons is weak, and control experiments revealed that some behavioral effects rode on stimulation of nerves in the scalp rather than on any change in the brain's oscillation-as-process.

56.3 The implication for a synthetic mind that has no field to measure

Now the question this book exists to ask.

The konnectome project, like any digital cognitive architecture, has no extracellular fluid, no summed ionic currents, and therefore no field potential: if you held an electrode up to it, you would hear nothing.

Does that silence mean it is missing something essential?

The measurement-against-process distinction gives a precise answer in two halves.

To the extent that a brain wave is a shadow, a synthetic mind loses nothing by lacking it, because the shadow never did causal work; it was our window, not the brain's mechanism.

What the synthetic mind must reproduce is the process behind the shadow, stated in implementation-free terms: shared schedules of readiness, so that units that must cooperate are active in the same window, which digital systems achieve with clock signals and time-division multiplexing, the practice of giving different data streams different time slots on a shared line; consistent ordering of events across components, which distributed systems achieve with logical clocks of the kind Leslie Lamport described in 1978, timestamps that guarantee everyone agrees on what happened before what without any shared physical wave; and gated, batched transfer of stored material, the sharp-wave ripple's job, which a database achieves with a scheduler and batch write-back, the practice of accumulating changes in a buffer and committing them in one planned burst.

Each of these is a case where the explicit digital mechanism, the timestamp, the scheduler, the pointer, does not merely imitate the wave-like solution but beats it, because the brain's rhythms are the workaround of a system that has no global clock, no addresses, and no way to send a timestamp, while a digital system has all three.

But the loss of the shadow has a second, subtler consequence, and honesty requires stating it.

The field potential is not only the neuroscientist's window; over evolutionary time it may also have been the brain's own cheap summary of itself, both through the gentle ephaptic feedback described above and through structures that read global state, and it is the signal by which the brain's states can be observed without opening the skull.

A synthetic mind with no field has no free global gauge: if its designers want the equivalent of an electroencephalogram, an at-a-glance readout of arousal, load, and coordination, they must build the instrumentation deliberately, as logging, telemetry, and health metrics.

That is this chapter's closing translation rule for the konnectome build.

Do not simulate the wave; implement the process the wave reveals, and then, separately and explicitly, implement the observability the wave provided for free.

A mind can do without shadows, but no mind, biological or synthetic, can be safely operated without a window.


CHAPTER 57. THE CROSS-SPECIES INVARIANTS IN DEPTH (CONTEXT)

This chapter widens the lens from one brain to all brains, and finds something quietly astonishing.

A mouse brain weighs less than half a gram; a human brain weighs about 1,400 grams; a whale brain can exceed 7,000 grams, a range of more than ten thousand fold.

Yet the rhythms this book has catalogued, from the slow waves of deep sleep to the ripples of memory replay, appear in all of these brains at very nearly the same frequencies, with the same temporal structure and the same behavioral correlates.

When a feature survives that much change in scale, biologists call it an invariant, and invariants are nature's way of underlining what actually matters.

57.1 The conserved frequency ladder

The definitive statement of the invariance is a 2013 review in the journal Neuron by Gyorgy Buzsaki, Nikos Logothetis, and Wolf Singer, titled Scaling Brain Size, Keeping Timing: Evolutionary Preservation of Brain Rhythms.

Their survey of recordings across mammals reached a striking conclusion: every known pattern of local field potential found in the human brain is present in every other mammal examined, and not only the frequency bands but their durations, their temporal evolution, and their links to behavior are conserved.

Slow oscillations of deep sleep sit near 1 cycle per second in mice and in humans; sleep spindles sit near 10 to 15; hippocampal ripples sit near 100 to 200; and the whole set forms a ladder in which each band's center frequency stands in a roughly constant ratio to its neighbors, so that the bands are evenly spaced on a logarithmic scale, the kind of scale on which each step multiplies rather than adds.

The neighboring ratios also help explain why the bands do not blur into each other: rhythms with non-overlapping frequencies can coexist and interact through phase coupling without collapsing into one.

The conservation is not perfectly rigid, and stating the exceptions is part of respecting the invariant.

Hippocampal theta, the exploration rhythm, runs at about 6 to 10 cycles per second in running rats but appears slower and less continuous in humans, often nearer 1 to 4 cycles per second in virtual navigation studies, a discrepancy documented by Joshua Jacobs in a 2014 Philosophical Transactions review; later work, including real-world walking studies by Nanthia Suthana's group in 2017 and a 2020 Nature Communications study by Goyal, Jacobs, and colleagues, found faster theta during actual movement and evidence for two distinct human theta bands, so the human exception may be partly an artifact of studying navigation from an armchair.

An invariant with measured, explainable deviations is more convincing than a perfect one, because it shows the measurement is honest.

57.2 The timing-preservation account

Why should frequencies stay fixed while brains balloon?

The puzzle is sharper than it first appears.

The physical processes that pace brain rhythms, membrane time constants, synaptic decay times, and loop delays, depend on distances and diameters, and a bigger brain means longer axons and longer travel times, so naive scaling predicts that big brains should run slow, the way big church bells ring deeper than small ones.

They do not, and the Buzsaki, Logothetis, and Singer account of why is the timing-preservation hypothesis: evolution treats the temporal structure of neural computation as the thing that must not change, and re-engineers the hardware around it.

The clearest evidence is in the wiring.

As brains grew, most axons stayed thin and slow, but a small minority of axons became disproportionately thick and heavily myelinated, myelin being the fatty insulation that speeds conduction, and the largest brains show the widest range of axon calibers.

These fast highways keep the longest journeys within roughly the same time budget in a human brain as in a mouse brain, so that a rhythm's period still fits the loop it has to close.

The cost is real: fat, insulated axons are metabolically and spatially expensive, which is exactly why their selective deployment reads as evidence that timing was the constraint being defended.

The same logic explains why big brains did not simply slow their rhythms to match their size.

The rhythms are locked to things that do not scale with brain size: the timescales of muscles and sensors, the decay times of synaptic receptors, the pace of events in the physical world, and, as a 2025 PLOS Biology study on animal acoustic communication showed, even the tempo of communicative sounds across species clusters in the same slow delta range, suggesting sender and receiver brains are locked to a shared temporal currency.

A mouse and a human live in the same physics, so their brains keep the same clocks.

57.3 What an invariant across brains says about what a mind needs

The final step is to read the invariant as a message about minds in general, which is why this chapter carries the CONTEXT tag.

When an engineering feature is preserved across a ten-thousand-fold change in scale, at measurable metabolic cost, the parsimonious inference is that the feature sits close to the function, and what mammalian evolution preserved was not any particular anatomy but a temporal architecture: a fixed ladder of nested timescales, from tens of milliseconds to tens of seconds, with defined couplings between rungs.

That suggests a portable requirement, one that should survive translation to substrates evolution never touched.

Any mind operating a body-like interface in the physical world needs a hierarchy of processing timescales matched to the world's own timescales, fast loops for reaction, middle loops for coordination and evidence gathering, slow loops for consolidation and strategy, and it needs principled coupling between the rungs so that fast results can be packaged into slow decisions and slow context can gate fast processing.

For the konnectome build the translation is direct but two-sided, as this book's honesty rule requires.

The wave-like lesson is that the ladder itself matters: a design with explicit nested schedulers running at distinct, non-interfering rates, a fast perception-action cycle, a slower working-memory refresh, a much slower offline consolidation pass, inherits the invariant's logic.

The digital counter-lesson is that the specific frequencies do not matter and need not be copied, because they were set by constraints a computer does not share; where the brain spent expensive myelin to hold its timing budget, a digital system holds timing with a crystal oscillator and enforces order with Lamport-style logical clocks, and a scheduler can be retuned in software in a way no genome can.

What the cross-species invariant certifies, in the end, is modest and profound at once.

It does not certify that minds need brain waves.

It certifies that minds need time, structured, layered, and defended, and that any substrate that wants to think in a physical world will have to defend its temporal architecture as fiercely as evolution did.

CHAPTER 58. THE SLEEP-CONSOLIDATION PROTOCOL IN DEPTH (COMPUTATION)

This chapter takes the single best-attested computational function of any brain rhythm and slows it down enough to see the machinery.

The claim is that the sleeping brain runs a nightly protocol - a fixed, repeatable procedure - that copies the day's fragile memories from a fast temporary store into a slow durable one, and that specific rhythms are the timing signals of that protocol.

A neuron, for the newcomer, is a brain cell that sends brief electrical pulses called spikes.

A synapse is the tiny junction where one neuron passes its signal to the next, and the adjustable strength of synapses is where the brain stores what it learns.

The hippocampus is a small, curved structure deep in each half of the brain, named after the Latin word for seahorse, and it acts as the brain's fast scratchpad for new memories of events and places.

The neocortex is the large folded outer sheet of the brain, and it is the slow, capacious long-term store.

The reason this chapter carries the COMPUTATION tag, the top grade on this book's causal-evidence ladder, is that experimenters have deleted the rhythm and watched memory fail, stretched the rhythm and watched memory improve, and boosted the rhythm and watched memory improve again.

Few claims in this book can say all three.


58.1 The two-stage model as a memory-systems protocol

This section states the theory that the rhythms serve, because the rhythms only make sense as steps in a larger procedure.

The two-stage model of memory says that learning happens twice: once quickly and provisionally during waking, and once slowly and durably during sleep.

The idea descends from David Marr's theoretical work in 1970 and 1971, which argued that a fast hippocampal store and a slow cortical store must trade information, and it was given its modern rhythm-based form by Gyorgy Buzsaki in a much-cited 1989 paper on two-stage memory trace formation.

Stage one is the waking encode: while you experience something, hippocampal neurons fire in a particular sequence, laying down a quick, cheap, interference-prone copy.

Stage two is the sleeping write-back: during deep non-rapid-eye-movement sleep (non-REM sleep, the dreamless deep stage), the hippocampus replays those firing sequences at high speed and the neocortex, listening, gradually strengthens its own synapses to hold a permanent copy.

The replay itself was demonstrated by Matthew Wilson and Bruce McNaughton in 1994, who recorded rat hippocampal place cells - neurons that each fire at one location in space - and found that cells which fired together on the track fired together again during subsequent sleep.

The replay is packaged inside the sharp-wave ripple, a brief hippocampal event lasting roughly 50 to 150 milliseconds in which a slow deflection (the sharp wave) carries a fast oscillation of about 100 to 250 cycles per second (the ripple), compressing a seconds-long experience into a tenth of a second.

The protocol has three nested timing signals, and their coordination is the whole trick.

The neocortical slow oscillation, at less than one cycle per second, alternates the whole cortex between a silent down-state and an active up-state, and it acts as the master frame clock.

The sleep spindle, a waxing-and-waning burst at roughly 12 to 16 cycles per second generated by the thalamus (the brain's central relay station), rides the rising phase of the slow oscillation's up-state and is thought to open cortical synapses for change.

The hippocampal sharp-wave ripple nests, in turn, into the troughs of the spindle, delivering the replayed content at exactly the moment the cortex is receptive.

Slow oscillation, then spindle, then ripple: a clock, a write-enable signal, and a data burst, in that order.

The causal evidence for the ripple's role is the cleanest in the field.

Gabrielle Girardeau and colleagues showed in 2009 that electrically aborting ripples during post-learning sleep in rats impaired spatial memory, and Valerie Ego-Stengel and Matthew Wilson found the same in 2010.

Antonio Fernandez-Ruiz and colleagues showed in 2019, in Science, that optogenetically prolonging naturally occurring ripples - optogenetics being a technique that turns neurons on or off with light - improved maze memory, while cutting ripples short worsened it.

Deleting the event hurts, stretching it helps: that is what earns a COMPUTATION grade.

The honest caveats belong here too.

A 2023 study in eLife by the Kloosterman laboratory found that disrupting awake ripples did not impair a repeated-acquisition spatial task, so the ripple's necessity may be specific to sleep consolidation rather than to every memory operation.

And the related synaptic homeostasis hypothesis of Giulio Tononi and Chiara Cirelli, which holds that sleep's deeper job is to scale all synapses down rather than to copy content, remains actively contested, so the field has not settled whether the night's protocol is chiefly a copy or chiefly a cleanup, or both.


58.2 The closed-loop enhancement evidence

This section covers the second pillar of the causal case: not deleting the rhythm in animals, but boosting it in people, timed to the rhythm's own phase.

Closed-loop means that the stimulation listens before it acts: a computer reads the sleeper's electroencephalography (EEG) - the recording of the brain's electrical rhythms through electrodes on the scalp - detects a slow oscillation as it happens, and delivers a stimulus locked to a chosen moment of that wave.

The landmark study is Hong-Viet Ngo, Thomas Martinetz, Jan Born, and Matthias Molle, published in Neuron in 2013.

They played soft clicks of pink noise, each about 50 milliseconds long, timed to arrive on the up-state of the sleeper's slow oscillation, and the clicks entrained trains of additional slow oscillations, increased spindle activity, and improved overnight retention of word pairs compared with sham nights.

Crucially, the same clicks delivered out of phase did not help, which is the signature of a timing computation rather than a mere arousal effect.

The phase mattered, not just the sound.

A second line of causal evidence is targeted memory reactivation, which biases which memories get written back.

Bjorn Rasch, Jan Born, and colleagues showed in Science in 2007 that an odor present during learning, re-presented during slow-wave sleep, improved memory for the learned material and re-activated the hippocampus.

John Rudoy, Ken Paller, and colleagues showed in Science in 2009 that individual sounds paired with individual object locations, replayed quietly during a nap, selectively improved memory for exactly the cued objects.

The protocol, in other words, accepts an external tag that selects which entries to consolidate, which is behaviour you expect from a write-back queue, not from noise.

Honesty requires the replication record, and it is mixed at the edges.

A 2019 study in eNeuro by Nelly Henin and colleagues, among others, reproduced the enhanced slow oscillations from closed-loop clicks but found no memory benefit, and a 2021 study found no benefit for semantically incongruent word pairs.

A 2025 review in npj Science of Learning summarised the field as reliably able to modulate the physiology while the memory benefit varies with age, task, and protocol.

The fair reading is that the phase-locked lever demonstrably moves the machinery, that it sometimes and not always moves behaviour in healthy young adults who already consolidate well, and that this pattern - a real mechanism near ceiling - still supports the COMPUTATION grade earned by the animal deletion and prolongation work.


58.3 The batch-write-back analogue spelled out for konnectome

This section translates the protocol into engineering language for the konnectome build, and then, per this book's standing rule, argues the other side.

The translation is batch write-back.

In computing, a write-back cache is a small fast memory that accepts new data immediately and marks it dirty - meaning changed but not yet saved - and then, later and in bulk, flushes the dirty entries to the large slow main store.

The brain's protocol maps onto this almost line for line.

The hippocampus is the fast cache that accepts the day's entries cheaply.

Sleep is the scheduled maintenance window in which foreground input is gated off, exactly as a database takes a table offline for compaction.

The slow oscillation is the frame clock that marks each write cycle.

The spindle is the write-enable signal that makes the destination writable for a moment.

The sharp-wave ripple is the burst transfer, a compressed replay of one entry, and its 8 to 20 fold time compression is a familiar engineering move: batch transfers run faster than real-time logging because they need not wait for the world.

Targeted memory reactivation is a priority flag on the queue: tag an entry, and it is flushed preferentially.

Interleaved replay of old and new material during the flush is how the slow store avoids catastrophic forgetting, the failure mode in which training a neural network on new data overwrites the old, a lesson machine learning re-learned independently under the name experience replay in the deep reinforcement learning work of Volodymyr Mnih and colleagues in 2015.

For konnectome the concrete analogue is this: a fast episodic buffer that logs experience with timestamps during operation, a scheduled offline phase in which the buffer replays selected, compressed, priority-weighted entries into the slow weights, interleaved with rehearsal of old entries, with a write-enable gate ensuring the slow store only changes during the flush.

Now the other side, as the design test demands.

Every one of those functions has an explicit digital mechanism that beats the wave-like implementation on its own terms.

A dirty bit and a flush scheduler replace the slow oscillation, because software does not need a global voltage swing to know a write cycle has begun; a timestamped queue is strictly more precise than a phase.

A priority field on each buffer entry replaces the targeted-reactivation cue, with none of the cue's unreliability.

A write lock replaces the spindle, and it never fires at the wrong phase.

The brain uses nested rhythms because its billions of writers share no global clock and no addressing scheme, so phase alignment is the only way to say now, here, this one to distant tissue.

Konnectome has real clocks, real addresses, and real locks, so the honest engineering conclusion is: copy the protocol - buffer, schedule, compress, prioritise, interleave, gate - and discard the waves that the brain uses merely to implement it.

What must not be discarded is the one thing the rhythm bought that a naive design forgets: consolidation must be periodic, offline, selective, and interleaved, or the slow store will either never learn or catastrophically forget.


CHAPTER 59. THE ATTENTION-SAMPLING MECHANISM IN DEPTH (COMPUTATION)

This chapter examines the claim that attention - the mind's selection of one thing to process at the expense of others - is not a steady beam but a flickering one.

The everyday intuition is a spotlight that shines continuously on whatever you attend to.

The evidence of the last fifteen years says the spotlight blinks, brightening and dimming several times a second, and that the blinking follows the phase of slow brain rhythms in the theta band, roughly 4 to 8 cycles per second, and the alpha band, roughly 8 to 12 cycles per second.

If true, attention is a sampling loop: the brain takes discrete snapshots of the world and stitches them into the illusion of continuous watching, much as a movie camera does at 24 frames per second.

The chapter carries the COMPUTATION tag with a caveat the reader will meet below: the causal evidence here is thinner than for the sleep protocol, resting more on tight correlations between phase and behaviour, plus a growing body of perturbation work, and part of the field still disputes how rhythmic the behaviour really is.


59.1 The periodic spotlight evidence

This section lays out the evidence that detection of the world waxes and wanes rhythmically.

The behavioural method is simple and clever: flash a faint target at a random moment after a reset event, repeat thousands of times, and plot the probability of detecting it as a function of the delay.

If attention were continuous the curve would be flat.

It is not flat.

Ayelet Landau and Pascal Fries showed in Current Biology in 2012 that when attention is spread over two locations, detection at each location oscillates at about 4 cycles per second, and the two locations oscillate in counterphase - when one is up the other is down - as if a single sampler at about 8 cycles per second were alternating between them.

Ian Fiebelkorn, Yuri Saalmann, and Sabine Kastner showed in the same journal and the same year that even with attention held at a single cued location, detection there fluctuates at about 8 cycles per second, and sampling between objects alternates at about 4 cycles per second per object.

The physiological counterpart came from Fiebelkorn and Kastner's recordings in the monkey fronto-parietal attention network, summarised in their 2019 rhythmic theory of attention: the phase of theta rhythms in the frontal eye fields and the lateral intraparietal area - two hub regions that aim attention - predicts, moment by moment, whether a target will be seen.

Their proposal is that each theta cycle contains two states: a sampling state, in which processing of the attended location is boosted, and a shifting state, a window of opportunity in which attention can move somewhere new.

Attention on this view is a rhythmic exploration routine, and the theta rhythm is its loop counter.

Causal and clinical support has followed.

Work from the Kastner laboratory implicated the superior colliculus, an ancient midbrain orienting structure, in the shifting state, and rhythmic sampling has been reported across senses and species, from monkeys to humans.

The dispute must be stated with equal clarity.

Geoffrey Brookshire argued in Nature Human Behaviour in 2022 that the standard analysis of these behavioural experiments can manufacture apparent rhythms from aperiodic data, because detrending and spectral testing against the wrong null inflates false positives.

Replies, including a 2023 perspective by Fiebelkorn in the Journal of Cognitive Neuroscience titled There Is More Evidence of Rhythmic Attention than Can Be Found in Behavioral Studies, answered that the physiological phase-behaviour correlations do not depend on the contested statistics.

The fair summary is that the neural phase-to-performance link is solid, the purely behavioural oscillation is statistically fragile in some studies, and the COMPUTATION grade here rests on the physiology plus perturbation, not on behavioural curves alone.


59.2 The alpha-and-theta division of labour

This section separates the two slow rhythms that attention research kept finding, because they do different jobs.

The theta rhythm, 4 to 8 cycles per second, is the sampler: as the previous section described, it sets the tempo at which the attention network probes a location and considers moving on.

The alpha rhythm, 8 to 12 cycles per second, is the suppressor: it does not select what to process so much as silence what to ignore.

The modern statement of alpha's role is the gating-by-inhibition hypothesis of Ole Jensen and Ali Mazaheri, published in Frontiers in Human Neuroscience in 2010.

On this account, alpha power - the size of the alpha wave - rises over cortical regions handling irrelevant input, applying rhythmic pulses of inhibition that shut those regions off roughly ten times a second, and falls over regions handling the attended input, releasing them to work.

The classic observation, going back to Worden and colleagues in 2000 and replicated many times since, is that when you attend left, alpha rises over the brain areas representing the right visual field, and the opposite when you attend right.

Rufin VanRullen's perceptual cycles programme, summarised in Trends in Cognitive Sciences in 2016, adds the perceptual half of the story: the phase of ongoing alpha at the moment a faint stimulus arrives predicts whether it will be perceived, as if perception took a roughly 100 millisecond snapshot per alpha cycle.

A 2022 study by Christian Michel and colleagues in the European Journal of Neuroscience, and a 2024 review in Cognition by Camille Fakche and Laura Dugue, drew the division of labour explicitly: alpha paces perceptual sampling within a location, theta paces attentional sampling between locations.

The honest caveats: not every laboratory finds the alpha-phase perception effect, with a 2023 preregistered study finding amplitude but not phase effects, and the causal status of alpha suppression - whether it is the mechanism of ignoring or a downstream marker of it - is still argued, notably in a 2024 Communications Psychology study suggesting the suppression is controlled indirectly.

So the sampler-plus-suppressor picture is well supported but its edges are live science, and this book grades the sampling loop as COMPUTATION while flagging that alpha's inhibitory role, taken alone, sits closer to the boundary.


59.3 The polling-loop analogue for konnectome

This section translates the blinking spotlight into engineering terms, and then argues the other side.

The translation is a polling loop.

Polling, in computing, is the strategy in which one controller visits each input source in turn on a fixed schedule and asks: anything new?

The theta sampler is a polling loop over attention targets, visiting each roughly four to eight times a second, with the counterphase alternation of Landau and Fries being exactly a two-slot round-robin schedule - round-robin meaning each client is served in fixed rotation.

The sample-then-shift structure of each theta cycle is the loop body: process the current item, then run the branch that decides whether to stay or move on.

The alpha suppressor is the masking of interrupts from deprioritised sources, holding noisy channels quiet so the loop is not dragged off schedule.

The frame-like snapshot of each alpha cycle is a sampling window with a defined integration time, the engineering term for how long a sensor gathers input before reporting one reading.

For konnectome the concrete analogue is this: a scheduler that time-slices perceptual processing across candidate targets at a fixed loop rate, an explicit stay-or-shift decision evaluated once per slice using accumulated evidence, and per-channel gain masks that suppress deprioritised streams between their turns.

The loop rate need not be 8 cycles per second; the brain's number reflects its conduction delays and integration times, and konnectome should set the rate from its own latencies.

Now the other side, as the design test requires.

Polling is famously the weaker pattern in engineering: interrupt-driven input - in which a source announces its own event the moment it happens - wastes no cycles on empty channels and misses nothing that arrives between visits.

A priority queue with explicit numeric priorities beats rhythmic alternation, because it can give one target 90 percent of the budget rather than the rigid half-and-half of counterphase sampling.

An explicit scheduler with timestamps never suffers the blind phase: the brain demonstrably misses stimuli that land in the trough, a cost konnectome need not pay.

The brain polls because its sensors cannot raise interrupts with priority labels attached; selection has to be imposed centrally by rhythmic excitability because there is no other way to arbitrate billions of channels without an addressing scheme.

The honest engineering conclusion: keep the loop's wisdom - attention as a budgeted, periodic, revisit-and-reconsider process with explicit suppression of the unattended - and implement it with a real scheduler, real priorities, and interrupts, keeping periodic revisiting only as the guarantee against starvation, the scheduling failure in which a low-priority input never gets served at all.


CHAPTER 60. THE ROUTING MECHANISM IN DEPTH (COMPUTATION)

This chapter examines the claim that brain rhythms decide where information travels.

The puzzle it answers is real and hard: the anatomical wiring of the cortex is fixed on the timescale of a moment, every region is connected to many others, and yet the effective flow of signals reconfigures in a fraction of a second when a task changes.

Something must be switching traffic over static wires.

The proposal examined here is that oscillations do the switching: two regions that beat in time exchange information effectively, and two regions out of time do not, so the brain routes by synchrony the way a railway routes by timetable rather than by laying new track.

The chapter carries the COMPUTATION tag on the strength of selective-routing recordings and perturbation evidence, with the conduction-delay objection - one of this book's three recurring objections - given its full weight below.


60.1 Communication through coherence as the biological router

This section states the hypothesis and the evidence that made it famous.

Communication through coherence is Pascal Fries's proposal, published in Trends in Cognitive Sciences in 2005 and revised in Neuron in 2015 under the title Rhythms for Cognition.

The mechanism rests on one physiological fact: a neuronal population oscillating in the gamma band - roughly 30 to 80 cycles per second, paced by fast-spiking inhibitory interneurons, the neurons whose job is to briefly silence their neighbours - is not equally receptive all the time.

Each gamma cycle has a brief excitable window when inhibition has just worn off, and a closed window when inhibition is fresh.

If a sending region's volleys of spikes arrive at the receiver's open window, they drive it strongly; if they arrive at the closed window, they are absorbed by inhibition and lost.

Coherence - a consistent phase relationship between the sender's rhythm and the receiver's rhythm - therefore renders a fixed anatomical connection effective, and its absence renders the same wire ineffective.

The flagship evidence is Conrado Bosman, Thilo Womelsdorf, Robert Oostenveld, Fries, and colleagues in Neuron in 2012.

They placed electrode grids over monkey visual areas V1, the first cortical visual area, and V4, a later area that receives from V1, and arranged two stimuli so that two separate V1 sites and one V4 site were all in play.

Both V1 sites generated gamma, but only the V1 site representing the attended stimulus became coherent with V4; the unattended site's gamma found no echo downstream.

Same wires, same stimuli on the retina, different routing, selected by attention - and Grothe and colleagues in 2018 showed the gating extends to the transmission of stimulus content, not just rhythm.

Andre Bastos, Fries, and colleagues added a directional discovery in Neuron in 2015: across 28 pairs of visual areas, feedforward influence - from earlier areas toward later ones - travelled in the theta and gamma bands, while feedback influence travelled in the beta band, roughly 14 to 30 cycles per second, so ascending and descending traffic use different carrier frequencies, like uplink and downlink channels in radio.

Now the objection, stated as this book's discipline requires.

Axonal conduction is slow, at several milliseconds between areas, and a gamma cycle is only about 12 to 30 milliseconds long, so a sender aligned with one receiver is thereby misaligned with another at a different distance, and zero-phase synchrony between distant areas seemed physically impossible to exploit.

Ray and Maunsell's finding, met again in Chapter 61, that gamma frequency drifts with stimulus contrast makes stable alignment harder still.

The hypothesis survived by amendment, which is how honest science proceeds: Bastos, Julien Vezoli, and Fries showed in Neuron in 2015, in Communication Through Coherence with Inter-Areal Delays, that observed inter-areal phase relations are non-zero and match the conduction delays, so that spikes leaving the sender arrive precisely at the receiver's open window - the timetable includes the travel time.

The grade holds, but the reader should know it holds on the amended, delay-aware version of the theory, not the original zero-phase version.


60.2 The interference-minimising refinement

This section covers the second-generation refinement: routing is not only about connecting the chosen partners but about protecting the channel from everyone else.

A router that merely connects A to B has done half the job; the hard half is preventing C's traffic from corrupting the line, which engineers call interference and the brain confronts as multiple converging inputs onto one receiver.

The refinement observes that coherence solves this too, in three ways.

First, winner-takes-most gating: in the Bosman 2012 data the attended V1 site did not merely reach V4, it captured V4's rhythm, and once the receiver's excitable windows are enslaved to the attended sender, the unattended sender's volleys arrive at closed windows by default - selecting one input actively suppresses the others without any extra hardware.

Second, frequency offset: Bosman's attended V1 site ran its gamma 2 to 3 cycles per second faster than the unattended site and 4 to 6 faster than V4, and modelling work - notably Agostina Palmigiano, Fred Wolf, and colleagues in Nature Neuroscience in 2017 on the flexibility of transient synchrony, and the laminar model of Jorge Mejias and colleagues - shows that such offsets let the faster sender consistently win the receiver's open window, a mechanism with the flavour of frequency-division multiplexing, the radio technique of separating channels by carrier frequency.

Third, phase separation of multiplexed content: Fries's 2015 revision proposes that when a receiver must listen to two senders, it can hold them at different phases of its cycle, giving each an assigned time slot - time-division multiplexing, the telephone-network technique of interleaving many conversations on one line by taking turns.

The perturbation evidence has strengthened this account: optogenetic entrainment of interneuron-paced gamma has been shown to bias which of two inputs drives a downstream population, moving the refinement from correlation toward cause.

The honest limits: most of the decisive recordings come from the visual system of two monkey species, coherence measured between field potentials can be inflated by volume conduction - the passive spread of electrical signals through tissue - and a 2021 Neuron paper by Marius Schneider, Martin Vinck, and colleagues showed that inter-areal coherence can arise as a byproduct of connectivity and power without any communication being gated at all.

That last objection is the serious one, because it attacks the inference from measured coherence to routing; the perturbation studies are the field's answer, and they are why the grade remains COMPUTATION rather than falling to CONTESTED.


60.3 The timing-gated-bus analogue for konnectome

This section translates the router into engineering terms, and then argues the other side.

The translation is a timing-gated bus.

A bus, in computing, is a shared communication line that many components use to exchange data, and the eternal bus problem is arbitration: deciding who may transmit to whom, and when, so that messages do not collide.

Communication through coherence is bus arbitration by clock phase.

The receiver's gamma is a gating clock: a periodic signal defining when the receiver's input latch is open.

Coherence with built-in delay compensation is the timing closure of the channel - the hardware-design discipline of ensuring a signal launched on one clock edge arrives within the receiver's capture window, wire delay included.

The Bastos feedforward-gamma, feedback-beta split is two frequency-separated channels on the same fabric, uplink and downlink.

The phase-separation refinement is time-division multiplexing, and the frequency-offset refinement is a fast sender out-clocking rivals for the receiver's window.

For konnectome the concrete analogue is this: a message fabric in which each receiving module exposes explicit receptive windows, senders schedule transmissions to land inside the window of their intended receiver with transport delay counted, attention-like control reassigns window ownership to reroute traffic without rewiring, and ascending and descending traffic run on separated channels.

Now the other side, as the design test requires.

Digital engineering solved routing long ago with a mechanism the brain cannot use: the address.

A packet-switched network - a network that wraps every message in an envelope carrying its destination, as the internet does - routes anything to anywhere with no phase alignment at all, and a network-on-chip crossbar does the same inside a processor.

Explicit addressing is strictly more flexible than coherence: it needs no continuous entrainment before the first message passes, it cannot be captured by an interfering fast sender, and it does not degrade when a channel's frequency drifts.

Lamport's logical clocks - Leslie Lamport's 1978 scheme in which every message carries a counter so that order is established without any shared physical clock - give konnectome the ordering guarantees that phase alignment only approximates.

The brain routes by timing because evolution could not retrofit destination headers onto axons; every spike is anonymous, so when and how often are the only fields in the packet.

The honest engineering conclusion: konnectome should route by address and order by logical clock, and borrow from coherence only the three ideas that survive translation - that routing state should be reconfigurable over a fixed fabric on a fast timescale, that a receiver should own explicit gating windows so an unattended sender is cheap to ignore at the receiver rather than expensive to filter afterward, and that separating feedforward from feedback traffic onto distinct channels keeps a recurrent system analysable.


CHAPTER 61. THE BINDING MECHANISM IN DEPTH (COMPUTATION)

This chapter examines the oldest, grandest, and most battered claim in the science of brain waves: that synchrony is how the brain glues features together into objects.

The binding problem, first: the brain processes colour, shape, motion, and location in different neural populations, yet you perceive one red ball rolling left, not free-floating redness plus roundness plus leftward motion.

Something must mark which features belong to which object, and must do it afresh each moment for objects never seen before.

The proposal examined here is binding by temporal synchrony: neurons representing features of the same object fire in time with one another, and neurons representing different objects fire out of time, so the glue is not a place or a wire but a shared moment.

This chapter carries the COMPUTATION tag by the outline's assignment, and it must earn that tag carefully, because the original hypothesis failed in its strong form, and what survives is a narrower, revived, partly synthetic version.

The reader will watch a hypothesis die and be reborn smaller, which is the most instructive spectacle science offers.


61.1 The synchrony proposal and its failure as a fixed code

This section tells the rise and fall honestly.

The rise: Charles Gray and Wolf Singer reported in 1989, in Nature and in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, that neurons in cat visual cortex fired in oscillatory bursts near 40 cycles per second, and that two separated neurons synchronised their firing when stimulated by one continuous moving bar but not by two separate bars.

The synchrony tracked perceptual grouping - the Gestalt rules by which the eye groups fragments into wholes - and the temporal correlation hypothesis, articulated by Singer, Andreas Engel, Peter Konig, and by Christoph von der Malsburg's earlier 1981 theoretical correlation theory, made 40-cycle synchrony a candidate code for object identity, and for a heady decade some proposed it as a substrate of consciousness itself.

The fall came in three named blows, each of which this book's discipline requires in full.

First, Michael Shadlen and Anthony Movshon's 1999 critique in Neuron, Synchrony Unbound, argued that the hypothesis was conceptually incomplete - synchrony can tag a grouping but cannot compute which grouping is correct, which is the actual hard problem - and that the physiological evidence was inconsistent, with several laboratories failing to find the predicted oscillations in awake monkeys.

Second, Supratim Ray and John Maunsell showed in Neuron in 2010 that gamma frequency in monkey V1 is not a stable carrier: it rises monotonically with stimulus contrast, so two parts of one object at different contrasts oscillate at different frequencies and drift apart in phase within a few cycles - a clock that speeds up with the brightness of what it looks at cannot serve as a shared timing reference - and their related 2011 work showed much high-frequency signal is not oscillatory at all.

Third, direct psychophysical tests failed: Sander Nieuwenhuis and colleagues, and Farid Kingdom's and David Field's related work, and most pointedly a 2001 Nature Neuroscience study by Marius Usher and colleagues' opponents titled Synchrony Does Not Promote Grouping in Temporally Structured Displays, found that making display elements flicker in synchrony does not make observers group them.

Even Singer's own retrospectives, including a 2023 accounting in which he acknowledged that the strong version required revision toward transient, flexible dynamics rather than a fixed 40-cycle code, mark the strong hypothesis as closed.

The verdict on the original claim, stated plainly: binding by a fixed gamma synchrony code is dead, killed by the frequency-drift objection above all, and no honest book grades it otherwise.


61.2 The modern rotating-features and phase-tag revival

This section explains what came back, because something did.

The revival has two branches, one in machine learning and one in neuroscience, and their convergence is the interesting fact.

The machine-learning branch: standard artificial neurons output a single number, so a network representing two objects at once superimposes them with no marker of which feature belongs to which - the binding problem, rediscovered inside engineering.

Sindy Lowe, Phillip Lippe, Francesco Locatello, and Max Welling built Complex-Valued Autoencoders and then Rotating Features, published at the Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems in 2023, in which every neuron's activation is a small vector with a magnitude and an orientation: magnitude says a feature is present, orientation says which object it belongs to.

Features of the same object align their orientations during processing, and objects separate without any labels being provided - which is von der Malsburg's phase tag, implemented and working on real images, with orientation playing the role that spike timing played in the 1989 story.

The neuroscience branch: rather than a fixed gamma code, the modern proposals use the phase of slower rhythms as a temporary tag.

Earl Miller's laboratory and others report working-memory items held at different phases of underlying rhythms, and a 2024 study by Marte Otten and colleagues reported alpha phase-coding supporting feature binding during working-memory maintenance, with an object's features riding a shared alpha phase.

The theta-gamma code of John Lisman and Ole Jensen, treated fully in Chapter 62, is the same move for sequences: items as gamma packets, ordered by their position in a theta cycle.

What distinguishes the revival from the original: the tag is transient and task-driven rather than a standing stimulus-driven code, it lives at lower and more stable frequencies than the drifting gamma, and its strongest existence proof is now synthetic - the mechanism demonstrably solves binding in an artificial system, whatever the brain does.

The honest status: the machine-learning result proves sufficiency, not biology; the human phase-coding studies are recent, few, and correlational; and Lowe's own 2024 follow-up, Binding Dynamics in Rotating Features, reports that the alignment mechanism has failure modes of its own.

So this chapter's COMPUTATION grade attaches to the narrow claim that phase-tagging is a workable binding computation - proven by construction in machines, plausibly and transiently used by brains - and not to the dead strong claim of 61.1.


61.3 The pointer-against-phase decision for konnectome

This section poses the design decision the two previous sections force, and argues both sides as the design test requires - and here, unusually, the digital side has already won the argument once in public.

The decision: when konnectome represents several objects at once, should it bind each object's features with a pointer or with a phase?

A pointer, in computing, is a stored address: object 7's colour and shape each carry a reference to a shared object record, and binding is explicit, exact, and permanent until released.

A phase tag is the rotating-features move: features carry an orientation, features of one object align, and binding is implicit in the geometry of the representation.

The case for the pointer is the case Shadlen and Movshon made: it is exact, it scales to as many objects as there are addresses, it cannot drift, two bindings cannot blur into each other, and it is inspectable - a debugger can follow a pointer, which matters for a build that intends to audit its own cognition.

The case for the phase tag is what the machine-learning revival proved: it is learned rather than allocated, so it binds novel feature combinations without a supervisor assigning addresses; it is soft, so partial alignment can express uncertainty about whether a feature belongs; it is parallel, with binding emerging in the same operations that process the features, with no separate allocation step to bottleneck; and it degrades gracefully rather than dangling - a dangling pointer, the classic software fault of an address whose target has been freed, has no analogue in a geometry that merely drifts.

The capacity fact cuts both ways: phase codes have a natural limit of roughly four to seven separable tags per cycle, which matches working-memory limits and may even explain them, but konnectome is not obliged to inherit the brain's bottleneck.

The decision this book recommends is layered, and it mirrors what the brain's own revival suggests.

In the fast perceptual layers, where novel objects must be segmented from raw input with no supervisor, use phase-style soft binding - rotating-feature vectors - because that is the regime where the pointer's allocation step does not know what to allocate yet.

At the moment an object becomes cognitively load-bearing - attended, named, stored, reasoned about - promote the soft binding to a hard one: allocate an explicit object record and pointers, and let the phase geometry be garbage-collected.

The promotion step is the konnectome translation of what attention plus working memory seem to do in the brain: convert a transient perceptual grouping into a durable, addressable token.

Where the digital mechanism simply wins, say so: for anything persistent, communicable, or auditable, the pointer beats the phase, because an address survives interruption, crosses modules without entrainment, and can be logged.

Where the wave-like mechanism wins, say so with equal honesty: at the raw front end, binding must be discovered before it can be addressed, and the alignment dynamics that grew out of the synchrony hypothesis are, on current evidence from both machine learning and neuroscience, the best known way to discover it.

That double verdict - phase to discover, pointer to keep - is this chapter's closing judgement, and it is the binding hypothesis's afterlife in engineering: wrong as a theory of a fixed gamma glue, right as the ancestor of a mechanism a synthetic mind can actually use.

CHAPTER 62. THE WORKING-MEMORY BUFFER IN DEPTH (COMPUTATION)

Working memory is the mental scratchpad you use to hold a phone number in mind while you cross the room to dial it.

This chapter takes the most celebrated rhythmic proposal about that scratchpad - the idea that two nested brain rhythms act together as a multi-item buffer - and works through it slowly, from the biology to the engineering translation.

A brain rhythm, throughout this book, means a repeating rise and fall of electrical activity produced by large groups of neurons - the brain's individual signalling cells - firing and pausing together.

The two rhythms in question are theta, a slow wave of roughly 4 to 8 cycles per second, and gamma, a fast ripple of roughly 30 to 100 cycles per second, and the proposal is that several gamma cycles ride on each theta cycle the way several ticks of a fast clock fit inside one tick of a slow one.

The chapter carries the tag COMPUTATION because the buffer proposal, unlike many rhythm stories, makes a specific mechanical claim that has survived decades of testing in partial but real form, and because it translates directly into a buffer design that konnectome can either adopt or beat with a simpler digital structure.

62.1 The theta-gamma slot code

The slot-code idea is the heart of the proposal, so we begin by stating it plainly and then naming the evidence.

John Lisman and Marco Idiart published the founding model in Science in 1995, and Lisman and Ole Jensen restated and updated it as The Theta-Gamma Neural Code in the journal Neuron in 2013.

The claim is this: each item held in working memory is represented by a small group of neurons firing together within one gamma cycle, and successive items occupy successive gamma cycles nested inside a single theta cycle, so the theta cycle acts like a rotating tray with a fixed number of slots.

Picture a sushi conveyor belt that completes one loop every 150 milliseconds, carrying five to eight plates, with each plate holding one item - the belt is theta, the plates are gamma cycles, and the items are the digits of the phone number you are rehearsing.

On every pass of the belt the whole list is replayed in order, which is why the model is sometimes called a phase code: which item you are means which phase, which position within the theta cycle, your neurons fire at.

Several lines of evidence support the picture.

Ryan Canolty and colleagues showed in Science in 2006 that in the human neocortex - the brain's folded outer sheet - the strength of gamma activity is systematically tied to the phase of the theta wave, a phenomenon called theta-gamma phase-amplitude coupling.

In the hippocampus - the seahorse-shaped structure deep in the temporal lobe that is essential for forming new memories - John O'Keefe and Michael Recce discovered phase precession in 1993: as a rat runs through a place cell's preferred location, the cell fires at progressively earlier phases of the theta cycle, exactly the kind of orderly phase-to-content mapping the slot code requires.

In humans, work by Jan Kaminski, Jonathan Daume, Ueli Rutishauser and colleagues, published in 2023 and after, recorded single hippocampal neurons in epilepsy patients and found that theta-gamma phase-amplitude coupling in the hippocampus tracks working-memory maintenance and predicts performance, tying the coupling to the task rather than to mere arousal.

And Mikael Lundqvist, Earl Miller and colleagues showed in Neuron in 2016 that in the monkey prefrontal cortex - the front part of the brain associated with planning and control - working-memory contents are carried by brief bursts of narrow-band gamma at roughly 45 to 100 cycles per second rather than by steady sustained firing, which fits a buffer that refreshes items intermittently instead of holding them continuously.

Now the honest other side, because this book never oversells.

The three standing technical objections from Part Five all apply here: gamma frequency drifts with stimulus properties, so what looks like a fixed slot clock may be a moving target; theta phase is hard to align across brain regions because signals take time to travel; and non-sinusoidal wave shapes - theta in particular is famously sawtooth-shaped, not a smooth sine - can create the statistical appearance of phase-amplitude coupling where none exists, as Scott Cole and Bradley Voytek warned in 2017.

Careful studies address these objections with controls, and the human single-neuron work is much harder to dismiss than scalp recordings, but the field has not produced the decisive experiment - selectively perturbing gamma slots and watching specific list items vanish - so the slot code earns its COMPUTATION tag on the strength of converging correlational and mechanistic evidence plus the causal precedent of the parvalbumin gamma work, not on a single clean knockout.

62.2 The capacity limit as a ratio

The most charming feature of the slot code is that it predicts one of psychology's oldest numbers for free, and this section examines how seriously to take that.

George Miller's famous 1956 paper put the span of immediate memory at seven plus or minus two items, and later work by Nelson Cowan in 2001 argued the purer limit is closer to four.

The slot code says the limit is simply a ratio: the number of gamma cycles that fit inside one theta cycle.

Divide a theta period of roughly 125 to 250 milliseconds by a gamma period of roughly 10 to 25 milliseconds and you get something in the range of five to nine slots, straddling Miller's number, or fewer if the relevant gamma is the slower 30 to 50 cycle variety, edging toward Cowan's four.

Lisman and Jensen made exactly this argument in 2013, and it is a beautiful piece of reasoning: an arbitrary-seeming psychological constant explained as a clock-divider ratio, the way a wall clock's twelve hours come from gearing.

Some supporting evidence exists beyond arithmetic.

Studies have reported that individuals with slower theta or faster gamma - hence more slots - show larger memory spans, and transcranial alternating current stimulation studies claimed that slowing theta stretches capacity while speeding it shrinks capacity.

But this is where discipline matters.

The stimulation studies inherit the general objection, established by Asamoah and colleagues in Nature Communications in 2019, that weak scalp currents may act through stimulated nerves in the skin rather than through the brain, so they cannot carry the causal weight alone.

The correlational studies are small and inconsistently replicated.

And the capacity limit itself has rival explanations - interference between overlapping neural patterns, limited attentional resources, synaptic fatigue - that need no clock ratio at all.

The fair verdict is that the ratio account is a live, elegant, testable hypothesis with suggestive support, not an established fact, and the prose of this book treats the number-of-slots claim as the model's boldest and least secured plank.

62.3 The serialised-buffer analogue for konnectome

Now the translation, in the systems vocabulary this Part uses throughout.

For the newcomer: a buffer, in computing, is a small holding area for data awaiting use; a ring buffer is a buffer arranged in a circle so that a write pointer marches around it and wraps to the start, overwriting the oldest entry; and time-division multiplexing means letting several signals share one channel by giving each signal its own repeating time slot, which is exactly how early telephone trunks carried many calls on one wire.

The theta-gamma buffer is, in these terms, a time-division multiplexed ring buffer: one shared population of neurons - one channel - carries several items by assigning each item a recurring time slot, and the whole ring is re-read on every theta sweep.

What does this buy, and what does it cost?

The biological design gets three things: many items share one physical substrate without interfering, because they never fire at the same instant; order comes for free, because slot position is list position; and every item is automatically refreshed on every cycle, which suits a memory medium - recurrent neural firing - that decays in tens of milliseconds if not reactivated.

The costs are equally clear: capacity is hard-capped by the slot ratio; access is serial, so reading item five means waiting for slot five to come around, predicting reaction times that grow with list position - a prediction, incidentally, borne out by Saul Sternberg's classic scanning experiments of 1966; and the whole scheme is only worth it because neurons cannot cheaply write a stable random-access memory on the fly.

The design test this book applies to every translation now cuts the other way.

Konnectome runs on digital hardware where a stable store costs nothing: an ordered list of working items is just an array with an index, capacity is whatever memory allows, access is random rather than serial, and no refresh sweep is needed because bits do not decay.

So the honest recommendation is double.

Konnectome should not implement a literal oscillating buffer to hold items, because an explicit indexed queue beats it on every engineering axis.

But konnectome should steal the scheduling insight: when many items must share one scarce processing resource - one attention head, one inference pass, one write port - a fixed rotating slot schedule with a hard small capacity and automatic refresh is a proven, starvation-free design, and the theta-gamma system is its four-hundred-million-year field trial.

CHAPTER 63. THE TRAVELING-WAVE ORGANISER IN DEPTH (COMPUTATION)

So far this book has mostly treated a brain rhythm as if a whole region rose and fell in unison, like a hall full of people clapping in time.

This chapter corrects that picture: much of the time the clapping is not simultaneous but sweeps across the hall like a stadium wave, and the sweep itself may be doing work.

A traveling wave, in this context, is an oscillation whose phase - its position in the rise-and-fall cycle - shifts smoothly across the cortical surface, so that the peak of activity moves across the tissue at a measurable speed.

The chapter carries the COMPUTATION tag because recent causal-adjacent evidence ties the position and timing of these waves to perception and memory on a moment-by-moment basis, and because the wavefront idea translates into a concrete konnectome mechanism for sweeping updates across a data structure.

63.1 The propagation phenomenology

Before asking what traveling waves are for, we establish what they measurably are.

Lyle Muller, Frederic Chavane, John Reynolds and Terrence Sejnowski gathered the field's phenomenology in a landmark review, Cortical travelling waves: mechanisms and computational principles, in Nature Reviews Neuroscience in 2018.

At the small scale, waves of activity sweep across single sensory areas at roughly 0.1 to 0.8 metres per second, a speed set by the conduction of unmyelinated horizontal axons - the slow local wires that link neighbouring patches of cortex.

At the large scale, Honghui Zhang, Joshua Jacobs and colleagues showed in Neuron in 2018, using electrode grids placed directly on the brains of neurosurgical patients, that human theta and alpha oscillations at roughly 2 to 15 cycles per second are very often traveling waves, propagating across the cortex at roughly 0.25 to 0.75 metres per second, most commonly from the back of the brain toward the front.

Sleep provides the most spectacular examples.

The slow oscillation of deep sleep travels as a wave from front to back, as Marcello Massimini and colleagues showed in 2004, and Muller and colleagues showed in 2016 that sleep spindles - the 11 to 15 cycle per second bursts of stage two sleep - circulate as rotating waves sweeping from temporal to parietal to frontal cortex and around again.

Uma Mohan, Jacobs and colleagues added in Nature Human Behaviour in 2024 that the direction of theta and alpha traveling waves in humans flips with cognitive state: waves tend to travel forward, from sensory toward frontal cortex, during memory encoding, and backward during recall.

Two cautions keep the phenomenology honest.

First, an electrode grid sees only the cortical surface, and a deep source activating superficial areas at slightly different delays can masquerade as a traveling wave, so careful studies must rule out such fictive propagation.

Second, wave direction and speed statistics depend on the analysis method - circular statistics on noisy phase estimates - and methods papers continue to argue about how much apparent propagation is real.

The consensus after these corrections is that genuine mesoscopic and macroscopic traveling waves are common, structured and state-dependent, which by itself already refutes the simple standing-wave cartoon.

63.2 The spatial-organisation proposals

Granting that the waves exist, this section lays out what they are proposed to do, and grades the evidence.

The first proposal is that a traveling wave is a sweeping excitability broom: since the wave's peak is a moment of heightened readiness in each patch it crosses, the wave carries a moving window of opportunity across the map of the sensory world.

The strongest evidence here is causal-adjacent and recent.

Zachary Davis, John Reynolds and colleagues showed in Nature in 2020 that in awake behaving marmosets - small monkeys - spontaneous traveling waves sweep across the visual cortex, and a faint visual target is detected far more often when it arrives just as a wave peak passes its cortical location.

The wave's position predicted perception trial by trial, which is exactly what an excitability broom should do, though note the grading discipline: predicting is not perturbing, and no one has yet steered a wave and steered perception with it.

The second proposal is that waves organise timing for plasticity and memory: a wavefront crossing a sheet of neurons imposes an orderly sequence of firing times, and since synapses - the connections between neurons - strengthen or weaken according to precise spike timing, a repeated wave can sculpt connections along its path.

The spindle rotating waves of Muller and colleagues have been proposed, on this logic, as a mechanism for stitching memories across distant areas during sleep, and Mohan and colleagues' encoding-versus-recall direction flip fits a system that reverses its information flow between writing and reading.

The third proposal, argued by Muller, Reynolds and Sejnowski and picked up by machine-learning researchers, is computational in the engineering sense: a wave turns time into space and space into time, holding a short history of inputs as a spatial pattern the way ripples on a pond hold the recent history of thrown stones, and providing each location with context from its neighbours' recent past.

The grade across these proposals is a strong COMPUTATION-leaning verdict with a stated gap: the existence, structure and behavioural correlation of the waves are solid, the Davis result approaches causal force, but direct perturbation experiments - injecting or blocking a wave and changing the outcome - remain the missing keystone.

63.3 The wavefront-reset analogue for konnectome

The translation for konnectome starts with a definition for the newcomer.

In a computer, updating every element of a large data structure at once is expensive and often impossible; systems therefore use sweeps - garbage collectors that walk the heap, cache systems that walk their entries, robots vacuuming a floor - where a single moving frontier visits every location in an orderly, bounded-cost way.

A traveling wave is biology's sweep: one moving frontier of heightened activity that visits every patch of a cortical map on a fixed schedule, delivering three services as it passes - a refresh, a moment of prioritised readiness, and a shared timing reference that tells each patch where its neighbours are in their cycle.

The konnectome analogue is a wavefront-reset pass: a background process that walks the graph of knowledge nodes in a spatially ordered pattern at a fixed cadence, refreshing decayed activations, granting each node its brief window of preferential access to shared machinery, and stamping local order among neighbours as it goes.

The stamped local order matters because it is a cheap partial substitute for a Lamport logical clock - Leslie Lamport's 1978 scheme, explained fully in Chapter 64's neighbour and earlier in Part Thirteen, for ordering events in a distributed system without any shared physical clock.

Now the design test, as always in both directions.

The wave-like sweep pays for itself when the structure is large, updates are cheap per element, strict global ordering is unnecessary, and the hardware rewards predictable sequential access - which real memory hierarchies emphatically do, since walking memory in order is many times faster than jumping randomly.

The explicit digital mechanism wins when updates are sparse and urgent: if only three nodes out of a million changed, an event-driven design - a dirty list, a priority queue, a scheduler that touches only what changed - beats any sweep that dutifully visits a million clean nodes.

The recommendation for konnectome is therefore a hybrid with a clear division of labour: an event-driven scheduler for sparse urgent updates, and a slow wave-like background sweep for the maintenance work - decay, consistency checking, refresh - whose cost must be smooth, bounded and starvation-free, which is precisely the work the cortex appears to assign to its waves.

CHAPTER 64. THE STATE-REGISTER MECHANISM IN DEPTH (COMPUTATION)

Every earlier Part of this book kept meeting the same fact from a different side: the brain's rhythms shift wholesale when the brain changes state, from the slow rolling waves of deep sleep to the fast desynchronised chatter of alert waking.

This chapter turns that fact around and asks whether the global oscillatory pattern is itself the brain's state register - the one place where the current operating mode is written down for every component to read.

In a computer, a status register is a small set of bits that any part of the machine can consult to learn the current mode - user or supervisor, interrupts on or off - and the analogy will be earned, not assumed, over the next three sections.
