The COMPUTATION tag here rests on the sturdiest causal evidence in the whole book outside the sharp-wave ripple: experimenters can flip the brain's global rhythm with neuromodulatory drugs and stimulation, and the functional consequences - what the animal can perceive, learn and remember - flip with it.

64.1 The global oscillatory state as a broadcast mode

This section establishes that the global rhythm really does behave like a readable, mode-setting broadcast rather than a mere symptom.

Mircea Steriade, David McCormick and Terrence Sejnowski laid the foundation in Science in 1993: the thalamus and cortex - the brain's central relay station and its outer sheet - form a looped circuit whose intrinsic properties support two grossly different regimes, a slow synchronised oscillatory regime in sleep and anaesthesia and a fast desynchronised regime in waking.

The regimes are not decorative; they set what the circuit can do.

In the synchronised regime, thalamic relay cells fire in bursts locked to the slow rhythm, sensory transmission to the cortex is degraded, and the cortex cycles between silent down-states and active up-states, a pattern hospitable to memory replay and consolidation but hostile to fine perception.

In the desynchronised regime, relay cells fire in a faithful tonic mode, transmission is high-fidelity, and cortical neurons decorrelate, which Kenneth Harris and Alexander Thiele's 2011 review in Nature Reviews Neuroscience showed is the regime that attention locally recreates: attending to a stimulus desynchronises exactly the patch of cortex that represents it.

The broadcast character comes from the physics established in Part Three: a low-frequency, high-amplitude oscillation is visible in the field potential across wide territories, so any neuron with ordinary synaptic and membrane machinery can read the current mode simply by feeling the rhythm around it - no dedicated wiring needed.

The honest qualification is the engine-or-exhaust question of Part Twelve.

The mode itself - the constellation of membrane potentials, firing regimes and neuromodulator levels - is unambiguously causal for function; what remains debatable is whether the measured oscillation is the register or merely the register's glow, and the 2024 statement on decoupling measurement from process, cited in the chapter notes, keeps that distinction sharp.

For the translation it hardly matters: either way, the system maintains one globally readable state variable, and that is the design fact konnectome needs.

64.2 The neuromodulatory setting of the mode

If the global rhythm is the register, something must write to it, and this section names the writers.

A neuromodulator is a chemical broadcast signal: rather than carrying a specific message from one neuron to the next, it is released diffusely by small clusters of deep-brain neurons and changes how billions of other cells respond to their inputs, the way stage lighting changes how a whole scene reads without changing the script.

The main writers are acetylcholine from the basal forebrain and brainstem, norepinephrine from the locus coeruleus - a tiny blue-tinged nucleus in the brainstem - serotonin from the raphe nuclei, histamine from the hypothalamus, and orexin from the lateral hypothalamus, and Clifford Saper and colleagues showed in Neuron in 2010 that these systems are wired into a flip-flop switch whose mutual inhibition makes brain states discrete and transitions fast, like a light switch that resists resting halfway.

The causal evidence that the writers set the register is old, deep and consistent.

Cholinergic stimulation abolishes cortical slow oscillations and shifts thalamic cells from burst mode to tonic mode; blocking noradrenergic signalling in an awake animal lets slow synchronised waves intrude; and bathing a slice of isolated cortex in acetylcholine and norepinephrine converts its spontaneous slow oscillation into an awake-like desynchronised pattern, showing the modulators are sufficient to rewrite the mode in miniature.

Harris and Thiele's synthesis added the crucial refinement that the register has spatial granularity: neuromodulation plus focused excitatory drive can desynchronise one cortical territory while its neighbours stay synchronised, so the brain runs different patches in different modes at once, as local sleep studies and the drowsy driver's lapsing cortex both attest.

Note the loop structure, because the translation will need it: the modulatory nuclei read the brain's needs - circadian time, sleep pressure, threat, novelty, reward - through their inputs, write the mode through their broadcasts, and the mode in turn gates what the brain notices, which updates the nuclei again.

The register is not set by a master programmer; it is set by a small committee of feedback controllers, each nudging the mode along its own axis, arousal by norepinephrine, attentional precision by acetylcholine, and the committee's compromise is the state you are in right now while reading this sentence.

64.3 The mode-register analogue for konnectome

The translation begins, as always, with the plain-words definitions.

A register, again, is a small piece of fast storage every component can read; clock gating is the hardware trick of switching off the clock signal to idle circuit blocks so they burn no switching power; and a scheduler is the operating-system component that decides which task runs when.

The brain's design, assembled from the two sections above, is this: one globally readable mode variable, written by a handful of slow feedback controllers, expressed as a change in the global timing regime, with per-region granularity, discrete states, fast transitions and hysteresis - meaning the switch resists flickering back and forth.

The konnectome analogue is a mode register with exactly those properties: a small enumerated state - for instance ingest, consolidate, serve, idle - published where every subsystem can read it cheaply, written only by a few supervisory controllers that integrate slow signals such as load, error rate, data staleness and time of day, with per-partition granularity so one shard can consolidate while another serves, and with deliberate hysteresis so the system does not thrash between modes.

The biological lesson konnectome should copy most faithfully is that mode is not a message but an ambient condition: components do not receive a command to behave differently, they consult the register and gate themselves, which is cheap, scalable and robust to any single component missing a memo.

Now the design test in both directions.

The rhythm-like broadcast wins where the brain's constraints hold: enormous numbers of components, no reliable point-to-point signalling to all of them, and modes that change slowly relative to the work - conditions under which an ambient readable state beats delivering millions of individual notifications.

The explicit digital mechanism wins on transitions and on proof: a software system can do what biology cannot, namely quiesce cleanly - finish in-flight work, write a checkpoint, flip the register atomically, and log the exact timestamped moment of the change - and where audit or consistency matters, that explicit transaction beats any gradual ambient drift between states.

So konnectome should build the register as an explicit, atomically updated, timestamped variable - the digital win - while copying biology's read side, in which every component consults the ambient mode and gates its own behaviour, including switching off its own idle machinery in the spirit of clock gating, rather than waiting to be told.

CHAPTER 65. THE DESIGN-TEST CASE STUDIES (COMPUTATION)

Every translation chapter in Parts Thirteen and Fourteen ended with the same discipline, the design test: for each wave-like mechanism, ask where it genuinely pays for itself and where an explicit digital mechanism - a timestamp, a scheduler, a pointer - simply wins.

This chapter promotes the design test from a closing paragraph to the whole show, by working two concrete konnectome design problems end to end, one falling each way, and then generalising the decision procedure.

The point of working them honestly is that this book's credibility rests on being willing to conclude, in public, that the brain's solution is sometimes the wrong solution for a machine.

Nothing in this chapter is hypothetical about the neuroscience; every borrowed mechanism cites the biology established in the earlier Parts.

65.1 A worked case where a rhythm-like mechanism pays for itself

The problem: konnectome must run continuous background maintenance over a very large knowledge graph - refreshing staleness scores, decaying unused activation, verifying local consistency between neighbouring nodes - across, say, one hundred million nodes, on hardware shared with live query traffic, without ever visibly degrading that traffic.

The explicit-digital candidate is the obvious one: keep a dirty set, timestamp every node's last update, and let a scheduler visit nodes in priority order whenever load permits.

Work it through and three costs surface.

First, bookkeeping: a priority queue over one hundred million timestamped entries is itself a large mutable structure that must be updated on every touch, contended by every writer, and its random-access visit order fights the memory hierarchy, which rewards sequential scans by an order of magnitude.

Second, burstiness: priority-driven maintenance arrives in unpredictable clumps, exactly what a latency-sensitive service cannot absorb, so the scheduler must be wrapped in throttles and budgets that are themselves tuning-sensitive machinery.

Third, starvation: any priority scheme leaves the lowest-priority nodes perpetually unvisited unless anti-starvation logic is bolted on, and that logic is, in disguise, a periodic sweep.

The rhythm-like candidate is the wavefront pass of Chapter 63 combined with the slow-oscillation lesson of the sleep Parts: a fixed-cadence frontier that walks the graph in storage order, touching every node exactly once per period, doing all three maintenance jobs as it passes, with the period - the analogue of the rhythm's frequency - as the single tuning knob trading freshness against overhead.

Its costs are perfectly predictable and smooth: no per-node bookkeeping, no queue contention, sequential access that the hardware loves, a hard guarantee that no node waits longer than one period, and a trivially stated performance contract - the sweep consumes a fixed few percent of machine capacity, always, like a heartbeat.

This is also, not coincidentally, the shape of biology's solution to the same problem: the sleeping brain's slow oscillation and its traveling waves visit the whole cortical sheet on a fixed cadence to do maintenance - consolidation, renormalisation, cleanup - that would be ruinous to attempt in the middle of waking traffic, as Diekelmann and Born's 2010 synthesis and the Part Six chapters detailed.

Verdict: for dense, cheap, uniform, latency-tolerant maintenance over a huge structure, the rhythm-like mechanism pays for itself, and konnectome should build the sweep and skip the queue.

65.2 A worked case where an explicit timestamp wins

The problem: konnectome ingests assertions about the world from many concurrent sources, and must answer, for any two conflicting assertions, which one is newer, so that the newer one wins - the ordering problem at the heart of any distributed knowledge store.

The rhythm-like candidate borrows the brain's most impressive ordering trick, phase coding: as Chapters 40 and 62 established, the hippocampus encodes the order of events as firing phase within a theta cycle, and Kayser and colleagues showed in Neuron in 2009 that phase-of-firing carries stimulus information cortex-wide, so one could imagine konnectome nodes sharing a common oscillation and encoding recency as phase.

Work it through and the scheme collapses under exactly the objections this book has rehearsed since Part Five.

First, phase is cyclic: a phase value orders events within one cycle but says nothing across cycles, so two assertions a minute apart in a 100-millisecond cycle are indistinguishable without an additional cycle counter - and a cycle counter plus a phase is just a timestamp wearing a costume.

Second, phase alignment across machines is the conduction-delay objection reborn: network latency between nodes is variable and often exceeds a plausible cycle period, so the shared oscillation the scheme presupposes cannot be maintained precisely enough, which is the same reason the binding-by-synchrony literature struggled to align phase across distant brain regions.

Third, the brain itself does not use phase for durable order: phase precession orders events within a theta cycle for the milliseconds-scale needs of synaptic plasticity, but durable before-and-after knowledge is stored structurally, in strengthened synapses and remodelled maps, not as a preserved phase.

The explicit-digital candidate is sixty years old and settled: give every assertion a timestamp - and where clocks cannot be trusted, use Leslie Lamport's 1978 logical clocks, in which every node keeps a counter, stamps each event, attaches the stamp to each message, and sets its counter to the maximum of its own and any received stamp plus one, guaranteeing that causally related events order correctly with no synchronised clocks at all.

The comparison is not close: the timestamp is durable, transitive, comparable across arbitrary gaps, cheap to store, and proof-friendly, and modern systems refine it further with vector clocks and hybrid logical clocks that add physical time back in.

Verdict: for durable ordering across an unreliable network, the explicit timestamp wins outright, and konnectome should not simulate an oscillation to do what an integer does better - the brain uses phase here only because it has no integers, and honesty about that constraint is the whole point of the design test.

65.3 The decision procedure generalised

Two worked cases are anecdotes; this section extracts the rule.

Lay the cases side by side and the discriminating variables come into focus.

The sweep won where the work was dense - nearly every element needs touching - uniform, cheap per element, tolerant of bounded delay, and endless; the timestamp won where the property needed was durable, exact, comparable across arbitrary spans of time and space, and provable.

Generalise, and the decision procedure for any proposed rhythm-like mechanism in konnectome reads as follows.

• Ask what the oscillation is for in the biology: if it exists to overcome a resource the brain lacks - no random-access memory, no integers, no global clock, no addressed messaging - and konnectome has that resource, use the resource, not the imitation.

• Ask whether the workload is dense or sparse: rhythmic mechanisms amortise beautifully over dense uniform work and waste ruinously over sparse work, where event-driven schedulers exist precisely to touch only what changed.

• Ask whether the guarantee needed is statistical or exact: rhythms deliver bounded-staleness, fairness and graceful degradation; timestamps, pointers and transactions deliver exactness, durability and auditability - match the mechanism to the guarantee the requirement actually states.

• Ask whether the coordination span exceeds the coherence span: a shared rhythm only coordinates components whose communication delay is small next to the cycle period, so within a machine rhythmic tricks may hold, across a network they rarely do - this is the conduction-delay objection turned into an engineering rule.

• Ask what the single tuning knob costs: a rhythm's one frequency is its elegance and its rigidity, so if different parts of the workload need different cadences, either run nested rhythms as the brain does - the theta-gamma lesson - or admit you need a scheduler.

The compressed form of the whole procedure fits in one line: copy the brain's scheduling wisdom, never its arithmetic poverty.

And the two cases show the line has teeth in both directions, which is what separates a design test from a metaphor.

CHAPTER 66. THE CLOSING SYNTHESIS (CONTEXT)

Every book about the brain's rhythms ends with a choice between two temptations: to crown the rhythms as the secret of thought, or to dismiss them as the hum of the machinery.

This book was built to refuse both, by grading every rhythm's claim to computation on causal evidence, teaching every objection by name, and forcing every surviving function through an engineering design test.

This closing chapter gathers the verdicts, states what konnectome should do about them, and ends with the one sentence the entire document was written to earn.

It carries the tag CONTEXT because its job is not new evidence but honest assembly.

66.1 The rhythm-by-rhythm verdict in one table

The outline promised the verdict in one table, and in a plain-text book the table is a verdict list, one rhythm per entry, each carrying the grade its chapters earned.

• The hippocampal sharp-wave ripple - the brief 110 to 200 cycle per second burst of the resting and sleeping hippocampus - grades COMPUTATION, the cleanest verdict in the book: Girardeau and colleagues silenced ripples in 2009 and impaired memory, later work prolonged ripples and improved it, and the ripple stands as the field's proof that a rhythm can be a mechanism.

• Parvalbumin-driven gamma - the fast local rhythm generated by the parvalbumin class of interneurons, the inhibitory cells that pace their neighbours - grades COMPUTATION at the circuit level: Cardin and Sohal's 2009 optogenetic experiments generated the rhythm and changed information flow, though what gamma computes at the cognitive level remains contested.

• Hippocampal theta and its phase code grade COMPUTATION with a stated boundary: Boyce and colleagues' 2016 silencing showed rapid-eye-movement theta is needed for consolidation, and phase precession is real, but the grand theta-gamma capacity arithmetic of Chapter 62 remains elegant hypothesis, not settled fact.

• The sleep architecture of slow oscillations, spindles and their nesting grades COMPUTATION for consolidation: closed-loop stimulation locked to the slow oscillation's rhythm strengthens memory in humans, the rare case where a rhythm-timed intervention works in our own species - while the synaptic homeostasis account of why, and the glymphatic clearance story, stay honestly CONTESTED.

• Alpha as gating inhibition, beta as status-quo maintenance and motor hold, and the attentional theta of rhythmic sampling all grade a serious CONTESTED: each has strong correlational structure, respected theory - Jensen and Mazaheri 2010, Engel and Fries 2010, Fiebelkorn and Kastner 2019 - and no decisive perturbation.

• Binding-by-synchrony, the book's cautionary tale, grades CONTESTED trending toward historical: Shadlen and Movshon's 1999 critique, Ray and Maunsell's frequency-drift objection of 2010, and Singer's own 2023 turn toward assemblies without oscillation mark the retreat of the strongest claim ever made for a rhythm.

• The clinical biomarkers divide sharply: basal-ganglia beta in Parkinson's disease grades COMPUTATION-adjacent and already drives adaptive stimulation, while the theta-beta ratio for attention deficit hyperactivity disorder and frontal alpha asymmetry for depression grade failed or unreliable, and the 40-hertz Alzheimer's stimulation grades CONTESTED on mixed human replications.

• Traveling waves and the global state register, this Part's own subjects, grade COMPUTATION-leaning: the state-setting evidence is causally deep, the wave-perception link of Davis 2020 is striking, and the perturbation keystone is still missing.

• And the broad remainder - the countless correlations of band power with every task ever run - grades SUBSTRATE until shown otherwise: real signal, engine noise, honest measurement of a machine that computes by other means.

66.2 What konnectome should build, what it should simulate, and what it should ignore

The verdict list converts into three bins of engineering instruction, and this section states them plainly.

Build - adopt as native architecture, because the design test of Chapter 65 showed the mechanism wins on digital hardware too:

• the two-stage memory system with an event-driven fast store and a rhythmic batch write-back consolidation pass, the sharp-wave ripple's lesson;

• the fixed-cadence maintenance sweep over the whole graph, the slow-wave and traveling-wave lesson;

• the ambient mode register with few writers, universal cheap readers, per-partition granularity and hysteresis, the neuromodulatory state lesson;

• the small fixed-slot rotating schedule wherever many items contend for one scarce resource, the theta-gamma scheduling lesson;

• and everywhere, explicit Lamport-style logical clocks for ordering, because the design test's sharpest finding is that the brain's phase tricks are workarounds for missing integers.

Simulate - model offline to learn from, but do not put in the serving path, because the evidence or the engineering case is not yet strong enough:

• oscillatory binding and phase-coded routing, including the Kuramoto-style artificial neurons of Miyato, Lowe and Locatello's 2025 line of work, which konnectome should watch as research, not adopt as infrastructure;

• wave-based context propagation as an alternative to attention mechanisms, an open research conversation;

• and any rhythm-timed intervention on its own knowledge dynamics, tested first in replayed traffic the way closed-loop stimulation was tested in sleep labs.

Ignore - spend no engineering effort, because the book's grading found no mechanism there to copy:

• frequency-band numerology, the habit of treating band boundaries as natural kinds when Chapter 12 showed they are conventions;

• biomarkers that failed their trials, the theta-beta ratio foremost;

• and literal oscillation of the substrate for its own sake - making units wave because neurons wave - which confuses the costume for the actor.

The three bins are the book's practical payload, and each entry traces back through a graded chapter to named experiments, which is the only justification an engineering recommendation drawn from biology can honestly claim.

66.3 The single sentence the whole document exists to earn

A document of this length owes its reader a destination that fits in one breath, and the sixty-five chapters behind this page were assembled so that the following sentence could be written with every word load-bearing and none of it faith.

Brain waves are neither the music of thought nor the hum of the machine, but the timetable of a computer built without clocks, integers or addresses - a timetable that computes in the few proven places where timing itself is the resource being allocated, and so konnectome should copy the brain's schedules and refuse its scarcities.

Read it back against the book and every clause cashes out.

Neither music nor hum is Parts One through Twelve, the refusal of both temptations, earned by grading instead of believing.

The timetable of a computer built without clocks, integers or addresses is the biophysics and the mechanisms, Parts Three and Four, which showed rhythms arising wherever excitation and inhibition chase each other, and the reckoning of Part Twelve, which showed the brain using that timing structure because it has nothing better.

Computes in the few proven places is the ripple, the driven gamma, the silenced theta, the closed-loop slow oscillation - the short honest list of Section 66.1.

Timing itself is the resource being allocated is the deepest pattern the book found: every graded computation - the ripple's offline replay window, the theta cycle's ordered slots, the state register's regime switching, the wave's moving window of readiness - is, in the end, a scheduling mechanism.

And copy the schedules, refuse the scarcities is Chapter 65's decision procedure compressed to five words, the design test that let this book take the brain seriously without taking it literally.

That is the sentence, and the book rests here.

The reader who began at Chapter 1 knowing only that the brain hums can now say what the hum is made of, which parts of it think, how we know, who disputes it and why - and the builder who came for konnectome leaves with a timetable worth stealing.

END OF CHAPTERS 62 TO 66.
